D.4 Additional results License: arXiv.org perpetual non-exclusive license arXiv:2611.00448v1 [cs.LG] 30 Oct 2026 Learning to evolve by matching velocities Nicola Farenga † † thanks: MOX, Department of Mathematics, Politecnico di Milano, Milan, Italy. Andrea Manzoni 1 1 footnotemark: 1 Abstract Learning the continuous-time evolution of nonlinear dynamical systems commonly relies on trajectory-based objectives, which require simulating the learned dynamics during training and backpropagating through the numerical scheme, at a cost that grows with the rollout horizon.
We show that, beyond this cost, trajectory-based objectives degenerate as the rollout horizon increases, hindering gradient-based optimization, and that the numerical scheme biases the learned dynamics by constraining the spectrum of the learned Jacobian to its absolute stability region.
To overcome these limitations, we introduce Velocity Matching (VM), a simulation-free objective that relies on piecewise-linear interpolation and stochastic collocation, and approximates the velocity population risk to second order in the sampling step.
We develop a theoretical framework for velocity-based learning, introducing stability estimates that control trajectory errors in terms of velocity errors, thereby justifying simulation-free training via velocity supervision. These estimates are used to establish universal and continuous-time approximation results when the learned velocity field is parameterized by deep neural networks.
Building on a bias-variance decomposition of the VM objective, we show that its higher-order approximation of the velocity population risk yields a consistent minimizer; that its bias term relates to the residual of an A-stable continuous-stage Runge–Kutta scheme, thus connecting VM to implicit integration without requiring any implicit solve; and that it implicitly matches the action of the learned Jacobian to that of the true one along the observed trajectories, without requiring access to the underlying velocity field or its Jacobian.
Numerical experiments on nonlinear ordinary differential equations and semi-discretized time-dependent partial differential equations show that VM improves accuracy over trajectory-based objectives, while avoiding both the cost of simulation and the instabilities of long rollouts.
Empirical assessments of the continuous-time approximation and implicit Jacobian matching properties show that the models learned with VM can be simulated with schemes and step sizes different from those used during training without loss of accuracy, and that they preserve invariants and reproduce chaotic attractors that trajectory-based strategies fail to capture. normalization D.3 Training and evaluation D.4 Additional results 1 Introduction 1.1 Background and motivation Recent advancements in the field of deep learning have enabled to address the fundamental problem of modeling the evolution of complex physical processes governed by nonlinear ordinary differential equations (ODEs) and time-dependent partial differential equations (PDEs), by building efficient surrogates that drastically reduce the computational costs required for high-fidelity simulations.
Although significant progress has been made in terms of architectures, ranging from graph-based models [ Sanchez-Gonzalez et al., 2018 , Pfaff et al., 2021 , Lam et al., 2023 ] to operator learning frameworks [ Li et al., 2021 , Kovachki et al., 2023 ] , transformer-based architectures [ Geneva and Zabaras, 2022 , Li et al., 2023b , Herde et al., 2024 , Bodnar et al., 2025 , Holzschuh et al., 2025 ] , and generative approaches [ Price et al., 2025 , Kohl et al., 2026 , Kossaifi et al., 2026 ] , autoregression remains the core modeling paradigm for handling temporal dependencies. Consequently, training objectives are typically based on trajectory supervision, thus requiring simulation of the learned dynamics during training. Simulation-based learning requires unrolling the learned dynamics over multiple time steps, which, from the neural ODEs perspective [ Chen et al., 2018 , Kidger, 2022 ] , translates into the need to numerically integrate the learned dynamics over the rollout horizon, and relying on backpropagation through time (BPTT) or adjoint-based methods to compute gradients, thus introducing substantial computational overhead, often prohibitive for fine spatial and temporal discretizations, long time horizons, and large-scale models.
While rollout objectives are often employed to improve long-term predictive stability [ Uy et al., 2023 ] , undesired coupling effects between the learned dynamics and the integration scheme used during training may arise [ Lu and Tsai, 2026 ] , shaping the loss landscape and inducing overfitting to the numerical method and the temporal discretization, thus undermining the continuous-time approximation capabilities of the learned model [ Krishnapriyan et al., 2023 ] . Simulation-free alternatives commonly rely on teacher forcing (TF) schemes, in which autoregression is avoided during training by supervising the learned dynamics to predict the next state from the current ground truth, rather than regressing over multi-step rollouts. Such an approach, however, does not account for the distributional shift induced by error accumulation along inference-time rollouts, exposing a gap between one-step and multi-step objectives [ McCabe et al., 2023 , Koehler et al., 2024 ] , that has motivated the adoption of adversarial training strategies [ Stachenfeld et al., 2022 , Brandstetter et al., 2022 ] and stabilization techniques for error correction [ Pedersen et al., 2025 ] .
Given the residual nature of autoregressive updates, commonly reading as explicit Euler updates, velocity supervision represents a natural alternative to trajectory-based objectives [ Zhou and Barati Farimani, 2025 ] , reframing the learning problem as a regression over velocity fields. However, velocity-based learning is hindered by the inaccessibility of the true velocities, typically estimated from discrete observations via finite differences or interpolation techniques. In this context, Hou et al. [2026] extend flow matching [ Lipman et al., 2023 , Tong et al., 2024 ] to the operator learning setting, regressing the neural approximation of the right-hand side of a semi-discretized PDE onto the estimated velocities obtained by differentiating higher-order spline interpolants fitted to the observed trajectories. This construction mirrors the simulation-based and simulation-free duality in modern dynamical approaches to generative modeling [ Albergo et al., 2025 ] , and empirically demonstrates improved long-term stability and time-resolution invariance capabilities. Yet the relationship between trajectory-based and velocity-based learning objectives still lacks a comprehensive characterization, leaving the theoretical justification for the effectiveness of velocity-based approaches an open question. Whether simulation can be avoided during training by regressing directly onto velocity fields, while controlling the trajectory error and preserving continuous-time approximation properties, is a question that requires us to (i) identify what separates trajectory-based from velocity-based learning, that is, how the two objectives relate; and (ii) devise a suitable empirical objective that enables estimation of the velocity field while meeting these desiderata.
To this end we develop a theoretical framework for velocity-based learning, characterizing the velocity field of the underlying ODE as the minimizer of a quadratic functional over the space of admissible vector fields. We relate this objective to its trajectory-based counterpart by introducing suitable stability estimates mirroring the Lax equivalence theorem [ Lax and Richtmyer, 1956 ] ; these estimates justify simulation-free training based on velocity supervision, and transfer approximation guarantees on the vector field to the flow it generates.
Building on this analysis, we introduce the Velocity Matching (VM) objective, which combines stochastic collocation in time with piecewise-linear interpolation of the observed states to supervise the learned velocity field over the continuum of the time interval. By contrast, TF supervises the learned field at the observed states only, as illustrated in Figure 1 . A further theoretical analysis identifies stochastic collocation as the mechanism behind three properties of VM: a higher-order approximation of the velocity population risk, an implicit regularization of the Jacobian along observed trajectories, and a correspondence with implicit integration schemes. The main contributions of this work and the organization of the paper are summarized next. Figure 1: Learning paradigms for dynamical systems. Rows separate trajectory-based (top) from velocity-based objectives (bottom).
(a) Rollout training simulates the learned dynamics via numerical integration, and minimizes the discrepancy between the predicted x ^ k \hat{x}_{k} and ground truth x ⁡ ( t k ) x(t_{k}) states, with gradients backpropagated through the entire unrolled trajectory.
(b) Teacher forcing spans both rows, as it admits either reading: it replaces multi-step rollouts with one-step predictions, avoiding backpropagation through time.
As discussed in Section 2 , its loss can equivalently be read as a regression of the learned increment onto finite difference estimates v k ≈ ∂ t x ⁡ ( t k ) v_{k}\approx\partial_{t}x(t_{k}) , at the observed states; the bottom panel shows the explicit Euler case, where the increment is f ^ \hat{f} itself.
(c) Velocity Matching instead evaluates the learned velocity field f ^ \hat{f} along the piecewise-linear interpolant x ~ ​ ( t ) \tilde{x}(t) of the observations, at times t t sampled uniformly over the observation window, and regresses it onto the derivative ∂ t x ~ ​ ( t ) \partial_{t}\tilde{x}(t) of the interpolant. 1.2 Main contributions and organization This work develops a theoretical framework for velocity-based learning of dynamical systems, which characterizes the gap between trajectory-based and velocity-based learning, and serves as the foundation of a simulation-free empirical objective that we introduce, named Velocity Matching . First, Section 2 introduces the problem of learning dynamical systems in the form of ODEs, together with trajectory-based training objectives, ranging from multi-step rollouts to teacher forcing (TF), and discusses the challenges associated with simulation-based learning.
In particular, we study the objective defined over trajectories in a simplified continuous-time linear setting, where analytical expressions for the loss and its gradient can be derived. These expressions highlight the unsuitability of the objective for gradient-based optimization over long time horizons.
Starting from a similar setting and then extending to the more general nonlinear case, we discuss the role of the numerical scheme used to simulate the learned dynamics during training, showing how it shapes the resulting loss landscape and, consequently, constrains the spectrum of the Jacobian of the learned dynamics to the stability region of the scheme. In Section 3 , we develop the theoretical framework for velocity-based learning that underlies the simulation-free objective introduced next, whose main contributions are summarized as follows: • In Section 3.1 , we reframe the problem of learning dynamical systems in continuous-time as a regression over velocity fields, characterizing the true velocity field as the unique minimizer of a quadratic objective defined with respect to the occupation measure of the observed trajectories. • In Section 3.2 , we then show, by introducing a set of stability estimates,
that this theoretical velocity objective controls the one defined over trajectories, up to a stability constant depending on the Lipschitz constant of the learned field, thus providing the theoretical justification for simulation-free learning. These estimates extend to a discrete-time setting, where the step size and the order of the numerical method employed to simulate the learned dynamics are explicitly accounted for, highlighting the role of numerical consistency and stability in the estimation of the underlying dynamics. • Building on the stability estimates in continuous-time, in Section 3.3 we provide a universal approximation result for flow maps associated with the true dynamics, when the learned velocity field is parameterized by deep neural networks (NNs) with smooth activation functions, such as GELU.
Relying on this result and a further error decomposition, we then show that the learned dynamics satisfies a continuous-time approximation property, namely that it can be simulated, at inference, on arbitrarily fine temporal grids without loss of accuracy. Section 4 addresses empirical estimation, building on the developed theoretical framework, by introducing the Velocity Matching (VM) objective. VM is a simulation-free objective that extends naive velocity regression by evaluating the learned velocity field at points sampled uniformly along the piecewise-linear interpolant of the observed trajectories, and by supervising it with the velocities obtained by differentiating the interpolant. As a velocity-based objective, VM does not require numerical integration of the learned dynamics.
Section 4.1 introduces the objective and its algorithmic implementation. We then provide a theoretical analysis of its approximation properties, specifically: • In Sections 4.2 and 4.3 , we relate the VM loss to the residual of the average vector field method, an A-stable continuous-stage Runge–Kutta method, through a bias-variance decomposition of the VM objective with respect to the stochastic offset along the interpolant, so that VM inherits the properties of an implicit integration scheme without requiring any implicit solve. We then interpret the VM objective as a stochastic quadrature formula yielding an O ⁡ ( Δ ​ t 2 ) O({\Delta t}^{2}) approximation of the velocity population risk, whereas TF attains only an O ⁡ ( Δ ​ t ) O({\Delta t}) approximation. Combined with the stability estimates of Section 3 , this result yields a simulation-free bound on the trajectory loss, together with the consistency of the VM minimizer as the step size vanishes. • In Section 4.4 , we show that the VM objective admits an equivalent formulation as a regularized velocity regression problem, in which the bias term implicitly matches the action of the Jacobian of the learned velocity field to that of the true one along the flow, without requiring access to the latter, while the variance term penalizes the variation of the learned field along the flow. This characterization provides an interpretation of the improved long-term behavior of the learned dynamics, and connects VM to Jacobian matching approaches for chaotic systems. In Section 5 , we compare VM with TF and rollout-based strategies on nonlinear ODEs and semi-discretized time-dependent PDEs. Under a common iteration budget, VM attains errors lower than those of the baselines by more than an order of magnitude, at the training cost of TF, and trains stably where rollout objectives become unstable. A comparable reduction is observed for the Jacobian error, consistently with the implicit regularization result, although no Jacobian information is used during training. We further verify that the velocity fields learned by VM satisfy the continuous-time approximation property, and can thus be simulated with schemes and step sizes different from those used during training, whereas the models learned by trajectory-based strategies overfit both the scheme and the step size of the training discretization. On Hamiltonian and chaotic benchmarks, VM preserves the qualitative structure of the dynamics, namely the invariants and the chaotic attractors, which trajectory-based strategies fail to recover. Finally, we draw conclusions in Section 6 . 1.3 Related work We review prior work on autoregressive surrogates for dynamical systems, whose residual updates admit a continuous-time interpretation, thus connecting them to neural ODEs and simulation-free velocity supervision. Within the latter, we consider two-stage approaches, flow matching, and collocation methods. We conclude by reviewing universal approximation for neural ODEs, together with continuous-time approximation results and analyses of the numerical bias induced by simulation-based training. Autoregressive surrogates and velocity supervision. Autoregression is a fundamental paradigm for modeling temporal dependencies and underlies many recent deep learning approaches to dynamical systems and PDE surrogate modeling, regardless of the model architecture or specific application domain [ Koehler et al., 2024 , Herde et al., 2024 , Bodnar et al., 2025 , Holzschuh et al., 2025 , McCabe et al., 2024 ] .
While multi-step rollout objectives are employed to improve long-term predictive stability [ Pathak et al., 2022 , Lam et al., 2023 , Uy et al., 2023 ] , simulation-based training objectives might be computationally prohibitive to evaluate because of the high dimensionality of the state space, the long temporal horizons encountered in many applications, and the large parameter count of recent architectures. These factors motivate the introduction of simulation-free alternatives based on teacher forcing (TF).
Such autoregressive updates commonly adopt a residual formulation, so that the learned model is typically trained to predict the increment rather than the full state at the next time step, with the resulting update reading as an explicit Euler step [ McCabe et al., 2025 , Kossaifi et al., 2026 ] . Generalizing this residual update to higher-order Runge–Kutta (RK) schemes [ Sanchez-Gonzalez et al., 2019 ] naturally leads to a neural ODE interpretation [ Chen et al., 2018 , Rackauckas et al., 2021 , Kidger, 2022 ] , so that the learned model can be interpreted as the velocity field governing the evolution of the system. This continuous-time interpretation, together with the residual structure of autoregressive updates, motivates velocity supervision as an alternative to trajectory-based objectives. In this context, Zhou and Barati Farimani [2025] demonstrate the empirical effectiveness of velocity supervision, yielding a smoother optimization landscape and improved long-term stability. Velocity supervision, however, requires estimating the target velocities from discrete observations, leading to two-stage approaches that we discuss next. Two-stage approaches, gradient matching and flow matching. The reliance on prior velocity estimation from discrete state observations to supervise vector field learning, thus avoiding the simulation of the learned ODE, has its roots in the context of structural system identification [ Bellman and Roth, 1971 , Varah, 1982 , Brunton et al., 2016 ] . Two-stage approaches, also known as gradient matching methods [ Ellner et al., 2002 ] , first construct a sufficiently regular interpolant of the observed state trajectory from discrete observations and, in a second stage, regress the velocity field onto the velocities obtained by differentiating this interpolant.
For regression splines, the statistical properties of two-stage estimators, including consistency and convergence rates, have been established by Brunel [2008] .
The two-stage approach was extended to neural ODEs by Bonnaffé and Coulson [2023] for nonparametric population dynamics modeling, where the interpolant is itself parameterized by a neural network rather than a spline.
The reliance on a prior interpolation step to supervise the learning of the underlying velocity field shares strong similarities with flow matching approaches for simulation-free training of continuous normalizing flows [ Lipman et al., 2023 , Chen et al., 2018 ] , where the neural ODE is trained by regressing onto a prescribed velocity field generating the conditional probability path. A related perspective has recently been explored for autoregressive neural operators by Hou et al. [2026] , but differs from our approach in two key respects. First, it relies on quintic spline interpolation, whereas we show that piecewise-linear interpolation suffices to attain a higher-order approximation of the velocity population risk. Second, it adopts a stochastic interpolant between consecutive observations [ Albergo et al., 2025 ] , which perturbs the deterministic interpolant with isotropic noise according to a prescribed schedule. Notably, their own results indicate that isotropic noise injection is not essential in this setting, as the best performance is attained at low noise levels. This observation is consistent with our analysis, which identifies perturbations along the interpolant, rather than in isotropic directions, as the mechanism underlying the higher-order accuracy and the implicit regularization of the VM objective, established in Sections 4.3 and 4.4 , respectively. Collocation methods, Jacobian matching and chaotic dynamics. Collocation approaches for system identification and parameter estimation in ODEs share similarities with the outlined two-stage approach, but reframe the problem under a regularized regression perspective [ Ramsay et al., 2007 ] . Specifically, generalized smoothing techniques [ Ramsay and Hooker, 2017 ] consists in jointly optimizing with respect to the interpolant and the velocity field parameters, rather than adopting a separate two-stage approach. In particular, the solution is approximated by a basis function expansion, whose coefficients are considered to be functions of the velocity field parameters, while a regularization term in the form of ODE residual, enforces velocity consistency and acts as a smoothing. Variants of this technique have been explored in the context of neural ODEs [ Shapovalova and Tsay, 2025 ] , alongside the use of collocation-based training approaches [ Quaglino et al., 2020 , Roesch et al., 2021 , Norcliffe and Deisenroth, 2023 ] . Objectives and regularization terms that enforce consistency between the Jacobians of the learned and true velocity fields, termed Jacobian matching , have been shown to improve the performance of neural ODEs on chaotic dynamics [ Park et al., 2024 , Kang et al., 2026 ] , enabling the recovery of ergodic properties of the target system, such as statistical moments and Lyapunov exponents. These results are closely related to our finding, reported in Section 4.4 , that the VM objective, through its stochastic collocation mechanism, implicitly enforces a directional form of Jacobian matching along the observed trajectories, without requiring access to the true Jacobian. Universal approximation, continuous-time approximation and numerical bias. Approximation properties of neural ODEs have been extensively investigated [ Zhang et al., 2020 , Li et al., 2023a , Ruiz-Balet and Zuazua, 2023 , Celledoni et al., 2023 ] . Within the specific context of learning dynamical systems, Li et al. [2026] establish universal approximation properties for semi-autonomous neural ODEs, by leveraging universal approximation results for shallow NNs [ Pinkus, 1999 ] . Following a similar approximation framework, resting on the stability estimates we derive in Section 3.2 , we extend this result to velocity fields parameterized by deep NNs, by drawing on recent approximation results for deep NNs in Sobolev norms [ Gühring et al., 2020 , Gühring and Raslan, 2021 ] . Universal approximation property of the learned dynamics, is closely related to the notion of continuous-time approximation (CTA), which concerns the ability of simulating the learned dynamics at arbitrary temporal resolutions, without the need for retraining, hence overcoming the limitations of fixed time-step autoregressive models. A first investigation appeared in Krishnapriyan et al. [2023] , with empirical evidence suggesting that the choice of the numerical method used during training governs the emergence of this property, improving with the order of the employed RK scheme. A theoretical framing was provided by Farenga et al. [2025] , in the context of latent neural ODEs for model order reduction of parameterized time-dependent PDEs, where a universal approximation result has been established and related to CTA, highlighting the need for a sufficiently small time discretization step size to guarantee such a property.
The ability to recover the underlying dynamics, and hence to achieve CTA, is however hindered by the numerical bias introduced by the integration scheme used during training. In this context, Lu and Tsai [2026] provide an extensive analysis relating underdamping and overdamping effects in the learned dynamics to the choice of the numerical method, tracing such effects back to the stability region of the employed scheme. The use of linear multistep methods has been addressed in Keller and Du [2021] , where a characterization of the notions of consistency, stability and convergence in the context of dynamics discovery is provided. 1.4 Notation Throughout the paper, we denote state vectors by x ∈ ℝ n x\in\mathbb{R}^{n} , with n ∈ ℕ n\in\mathbb{N} being the dimension of the state space, and | ⋅ | |\cdot| the Euclidean norm.
Regarding function spaces, C k ​ ( Ω , ℝ n ) C^{k}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n}) denotes the space of k k -times continuously differentiable functions, from Ω ⊆ ℝ m \Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{m} with values in ℝ n \mathbb{R}^{n} . The space of p p -integrable functions from Ω \Omega to ℝ n \mathbb{R}^{n} is denoted by L p ​ ( Ω , ℝ n ) L^{p}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n}) , with ∥ ⋅ ∥ L p ​ ( Ω , ℝ n ) \|\cdot\|_{L^{p}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})} its norm and p ∈ [ 1 , ∞ ] p\in[1,\infty] . When a different measure μ \mu over Ω \Omega is considered, rather than the Lebesgue measure, it will be indicated as L p ​ ( μ , ℝ n ) L^{p}(\mu;\mathbb{R}^{n}) .
Analogously, W k , p ​ ( Ω , ℝ n ) W^{k,p}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n}) denotes the Sobolev space of functions with weak derivatives up to order k ∈ ℕ k\in\mathbb{N} in L p L^{p} , with ∥ ⋅ ∥ W k , p ​ ( Ω , ℝ n ) \|\cdot\|_{W^{k,p}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})} its norm. In particular, for W k , ∞ W^{k,\infty} -norms, the sum convention is adopted [ Evans, 2010 , Brezis, 2011 ] .
The Lipschitz constant of a function f : Ω → ℝ n f:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{n} is denoted by Lip ⁡ [ f ] \operatorname{Lip}[f] . In the case of additional time dependence f : [ 0 , T ] × Ω → ℝ n f:[0,T]\times\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{n} , the Lipschitz constant with respect to the spatial variable, uniformly in time, is considered as Lip ⁡ [ f ] = ess ​ sup t ∈ [ 0 , T ] ​ Lip ​ [ f ⁡ ( t , ⋅ ) ] \operatorname{Lip}[f]=\esssup_{t\in[0,T]}\operatorname{Lip}[f(t,\cdot)] . Finally, for a restriction of f f to a subset K ⊂ Ω K\subset\Omega , we denote Lip ⁡ [ f | K ] = ess ​ sup t ∈ [ 0 , T ] ​ Lip ​ [ f ⁡ ( t , ⋅ ) | K ] \operatorname{Lip}[f|_{K}]=\esssup_{t\in[0,T]}\operatorname{Lip}[f(t,\cdot)|_{K}] .
To indicate that a random variable Z Z is distributed according to a probability distribution ρ \rho , we write Z ∼ ρ Z\sim\rho , and we denote by 𝔼 Z ∼ ρ \mathbb{E}_{Z\sim\rho} the expectation with respect to the distribution ρ \rho . The pushforward of a measure μ \mu through a measurable map T T is denoted by T # ​ μ T_{\#}\mu . With a slight abuse of notation, the notion of measure and probability density are used interchangeably. Further notation, specifically related to the problem of learning dynamical systems, is introduced in the following sections as needed. 2 Trajectory-based learning This section formulates the problem of learning the dynamics in a continuous-time setting and introduces the trajectory-based learning objective in both theoretical and empirical settings. We then discuss the sources of approximation that arise in the latter, along with different learning strategies commonly adopted in the context of rollout-based training. Finally, the challenges of trajectory-based learning are addressed by studying the degeneracies of the trajectory objectives in both continuous-time and discrete-time, motivating a departure from this approach. 2.1 Theoretical objective and problem formulation We proceed to formulate the problem of learning the dynamics of a nonlinear system in a continuous-time setting. In particular, nonlinear dynamical systems are considered in the form of first-order ordinary differential equations (ODEs), reading as ∂ t x ⁡ ( t , x 0 ) = f ⁡ ( t , x ⁡ ( t , x 0 ) ) , x ⁡ ( 0 , x 0 ) = x 0 , \partial_{t}x(t,x_{0})=f(t,x(t,x_{0})),\quad x(0,x_{0})=x_{0}, (1) with x ⁡ ( t , x 0 ) ∈ ℝ n x(t,x_{0})\in\mathbb{R}^{n} being the solution at time t ∈ [ 0 , T ] t\in[0,T] , associated to the initial condition x 0 ∈ 𝒳 0 x_{0}\in\mathcal{X}_{0} , with 𝒳 0 ⊂ ℝ n \mathcal{X}_{0}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n} compact. Here, T > 0 T>0 is a finite time horizon within which the solution is well-defined and remains contained in a compact set of ℝ n \mathbb{R}^{n} , for any initial condition x 0 ∈ 𝒳 0 x_{0}\in\mathcal{X}_{0} . The velocity field f f governing the dynamics is generally assumed to be continuous in time and locally Lipschitz in space, namely f ∈ C 0 ​ ( [ 0 , T ] , W loc 1 , ∞ ​ ( ℝ n , ℝ n ) ) f\in C^{0}([0,T];W^{1,\infty}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})) , to comply with the standard assumptions of Cauchy–Lipschitz (Picard–Lindelöf) theory [ Teschl, 2012 , Brezis, 2011 ] . The resulting flow map x ⁡ ( t , ⋅ ) : 𝒳 0 → ℝ n x(t,\cdot):\mathcal{X}_{0}\to\mathbb{R}^{n} then depends continuously on the initial condition. When the dependence on the initial time needs to be made explicit, we denote the solution of ( 1 ) as x ⁡ ( t , t 0 , x 0 ) x(t,t_{0},x_{0}) , with t 0 ∈ [ 0 , T ] t_{0}\in[0,T] being the initial time. Having used a flow notation that makes the parameterization of the solution by the initial condition explicit, the time derivative of the solution is denoted by ∂ t x ⁡ ( t , x 0 ) \partial_{t}x(t,x_{0}) , although, when it is clear from the context, we may also use the notation x ˙ \dot{x} for the sake of brevity.
We denote by 𝒳 ⊂ ℝ n \mathcal{X}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n} the reachable set of states visited by the solution of ( 1 ) over the time interval [ 0 , T ] [0,T] , starting from admissible initial conditions in 𝒳 0 \mathcal{X}_{0} , defined as 𝒳 := { x ( t , x 0 ) : t ∈ [ 0 , T ] , x 0 ∈ 𝒳 0 } ⊂ ℝ n , \mathcal{X}:=\{x(t,x_{0}):t\in[0,T],\,x_{0}\in\mathcal{X}_{0}\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}, (2) compact by the above assumptions.
Hence, learning the dynamics of ( 1 ) in a continuous-time setting amounts to approximating the velocity field f f with f ^ \hat{f} , within a suitable class of admissible functions, solely relying on trajectory observations. The resulting learned flow x ^ ​ ( t , x 0 ) \hat{x}(t,x_{0}) is then obtained by integrating ∂ t x ^ ​ ( t , x 0 ) = f ^ ​ ( t , x ^ ​ ( t , x 0 ) ) , x ^ ​ ( 0 , x 0 ) = x 0 , \partial_{t}\hat{x}(t,x_{0})=\hat{f}(t,\hat{x}(t,x_{0})),\quad\hat{x}(0,x_{0})=x_{0}, (3) which we refer to as the learned ODE.
In particular, when f ^ \hat{f} is sought within the parametric class of neural networks, ( 3 ) is referred to as a neural ODE [ Chen et al., 2018 , Kidger, 2022 ] . Assuming access to the full trajectories x ⁡ ( t , x 0 ) x(t,x_{0}) , and denoting by ρ 0 \rho_{0} a probability distribution supported on 𝒳 0 \mathcal{X}_{0} , the learning problem can then be framed as a risk minimization problem over the space of admissible velocity fields f ^ \hat{f} , where the objective is the expected discrepancy between the true and learned trajectories, namely ℒ x ​ [ f ^ ] = 𝔼 x 0 ∼ ρ 0 ​ ℓ x ​ ( f ^ , x 0 ) , ℓ x ​ ( f ^ , x 0 ) = 1 T ​ ‖ x ⁡ ( ⋅ , x 0 ) − x ^ ​ ( ⋅ , x 0 ) ‖ L 2 ​ ( [ 0 , T ] , ℝ n ) 2 . \mathcal{L}_{x}[\hat{f}]=\mathbb{E}_{x_{0}\sim\rho_{0}}\ell_{x}(\hat{f};x_{0}),\quad\ell_{x}(\hat{f};x_{0})=\frac{1}{T}\|x(\cdot,x_{0})-\hat{x}(\cdot,x_{0})\|^{2}_{L^{2}([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{n})}. (4) which we refer to as the theoretical trajectory objective .
The expectation argument ℓ x ​ ( f ^ , x 0 ) \ell_{x}(\hat{f};x_{0}) denotes the per-trajectory loss, defined as a mean L 2 L^{2} error between the true x ⁡ ( ⋅ , x 0 ) x(\cdot,x_{0}) and learned x ^ ​ ( ⋅ , x 0 ) \hat{x}(\cdot,x_{0}) trajectories over [ 0 , T ] [0,T] . The dependence of the theoretical objective ℒ x ​ [ f ^ ] \mathcal{L}_{x}[\hat{f}] on the learned model f ^ \hat{f} is implicit through the learned solution x ^ \hat{x} , making the objective nonlinear in f ^ \hat{f} even in the case of a linear dynamics, as discussed in Section 2.3.1 . As a notational remark, we use the subscript x x in ℒ x \mathcal{L}_{x} to indicate that the objective is defined over trajectories, as opposed to velocity-based objectives, denoted by ℒ f \mathcal{L}_{f} and introduced in Section 4 , which directly supervise the learned velocity field f ^ \hat{f} rather than the resulting trajectories. 2.2 Empirical objective and learning strategies When addressing the learning problem in an empirical setting, the theoretical trajectory objective ℒ x ​ [ f ^ ] \mathcal{L}_{x}[\hat{f}] defined in ( 4 ) is commonly approximated by its empirical counterpart ℒ x , N , N t ​ [ f ^ ] \mathcal{L}_{x,N,{N_{t}}}[\hat{f}] , involving a three-fold approximation. First, the expected risk is approximated through a Monte Carlo estimate using a finite number N N of trajectories corresponding to independent realizations of the initial condition x 0 ( i ) ∼ ρ 0 x_{0}^{(i)}\sim\rho_{0} , for i = 1 , … , N i=1,\dots,N ; second, the discrete-time nature of the available observations at time instances { t k } k = 0 N t \{t_{k}\}_{k=0}^{{N_{t}}} induces an approximation of the L 2 L^{2} norm over the time interval [ 0 , T ] [0,T] in ℓ x \ell_{x} ; third, the need to compute the mismatch between the true and learned trajectories at the discrete time instances { t k } k = 0 N t \{t_{k}\}_{k=0}^{{N_{t}}} , coupled with the nonlinear nature of f ^ \hat{f} , requires to rely on numerical integration of ( 3 ) to simulate the learned dynamics and obtain x ^ k ​ ( x 0 ) ≈ x ^ ​ ( t k , x 0 ) \hat{x}_{k}(x_{0})\approx\hat{x}(t_{k},x_{0}) . Hence, the empirical counterpart of the theoretical trajectory objective reads as ℒ x , N , N t ​ [ f ^ ] = 1 N ​ ∑ i = 1 N ℓ x , N t ​ ( f ^ , x 0 ( i ) ) , ℓ x , N t ​ ( f ^ , x 0 ) = 1 N t ​ ∑ k = 1 N t | x ⁡ ( t k , x 0 ) − x ^ k ​ ( x 0 ) | 2 , \mathcal{L}_{x,N,{N_{t}}}[\hat{f}]=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\ell_{x,{N_{t}}}(\hat{f};x_{0}^{(i)}),\quad\ell_{x,{N_{t}}}(\hat{f};x_{0})=\frac{1}{{N_{t}}}\sum_{k=1}^{{N_{t}}}\left|x(t_{k},x_{0})-\hat{x}_{k}(x_{0})\right|^{2}, (5) where subscripts N N and N t {N_{t}} in ℒ x , N , N t \mathcal{L}_{x,N,{N_{t}}} and ℓ x , N t \ell_{x,{N_{t}}} are used to indicate the dependence of the empirical objective on the finite number of trajectories and time instances, respectively.
The need for simulating the learned dynamics, whether through a simple residual update or higher-order numerical schemes, leads to a rollout-based training strategy, where the trajectory predictions are obtained by rolling out the learned model over the time horizon of interest, and model weights are updated by backpropagating gradients through the autoregressive steps, commonly referred to as backpropagation through time (BPTT) [ Werbos, 1990 ] . While rollout-based training strategies have been widely adopted for learning autoregressive neural surrogates for dynamical systems, due to their ability to provide stable long-term behavior and mitigate error accumulation over time [ Uy et al., 2023 , Koehler et al., 2024 ] , they are commonly associated to significant computational and memory costs, which are further induced by larger model sizes, often prohibitive for recently proposed large-scale neural surrogates [ McCabe et al., 2025 ] .
The issues associated with BPTT, arising from long rollouts and gradient degradation, have been widely studied in the context of recurrent neural networks (RNNs) [ Bengio et al., 1994 , Pascanu et al., 2013 , Bengio et al., 2012 , Bengio et al., 2015 , Gruslys et al., 2016 ] , and naturally carry over to the continuous-time ODE setting, which we elaborate on next.
To mitigate such issues, mainly related to long rollout horizons, and to reduce memory and computational costs, several strategies have been proposed in the literature, which we briefly review in the following. In particular,
a multiple shooting formulation of the problem is commonly adopted, where the full trajectory is split into sub-trajectories of length L ≤ N t L\leq{N_{t}} , yielding a per-trajectory loss of the form ℓ x , N t , L ​ ( f ^ , x 0 ) = 1 N t ​ ∑ j = 0 N t L − 1 ∑ k = 1 L | x ⁡ ( t j ​ L + k , x 0 ) − x ^ j ​ L + k ​ ( x ⁡ ( t j ​ L , x 0 ) ) | 2 , \ell_{x,{N_{t}},L}(\hat{f};x_{0})=\frac{1}{{N_{t}}}\sum_{j=0}^{\frac{{N_{t}}}{L}-1}\sum_{k=1}^{L}\left|x(t_{jL+k},x_{0})-\hat{x}_{jL+k}(x(t_{jL},x_{0}))\right|^{2}, (6) with L L being the shooting length, which, for L = N t L={N_{t}} , reduces to the standard rollout loss over the full trajectory. The empirical risk in this setting is denoted by ℒ x , N , N t , L ​ [ f ^ ] \mathcal{L}_{x,N,{N_{t}},L}[\hat{f}] .
Depending on the adopted shooting length L L , different training strategies arise, which we briefly recall in the following. Multiple shooting ( L < N t L<{N_{t}} ). Originating in optimal control and system identification [ Bock, 1983 , Bock and Plitt, 1984 , Baake et al., 1992 ] , multiple shooting partitions the full trajectory into sub-trajectories of length L ≪ N t L\ll{N_{t}} , over which the loss is evaluated, possibly enforcing continuity constraints at the interval boundaries. A parallel development of multiple shooting has been proposed in the RNNs literature, under the name of truncated BPTT [ Werbos, 1990 , Jaeger, 2002 , Tallec and Ollivier, 2017 ] , with recent extensions to neural ODEs [ Turan and Jäschke, 2022 , Iakovlev et al., 2023 ] , including stochastic scheduling of the integration horizon [ Ghosh et al., 2020 ] . Common implementations of multiple shooting typically feature overlapping sub-trajectories, leading to a variance reduction effect [ Beintema et al., 2023 ] .
Beyond empirical considerations, Ribeiro et al. [2020] provide a theoretical justification for multiple shooting in discrete-time state-space models by analyzing the smoothness of the loss landscape as a function of the simulation horizon. In particular, they show that both the loss and its gradient admit Lipschitz constants that grow exponentially with the rollout length for non-contractive dynamics, thereby motivating the adoption of shorter horizons. In this view, in Section 2.3.1 we provide a complementary analysis of the loss landscape in a continuous-time linear setting, where explicit rates of vanishing and exploding gradients, as well as the sharpening of the loss minimum as a function of the rollout horizon, are derived. Teacher forcing ( L = 1 L=1 ). Setting L = 1 L=1 in ( 6 ) yields the limiting case of multiple shooting, where the loss is computed over one-step predictions, with the model being fed with ground-truth observations at each time step, rather than its own predictions. This one-step strategy, known as teacher forcing (TF) [ Williams and Zipser, 1989 ] , ditches autoregression during training, and hence the need for backpropagation through the integrator, avoiding BPTT. In particular, when the autoregressive update is formulated as an explicit Euler step – being the one commonly adopted in neural PDE surrogates – TF admits an equivalent reading as a velocity-based objective, with the per-trajectory loss that can be rewritten as ℓ x , N t , 1 ​ ( f ^ , x 0 ) = Δ ​ t 2 N t ​ ∑ k = 0 N t − 1 | x ⁡ ( t k + 1 , x 0 ) − x ⁡ ( t k , x 0 ) Δ ​ t − f ^ ​ ( t k , x ⁡ ( t k , x 0 ) ) | 2 . \ell_{x,{N_{t}},1}(\hat{f};x_{0})=\frac{{\Delta t}^{2}}{{N_{t}}}\sum_{k=0}^{{N_{t}}-1}\left|\frac{x(t_{k+1},x_{0})-x(t_{k},x_{0})}{{\Delta t}}-\hat{f}(t_{k},x(t_{k},x_{0}))\right|^{2}. (7) In this view, TF represents a velocity-based learning strategy, where the learned velocity field f ^ \hat{f} is supervised pointwise in time, with the target velocity obtained by means of finite differences approximation.
While the resulting per-step regression problem is well-conditioned, it introduces a discrepancy between training and inference regimes, known as exposure bias [ Ranzato et al., 2016 ] . Since the model is never exposed to its own predictions during training, it is vulnerable to distribution shifts induced by error accumulation associated to autoregression at inference time, potentially resulting in unstable long-term behavior. Narrowing the training-inference gap has become a central issue in sequence modeling, motivating approaches such as scheduled sampling [ Bengio et al., 2015 , Vlachas and Koumoutsakos, 2024 ] and professor forcing [ Goyal et al., 2016 ] .
In the specific context of dynamical systems and PDEs surrogate modeling, the exposure bias can be understood through the lens of zero-stability [ Quarteroni et al., 2007 , Hairer et al., 2008 ] , with distribution shifts causing the model to diverge from the solution manifold. Recent benchmarking studies [ Koehler et al., 2024 , Ohana et al., 2024 ] have shown that models trained with one-step objectives struggle to perform autoregressive rollouts across different architectures, suggesting that the observed limitations are inherent to the training strategy, rather than the model architecture.
This observation motivates adversarial training strategies that promote stability of the learned dynamics [ Sanchez-Gonzalez et al., 2018 , Sanchez-Gonzalez et al., 2020 , Pfaff et al., 2021 , Stachenfeld et al., 2022 ] , by evaluating the loss ( 7 ) at perturbed states, either isotropically sampled from a Gaussian 𝒩 ⁡ ( x ⁡ ( t k , x 0 ) , σ 2 ​ I ) \mathcal{N}(x(t_{k},x_{0}),\sigma^{2}I) , whose variance σ 2 \sigma^{2} is a hyperparameter requiring tuning, or from the pushforward of the distribution of ground-truth states through the learned dynamics [ Brandstetter et al., 2022 ] , namely x ^ ( t k , t k − 1 , ⋅ ) # ρ ( t k − 1 ) \hat{x}(t_{k},t_{k-1},\cdot)_{\#}\rho(t_{k-1}) , with ρ ⁡ ( t k − 1 ) \rho(t_{k-1}) being the distribution of ground-truth states at time t k − 1 t_{k-1} .
Short rollout horizons, up to the TF limiting case L = 1 L=1 , are also adopted for learning chaotic dynamics, where the Lyapunov time scale governs the length of the rollout horizon for which BPTT is feasible [ Mikhaeil et al., 2022 ] , motivating a sparsely forced BPTT strategy, where the model is exposed to ground-truth observations at sparse time instances matching the Lyapunov time scale. Similarly, generalized teacher forcing evaluates the model at convex combinations of observed and predicted states, with the mixing coefficient being scheduled during training [ Hess et al., 2023 ] . While different in their implementation, these schemes share a common reading as a TF strategy coupled with a state perturbation mechanism, which can be interpreted as a form of regularization, promoting stability of the learned dynamics. Such a perspective is shared by the Velocity Matching (VM) objective which we introduce in Section 4 . Curriculum learning ( L = 1 → N t L=1\to{N_{t}} ). A possible strategy, known as curriculum learning , avoids committing to a fixed shooting length and instead schedules L L to progressively increase during training [ Bengio et al., 2009 , Bengio et al., 2015 ] . Such schedules can be either deterministic or stochastic, and enable to interpolate between TF and single-shooting regimes, focusing on short-term predictions at the beginning of training, and gradually increasing the temporal horizon up to the full horizon N t {N_{t}} as training proceeds. The adoption of curriculum strategies has been often regarded as necessary to achieve stable long-term rollouts, showing improved performance in the context of autoregressive PDE surrogates [ Takamoto et al., 2023 , Koehler et al., 2024 ] . 2.3 Degeneracies of the trajectory objective Trajectory-based learning unrolls the learned dynamics to obtain model predictions over a time horizon T T and compares them with reference observations at discrete-time instances. The cost of long rollouts motivates the adoption of multiple shooting and teacher forcing strategies, which in turn give rise to the exposure bias issue, feeding the common belief that long rollouts are necessary to achieve stable long-term behavior. Empirical evidence, however, suggests a more nuanced picture: longer rollouts may induce training instabilities, leading to a less smooth loss landscape and higher-variance gradients [ Metz et al., 2022 ] , thus requiring the adoption of curriculum strategies to stabilize the learning process [ Koehler et al., 2024 , List et al., 2025 ] .
Here we build understanding on these issues from an optimization standpoint by analyzing the loss landscape of the trajectory objective both in continuous and discrete-time, highlighting its dependence on the target dynamics regime and temporal horizon T T , as well as the role of the numerical scheme adopted for simulating the learned dynamics.
The analysis is deliberately carried out in a simplified linear setting, as it allows to derive explicit results thanks to closed-form derivations, and to isolate the analysis of the loss landscape from the complexity of the approximating function class.
In particular, in a continuous-time setting, it is shown that the trajectory objective exhibits vanishing and exploding gradients together with a sharpening of its minimum as T → + ∞ T\to+\infty , with such degeneracies developing exponentially fast in T T . In discrete-time, the interaction between the rollout loss ( 5 ) and the numerical integrator causes the landscape to collapse, in the infinite-horizon limit, onto the absolute stability region of the chosen numerical scheme, admitting a continuum of global minima, thereby hindering proper identification of the underlying system. Such a degeneracy translates into a constraint on the Jacobian spectrum of the learned dynamics in a nonlinear setting. 2.3.1 Loss degeneracy in continuous-time Figure 2: Trajectory loss landscape and gradient behavior. ( Left ) The energy-normalized trajectory objective ℓ ¯ x ​ ( λ ^ ) \bar{\ell}_{x}(\hat{\lambda}) (top) and its gradient ∂ λ ^ ℓ ¯ x ​ ( λ ^ ) \partial_{\hat{\lambda}}\bar{\ell}_{x}(\hat{\lambda}) (bottom) for the scalar linear system x ˙ = λ ​ x \dot{x}=\lambda x , over learnable parameters λ ^ ∈ [ − 10 , 10 ] \hat{\lambda}\in[-10,10] and target parameters λ ∈ [ − 5 , 5 ] \lambda\in[-5,5] , at horizons T = 1 T=1 and T = 10 T=10 .
( Right ) Asymptotic behavior of the gradient ∂ λ ^ ℓ ¯ x ​ ( λ ^ ) \partial_{\hat{\lambda}}\bar{\ell}_{x}(\hat{\lambda}) in the infinite-horizon limit T → + ∞ T\to+\infty , over the ( λ ^ , λ ) (\hat{\lambda},\lambda) plane, with the leading order behavior reported in each region.
In particular, the hatched right region indicates exploding gradients, while the top white faded region indicates vanishing gradients, both developing exponentially fast in T T . In the remaining lower-left quadrant, the gradient converges to a T T -independent finite value, vanishing along the line λ ^ = λ \hat{\lambda}=\lambda . The theoretical trajectory objective ( 4 ), despite being a quadratic functional in the solution x ^ \hat{x} , is nonlinear in f ^ \hat{f} even in the case of linear dynamics, as the map f ^ ↦ x ^ \hat{f}\mapsto\hat{x} implicitly defined by ( 3 ) is nonlinear, thus yielding a non-convex loss landscape. Given the empirical evidence of training instabilities in the long rollouts regime, it is of interest to analyze how such a landscape depends on the regime of the target dynamics, as well as on the temporal horizon T T , and to what extent these dependencies may hurt gradient-based optimization.
To quantify these considerations, we analyze the loss landscape of the theoretical trajectory objective, normalized by its average energy, which we denote by ℓ ¯ x ​ ( λ ^ ) \bar{\ell}_{x}(\hat{\lambda}) . A simplified linear setting is adopted, considering a scalar target system x ˙ = λ ​ x \dot{x}=\lambda x , with λ ∈ ℝ \lambda\in\mathbb{R} , to be learned from a fixed initial condition x 0 x_{0} by the parametric family x ^ ˙ = λ ^ ​ x ^ \dot{\hat{x}}=\hat{\lambda}\hat{x} . While not representative of the complexity of the general nonlinear case, such a linear setting is enough to expose the ill-suitedness of the trajectory objective to gradient-based optimization, allowing to provide explicit rates at which vanishing and exploding gradients develop, as well as the sharpening of the loss minimum as T → + ∞ T\to+\infty .
We defer detailed results and derivations to Appendix A.1 , while here we summarize the main findings. Regime dependence. For every fixed T > 0 T>0 , the loss landscape ℓ ¯ x ​ ( λ ^ ) \bar{\ell}_{x}(\hat{\lambda}) is smooth and admits a unique global minimum at λ ^ = λ \hat{\lambda}=\lambda at which ℓ ¯ x ​ ( λ ) = 0 \bar{\ell}_{x}(\lambda)=0 ; it flattens for λ ^ → − ∞ \hat{\lambda}\to-\infty , while it grows exponentially for λ ^ → + ∞ \hat{\lambda}\to+\infty . The presence of a positive horizontal asymptote and of the vanishing minimum imply non-convexity. Our interest in long rollouts motivates the analysis of the infinite-horizon limit T → + ∞ T\to+\infty , for which the loss exhibits a regime-dependent, discontinuous behavior. In the stable regime λ < 0 \lambda<0 , the loss landscape converges, for λ ^ < 0 \hat{\lambda}<0 , to a smooth non-convex profile, which diverges for λ ^ → 0 − \hat{\lambda}\to 0^{-} , while the loss blows up for λ ^ ≥ 0 \hat{\lambda}\geq 0 . In the unstable regime λ > 0 \lambda>0 , the loss landscape converges to a discontinuous profile: constant for λ ^ < λ \hat{\lambda}<\lambda , vanishing at λ ^ = λ \hat{\lambda}=\lambda , and diverging for λ ^ > λ \hat{\lambda}>\lambda . Such a discontinuous profile for unstable dynamics, reported in Figure 2 for T = 10 T=10 , denotes how ill-conditioning may develop in the presence of non-contractive dynamics, and motivates the adoption of shorter rollout horizons, as also suggested in Ribeiro et al. [2020] . Hence, in view of gradient-based optimization, it is worth discussing at which rate the smoothness of the loss landscape deteriorates as T → + ∞ T\to+\infty . Gradient degeneracy and sharpness. As a consequence of previous considerations, the gradient ∂ λ ^ ℓ ¯ x ​ ( λ ^ ) \partial_{\hat{\lambda}}\bar{\ell}_{x}(\hat{\lambda}) inherits the regime-dependent behavior of the loss landscape, provided in Proposition A.1 .
Its asymptotic behavior as T → + ∞ T\to+\infty is summarized in Figure 2 , which highlights the presence of regions of vanishing and exploding gradients over the ( λ ^ , λ ) (\hat{\lambda},\lambda) plane. Besides the stable quadrant λ ^ , λ < 0 \hat{\lambda},\lambda<0 , where the gradient converges to a finite non-zero value, the degeneracies observed
over the remaining quadrants develop exponentially fast in T T . In particular, over the positive quadrant λ ^ , λ > 0 \hat{\lambda},\lambda>0 , the behavior of the gradient is discontinuous over the line λ ^ = λ \hat{\lambda}=\lambda , with vanishing gradients for λ ^ < λ \hat{\lambda}<\lambda and exploding gradients for λ ^ > λ \hat{\lambda}>\lambda .
Hence, away from the minimizer, gradients become exponentially uninformative as T → + ∞ T\to+\infty , pointing to possible convergence issues of gradient-based optimization. Furthermore, as provided in Proposition A.2 , the sharpness at the minimum diverges as 2 ​ T 2 2T^{2} in the unstable regime. The development of vanishing and exploding gradients at an exponential rate in T T is coherent with Mikhaeil et al. [2022, Theorems 1, 2] , who relate the gradient behavior to the maximal Lyapunov exponent of the learned recursive map in the RNN setting, reducing here to λ ^ \hat{\lambda} . The gradient degeneracy of the unstable regime in Proposition A.1 thus connects to the chaotic setting, with λ ^ \hat{\lambda} and λ \lambda reading as the Lyapunov exponents of the learned and target dynamics in such a prototypical linear setting [ Chicone, 2024 ] .
In view of the above considerations, enlarging the temporal horizon T T clearly hinders gradient-based optimization. In particular, for non-contractive dynamics, the sharpening of the minimum implies that, for gradient descent to converge locally, the learning rate must scale as T − 2 T^{-2} , while the radius of guaranteed local convergence shrinks as T − 1 T^{-1} . 2.3.2 Loss degeneracy in discrete-time Figure 3: Discrete-time trajectory loss degeneracy. Representation of the loss collapse phenomenon in the infinite-horizon limit in the case of explicit Euler integration with step size Δ ​ t = 0.2 {\Delta t}=0.2 , for T = 0.2 , 2 , 20 T=0.2,2,20 , resulting in N t = 1 , 10 , 100 {N_{t}}=1,10,100 time steps, respectively. The loss landscape is shown as a function of the real and imaginary parts of the learned parameter λ ^ \hat{\lambda} , with the true parameter being λ = − 20 + 2 ​ i \lambda=-20+2\mathrm{i} . The dashed region represents the stability region 𝒜 Δ ​ t \mathcal{A}_{\Delta t} of explicit Euler for the given step size, towards which the loss landscape collapses as T → + ∞ T\to+\infty . The challenges of trajectory-based learning are further compounded in the empirical setting, where simulation-based training requires the adoption of numerical integration to unroll the learned dynamics. Recent works have addressed the interplay between numerical integration and rollout-based training, highlighting the role of numerical stability in shaping the loss landscape and its implications for gradient-based optimization. In particular, Fronk and Petzold [2025] isolate the role of numerical integration in causing vanishing gradients for stiff neural ODEs, with numerical stability attenuating the parameter sensitivity associated with stiff modes. The central object is the stability function R ⁡ ( z ) R(z) associated with the numerical scheme. Indeed, for the prototypical case of linear dynamics (e.g Dahlquist test equation), the rollout loss ( 5 ) is minimized exactly at the learned parameters λ ^ \hat{\lambda} satisfying R ⁡ ( λ ^ ​ Δ ​ t ) = e λ ​ Δ ​ t R(\hat{\lambda}{\Delta t})=e^{\lambda{\Delta t}} , implying that | R ⁡ ( λ ^ ​ Δ ​ t ) | < 1 |R(\hat{\lambda}{\Delta t})|<1 in the case of stable dynamics [ Lu and Tsai, 2026 , Theorem 2.1] .
While these results have been derived in the context of neural ODEs, they are not specific to this setting, but rather arise in the broader context of system identification [ Ramsay and Hooker, 2017 , Section 7.4.5] . Loss collapse in the infinite-horizon limit. In this view, the following result characterizes the limiting behavior of the loss landscape ( 5 ) in the long rollout regime, namely as N t → + ∞ {N_{t}}\to+\infty , showing that the loss landscape collapses onto such a stability region 𝒜 Δ ​ t = { λ ^ ∈ ℂ : | R ⁡ ( λ ^ ​ Δ ​ t ) | < 1 } \mathcal{A}_{\Delta t}=\{\hat{\lambda}\in\mathbb{C}:|R(\hat{\lambda}{\Delta t})|<1\} , for a fixed time step Δ ​ t {\Delta t} , and quantifying the rate at which such a collapse occurs. Proposition 2.1 (Loss degeneracy in discrete-time) . Fix Δ ​ t > 0 \Delta t>0 , and x 0 ∈ ℂ ∖ { 0 } x_{0}\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\} . Let the target dynamics be given by the linear system x ˙ = λ ​ x \dot{x}=\lambda x , x ⁡ ( 0 ) = x 0 x(0)=x_{0} , with λ ∈ ℂ \lambda\in\mathbb{C} , Re ⁡ ( λ ) < 0 \operatorname{Re}(\lambda)<0 , observed at t k = k ​ Δ ​ t t_{k}=k{\Delta t} , k = 0 , … , N t k=0,\dots,{N_{t}} . Let the parametric family x ^ ˙ = λ ^ ​ x ^ \dot{\hat{x}}=\hat{\lambda}\hat{x} , x ^ ​ ( 0 ) = x 0 \hat{x}(0)=x_{0} , with λ ^ ∈ ℂ \hat{\lambda}\in\mathbb{C} , be simulated by a one-step Runge-Kutta method with constant step size Δ ​ t {\Delta t} , and stability function R R . Then, lim N t → + ∞ ℓ x , N t ​ ( λ ^ , x 0 ) = { 0 , | R ⁡ ( λ ^ ​ Δ ​ t ) | < 1 , | x 0 | 2 , | R ⁡ ( λ ^ ​ Δ ​ t ) | = 1 , + ∞ , | R ⁡ ( λ ^ ​ Δ ​ t ) | > 1 , \lim_{{N_{t}}\to+\infty}\ell_{x,{N_{t}}}(\hat{\lambda};x_{0})=\begin{cases}0,&|R(\hat{\lambda}{\Delta t})|<1,\\
|x_{0}|^{2},&|R(\hat{\lambda}{\Delta t})|=1,\\
+\infty,&|R(\hat{\lambda}{\Delta t})|>1,\end{cases} (8) and the collapse of the loss within the stability region 𝒜 Δ ​ t \mathcal{A}_{\Delta t} occurs at rate N t − 1 {N_{t}}^{-1} , for every fixed λ ^ \hat{\lambda} . The proof, given in Appendix A.2 , relies on the explicit expression of the rollout loss ( 5 ), amounting to summing three geometric series whose convergence depends on the stability function R R . As a consequence, the limiting objective ( 8 ) is blind to the target dynamics on the whole 𝒜 Δ ​ t \mathcal{A}_{\Delta t} region, admitting a continuum of global minima, despite the fact that the true parameter λ \lambda may lie outside such a region, as illustrated in Figure 3 for the case of explicit Euler integration. This is a statement about the limit, and not about the finite-horizon minimizers, which remain confined to the finite set { λ ^ ∈ ℂ : R ⁡ ( λ ^ ​ Δ ​ t ) = e λ ​ Δ ​ t } \{\hat{\lambda}\in\mathbb{C}:R(\hat{\lambda}{\Delta t})=e^{\lambda{\Delta t}}\} , at every finite N t {N_{t}} , as shown in Lu and Tsai [2026, Lemma 2.1] . Such a collapse highlights that long rollouts do not displace the optimum, but rather flatten the loss landscape around it,
with loss values on 𝒜 Δ ​ t \mathcal{A}_{\Delta t} being pointwise O ⁡ ( N t − 1 ) O({N_{t}}^{-1}) and thus indistinguishable from the minimal value at that rate, representing a possible concern for gradient-based optimization. Jacobian spectral constraint in the infinite-horizon limit. These arguments extend to the nonlinear case, governed by ( 1 ) and ( 3 ) for the target and learned dynamics, respectively; we restrict to the autonomous setting, in which f f and f ^ \hat{f} do not depend explicitly on time. Assuming that the target dynamics admits an asymptotically stable equilibrium, the following result shows that convergence of the series defining the infinite-horizon rollout loss translates into a spectral constraint on the Jacobian ∂ x f ^ \partial_{x}\hat{f} of the learned vector field. Proposition 2.2 (Jacobian spectral constraint) . Fix Δ ​ t > 0 {\Delta t}>0 , and denote by 𝒜 ¯ Δ ​ t := { λ ^ ∈ ℂ : | 1 + λ ^ ​ Δ ​ t | ≤ 1 } \overline{\mathcal{A}}_{\Delta t}:=\{\hat{\lambda}\in\mathbb{C}:|1+\hat{\lambda}{\Delta t}|\leq 1\} .
Let f , f ^ ∈ C 1 ​ ( ℝ n , ℝ n ) f,\hat{f}\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n}) with Δ ​ t ​ Lip ⁡ [ f ^ ] < 1 {\Delta t}\operatorname{Lip}[\hat{f}]<1 , and x ¯ ∈ ℝ n \bar{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n} be an asymptotically stable equilibrium of f f . Let the learned dynamics
be rolled out by explicit Euler, with x ^ k + 1 = x ^ k + Δ ​ t ​ f ^ ​ ( x ^ k ) \hat{x}_{k+1}=\hat{x}_{k}+{\Delta t}\hat{f}(\hat{x}_{k}) .
Take δ > 0 \delta>0 , such that B ⁡ ( x ¯ , δ ) B(\bar{x},\delta) is contained in the basin of attraction of x ¯ \bar{x} for f f , and the rollout loss satisfies lim N t → + ∞ N t ​ ℓ x , N t ​ ( f ^ , x 0 ) < + ∞ , ∀ x 0 ∈ B ⁡ ( x ¯ , δ ) . \lim_{{N_{t}}\to+\infty}{N_{t}}\ell_{x,{N_{t}}}(\hat{f};x_{0})<+\infty,\qquad\forall\,x_{0}\in B(\bar{x},\delta). (9) Then, f ^ ​ ( x ¯ ) = 0 \hat{f}(\bar{x})=0 and σ ⁡ ( ∂ x f ^ ​ ( x ¯ ) ) ⊆ 𝒜 ¯ Δ ​ t \sigma\big(\partial_{x}\hat{f}(\bar{x})\big)\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{\Delta t} . The proof is given in Appendix A.2 .
The two results, Proposition 2.1 and Proposition 2.2 , are in a sense converse to one another at an equilibrium, even stated in different settings.
By the former, membership in 𝒜 Δ ​ t \mathcal{A}_{\Delta t} yields a rollout loss decaying as N t − 1 {N_{t}}^{-1} , that is, square-summable residuals; by the latter, square-summable residuals imply membership in 𝒜 ¯ Δ ​ t \overline{\mathcal{A}}_{\Delta t} .
Hence, rollout training shapes the learned Jacobian ∂ x f ^ ​ ( x ¯ ) \partial_{x}\hat{f}(\bar{x}) , constraining its spectrum to lie within the closure of the stability region of the numerical scheme 𝒜 ¯ Δ ​ t \overline{\mathcal{A}}_{\Delta t} , a set determined by the integrator and the step size alone, independent of the target dynamics.
As a consequence, the stabilization induced by long rollouts training is merely a numerical effect, rather than evidence of fidelity to the true dynamics. While carried out in a simplified setting, these arguments clarify that the challenges of trajectory-based learning are not restricted to the computational and memory costs of long rollouts, but concern the ill-suitedness of the trajectory objective to gradient-based optimization,
further compounded by numerical simulation of the learned dynamics. The above considerations thus motivate alternative, simulation-free, learning strategies, which we address in the following sections. 3 Velocity-based learning To address the challenges of trajectory-based learning, and motivated by the empirical effectiveness of recent velocity-based approaches [ Zhou and Barati Farimani, 2025 , Hou et al., 2026 ] , we develop a theoretical framework for the latter, which will serve as a foundation for the proposed Velocity Matching (VM) objective.
We introduce the theoretical objective over velocities and provide a variational characterization of the velocity field of the observed flow as the unique minimizer of this quadratic objective. A series of stability estimates, in both continuous and discrete time, relate the trajectory-based objective to the velocity-based one. These estimates (i) characterize the gap between the two objectives, both theoretically and empirically; (ii) yield a simulation-free objective, evaluating velocity fields only along observed trajectories and thereby avoiding backpropagation through time; and (iii) reduce the approximation of the true dynamics to the approximation of its velocity field, yielding a universal approximation result for dynamical systems through neural ODEs whose velocity fields are parameterized by deep neural networks. This result, in turn, is shown to guarantee a continuous-time approximation property, which can be interpreted as a time super-resolution property of the learned system. 3.1 Theoretical objective and variational characterization Trajectory-based learning minimizes the discrepancy between true and learned trajectories. The nonlinear dependence of the learned trajectories x ^ ​ ( t , x 0 ) \hat{x}(t,x_{0}) on the learned velocity field f ^ \hat{f} , through the solution of the initial value problem ( 3 ), makes the trajectory-based objective ℒ x \mathcal{L}_{x} non-convex in f ^ \hat{f} , yielding a challenging optimization problem.
Since the object of interest is the velocity field itself, it is natural to cast learning directly as a regression problem over velocities, by regressing the f ^ \hat{f} against the true field f f along observed trajectories, avoiding simulation of the learned system during training. We refer to the resulting population risk over velocities as the theoretical velocity objective , ℒ f ​ [ f ^ ] = 𝔼 x 0 ∼ ρ 0 ​ ℓ f ​ ( f ^ , x 0 ) , ℓ f ​ ( f ^ , x 0 ) = 1 T ​ ‖ f ⁡ ( ⋅ , x ⁡ ( ⋅ , x 0 ) ) ⏟ ∂ t x ⁡ ( ⋅ , x 0 ) − f ^ ​ ( ⋅ , x ⁡ ( ⋅ , x 0 ) ) ‖ L 2 ​ ( [ 0 , T ] , ℝ n ) 2 . \mathcal{L}_{f}[\hat{f}]=\mathbb{E}_{x_{0}\sim\rho_{0}}\ell_{f}(\hat{f};x_{0}),\quad\ell_{f}(\hat{f};x_{0})=\frac{1}{T}\|\underbrace{f(\cdot,x(\cdot,x_{0}))}_{\partial_{t}x(\cdot,x_{0})}-\hat{f}(\cdot,x(\cdot,x_{0}))\|^{2}_{L^{2}([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{n})}. (10) The objective ℒ f \mathcal{L}_{f} is a quadratic functional of the learned velocity field f ^ \hat{f} , whereas ℒ x \mathcal{L}_{x} is not.
Before addressing the relationship between the two objectives, we provide a variational characterization of the velocity field associated with the observed flow, showing that the true velocity field f f is the unique minimizer of ℒ f \mathcal{L}_{f} , which provides a first justification for casting learning as a regression problem over velocities. The arguments are carried out in the autonomous setting, in which f f does not depend explicitly on time and the flow map x ⁡ ( t , x 0 ) x(t,x_{0}) solves ∂ t x ⁡ ( t , x 0 ) = f ⁡ ( x ⁡ ( t , x 0 ) ) , x ⁡ ( 0 , x 0 ) = x 0 ∈ 𝒳 0 , \partial_{t}x(t,x_{0})=f(x(t,x_{0})),\quad x(0,x_{0})=x_{0}\in\mathcal{X}_{0}, (11) over [ 0 , T ] [0,T] , for all x 0 ∈ 𝒳 0 x_{0}\in\mathcal{X}_{0} . Recalling that ρ 0 \rho_{0} denotes the distribution of initial conditions, compactly supported on 𝒳 0 \mathcal{X}_{0} , we introduce the occupation measure μ \mu associated with the flow of ( 11 ), defined as μ = 1 T ​ ∫ 0 T x ​ ( t , ⋅ ) # ​ ρ 0 ​ 𝑑 t , \mu=\frac{1}{T}\int_{0}^{T}x(t,\cdot)_{\#}\rho_{0}\,dt, (12) namely, the time-averaged push-forward of ρ 0 \rho_{0} under the flow map, so that μ ⁡ ( A ) \mu(A) represents the fraction of time spent in A ⊂ ℝ n A\subset\mathbb{R}^{n} by trajectories starting from x 0 ∼ ρ 0 x_{0}\sim\rho_{0} .
Note that μ \mu is a probability measure, and is the normalized, ρ 0 \rho_{0} -averaged state marginal of the occupation measure of the process ( t , x ⁡ ( t , x 0 ) ) (t,x(t,x_{0})) used in the context of optimal control [ Lasserre et al., 2008 ] . As shown below, μ \mu turns the time-averaged regression problem over velocities into a space-averaged one, more amenable to a variational characterization of the velocity field. Proposition 3.1 (Velocity regression via occupation measure) . Let f ∈ W loc 1 , ∞ ​ ( ℝ n , ℝ n ) f\in W^{1,\infty}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n}) , x ⁡ ( t , x 0 ) x(t,x_{0}) be the flow of ( 11 ), and μ \mu be the associated occupation measure ( 12 ). Then, for every Borel measurable f ^ : ℝ n → ℝ n \hat{f}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n} , it holds that ℒ f ​ [ f ^ ] = 𝔼 x ∼ μ ​ | f ⁡ ( x ) − f ^ ​ ( x ) | 2 . \mathcal{L}_{f}[\hat{f}]=\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mu}|f(x)-\hat{f}(x)|^{2}. (13) As a consequence of the above result, ℒ f \mathcal{L}_{f} can be identified with the population risk of a noise-free regression problem over the velocity field f f , with respect to the occupation measure μ \mu . In particular,
the true velocity field f f can be characterized as the unique minimizer of the theoretical velocity objective ℒ f \mathcal{L}_{f} over the μ \mu -weighted L 2 ​ ( ℝ n , ℝ n ) L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n}) space, denoted by L 2 ​ ( μ , ℝ n ) L^{2}(\mu;\mathbb{R}^{n}) , as stated in the following result. Proposition 3.2 (Variational characterization) . Let f ∈ W loc 1 , ∞ ​ ( ℝ n , ℝ n ) f\in W^{1,\infty}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n}) , and μ \mu be the occupation measure associated with the flow of ( 11 ). Then, for f ^ ∈ L 2 ​ ( μ , ℝ n ) \hat{f}\in L^{2}(\mu;\mathbb{R}^{n}) , it holds that ℒ f ​ [ f ^ ] = 0 ⇔ f ^ = f ​ μ ​ -a.e. \mathcal{L}_{f}[\hat{f}]=0\iff\hat{f}=f\ \mu\text{-a.e.} (14) In particular, f f is the unique minimizer of ℒ f \mathcal{L}_{f} over L 2 ​ ( μ , ℝ n ) L^{2}(\mu;\mathbb{R}^{n}) . The proofs are given in Appendix B.1 .
While Proposition 3.1 only requires f ^ \hat{f} to be Borel measurable, which suffices for the subsequent variational characterization, well-posedness of the learned system ( 3 ) requires f ^ \hat{f} to be locally Lipschitz continuous.
Proposition 3.2 determines f f only on the support of μ \mu , namely the region of the state space visited by trajectories starting from 𝒳 0 \mathcal{X}_{0} . This mirrors the correspondence between velocity fields and flow maps arising from elementary arguments. Indeed, by Cauchy–Lipschitz theory, a locally Lipschitz continuous velocity field f f generates a unique flow map x ⁡ ( t , x 0 ) x(t,x_{0}) , solution of ( 11 ); conversely, a flow map determines its velocity field pointwise through the relation f ⁡ ( x ⁡ ( t , x 0 ) ) = ∂ t x ⁡ ( t , x 0 ) f(x(t,x_{0}))=\partial_{t}x(t,x_{0}) , but only at points visited by the flow. To a first approximation, the above consideration thus suggests minimizing a Monte Carlo estimate of ℒ f \mathcal{L}_{f} , which regresses f ^ \hat{f} onto f f at points sampled from the occupation measure μ \mu . This would require access to pointwise evaluations of the true velocity field f f at sampled points, which are not available in practice, as only flow observations are accessible.
In other words, ℒ f \mathcal{L}_{f} is a population risk whose empirical counterpart cannot be directly computed from data, motivating the introduction of suitable surrogate objectives involving velocity estimation from flow observations; these are the subject of Section 4 . Prior to that, we relate trajectory-based and velocity-based objectives. 3.2 Stability estimates and objective control In this section we address the relationship between the trajectory-based objective ℒ x \mathcal{L}_{x} and the velocity-based one ℒ f \mathcal{L}_{f} , by deriving stability estimates, in both continuous and discrete-time, that allow to control the former in terms of the latter, up to a stability constant quantifying the gap between the two. 3.2.1 Continuous-time and discrete-time stability estimates In a first instance, we provide a series of estimates in a continuous-time setting which are closely related to well-posedness results in the classical ODE theory [ Teschl, 2012 , Theorem 2.8] . The main goal is to bound the error between true x ⁡ ( t , x 0 ) x(t,x_{0}) and learned trajectories x ^ ​ ( t , x 0 ) \hat{x}(t,x_{0}) , in terms of the error between true f f and learned velocity fields f ^ \hat{f} along the true trajectory, with the aim of then using the latter as a learning objective.
Evaluation of the velocity fields only along the true trajectory turns out to be the key feature enabling to shift from a simulation-based framework to a simulation-free one, as the absence of x ^ ​ ( t , x 0 ) \hat{x}(t,x_{0}) in the right-hand side of the estimates allows to evaluate the velocity error without simulating the learned system, hence avoiding the need for backpropagation through time (i.e. through the numerical solver steps). As a result, the price to pay is the presence of a stability constant depending on f ^ \hat{f} through its Lipschitz constant, which is a classical feature of Gronwall estimates. However, this is not a limitation in practice, and is actually the gap for which proper control is required, and which the proposed velocity matching (VM) objective aims to address. The result is stated in a general setting, allowing for time-dependent velocity fields, under the assumption of local Lipschitz continuity in the state variable, uniformly in time, which is sufficient to ensure well-posedness of the target and learned systems. The estimates are provided in different norms, denoting by L p L^{p} - L q L^{q} the estimate of the L p L^{p} -norm of the trajectory error in terms of the L q L^{q} -norm of the velocity error, for p , q ∈ { 2 , ∞ } p,q\in\{2,\infty\} which are the most relevant in our context. Lemma 3.3 (Continuous-time stability estimates) . Let 𝒳 0 ⊂ ℝ n \mathcal{X}_{0}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n} be compact and T > 0 T>0 finite.
Let f , f ^ ∈ C 0 ​ ( [ 0 , T ] , W loc 1 , ∞ ​ ( ℝ n , ℝ n ) ) f,\hat{f}\in C^{0}([0,T];W^{1,\infty}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})) , and assume that for every x 0 ∈ 𝒳 0 x_{0}\in\mathcal{X}_{0} the initial value
problems ( 1 ), ( 3 ) admit classical solutions on [ 0 , T ] [0,T] . Then there exists a compact set K ⊂ ℝ n K\subset\mathbb{R}^{n} containing x ⁡ ( t , x 0 ) x(t,x_{0}) and x ^ ​ ( t , x 0 ) \hat{x}(t,x_{0}) for every t ∈ [ 0 , T ] t\in[0,T] and every x 0 ∈ 𝒳 0 x_{0}\in\mathcal{X}_{0} , such that, setting Λ := Lip ⁡ [ f ^ | K ] \Lambda:=\operatorname{Lip}[\hat{f}|_{K}] , the L p L^{p} - L q L^{q} estimate ‖ x ⁡ ( ⋅ , x 0 ) − x ^ ​ ( ⋅ , x 0 ) ‖ L p ​ ( [ 0 , T ] , ℝ n ) ≤ C p , q ​ ( Λ ) ​ ‖ f ⁡ ( ⋅ , x ⁡ ( ⋅ , x 0 ) ) − f ^ ​ ( ⋅ , x ⁡ ( ⋅ , x 0 ) ) ‖ L q ​ ( [ 0 , T ] , ℝ n ) , ∀ x 0 ∈ 𝒳 0 , \|x(\cdot,x_{0})-\hat{x}(\cdot,x_{0})\|_{L^{p}([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq C_{p,q}(\Lambda)\|f(\cdot,x(\cdot,x_{0}))-\hat{f}(\cdot,x(\cdot,x_{0}))\|_{L^{q}([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{n})},\quad\forall x_{0}\in\mathcal{X}_{0}, (15) holds for the following ( p , q ) (p,q) -pairs, with the corresponding constants: 1. p = q = ∞ p=q=\infty , with C ∞ , ∞ ​ ( Λ ) = ( e Λ ​ T − 1 ) / Λ C_{\infty,\infty}(\Lambda)=(e^{\Lambda T}-1)/\Lambda ; 2. p = ∞ p=\infty , q = 2 q=2 , with C ∞ , 2 ​ ( Λ ) = ( e 2 ​ Λ ​ T − 1 ) / ( 2 ​ Λ ) C_{\infty,2}(\Lambda)=\sqrt{(e^{2\Lambda T}-1)/(2\Lambda)} ; 3. p = q = 2 p=q=2 , with C 2 , 2 ​ ( Λ ) = e 2 ​ Λ ​ T − 2 ​ Λ ​ T − 1 / ( 2 ​ Λ ) C_{2,2}(\Lambda)=\sqrt{e^{2\Lambda T}-2\Lambda T-1}/(2\Lambda) . The three estimates of the above result, whose proof is provided in Appendix B.2 , serve different purposes. First, the L ∞ L^{\infty} - L ∞ L^{\infty} estimate, which provides a uniform bound on the trajectory error in terms of the uniform error between true and learned velocity fields along the true trajectory, is central to establishing universal and continuous-time approximation results in the context of neural ODEs, as discussed in Section 3.3 . The L 2 L^{2} - L 2 L^{2} estimate is relevant in practice, as it allows to relate the two theoretical objectives ℒ x \mathcal{L}_{x} and ℒ f \mathcal{L}_{f} , as they are defined in terms of L 2 L^{2} -norms over time, hence providing a theoretical justification for the adoption of the L 2 L^{2} velocity-based objective ℒ f \mathcal{L}_{f} , discussed in Section 3.2.2 . Finally, the L ∞ L^{\infty} - L 2 L^{2} estimate shows that the L 2 L^{2} velocity-based objective ℒ f \mathcal{L}_{f} is sufficient to control the trajectory error uniformly, a fact which is relevant in practice, as it will be used to establish control of the theoretical trajectory loss ℓ x \ell_{x} in terms of the proposed velocity matching loss, as discussed in Section 4.3 . The dependence of the stability constant C p , q ​ ( Λ ) C_{p,q}(\Lambda) on the Lipschitz constant of the learned velocity field f ^ \hat{f} points towards the need of controlling Lip ⁡ [ f ^ ] \operatorname{Lip}[\hat{f}] during training. The scalar linear setting provides further insights, as shown in Figure 4 . The overall bound, being the product of C p , q ​ ( Λ ) C_{p,q}(\Lambda) and the velocity error, is non-convex in λ ^ \hat{\lambda} , whereas the velocity error alone is convex and attains its minimum at λ ^ = λ \hat{\lambda}=\lambda , as does the trajectory error. The fact that the stability constant is minimized at λ ^ = 0 \hat{\lambda}=0 , highlights the fact that arbitrary regularization may pull the learned velocity field towards a trivial solution. Figure 4: Continuous-time stability estimate in a linear setting. Representation of the different contributions appearing in the continuous-time stability estimates in a simplified linear setting, with f ⁡ ( t , x ) = λ ​ x f(t,x)=\lambda x , f ^ ​ ( t , x ) = λ ^ ​ x \hat{f}(t,x)=\hat{\lambda}x , and λ , λ ^ ∈ ℝ \lambda,\hat{\lambda}\in\mathbb{R} . The trajectory ℓ ¯ x \bar{\ell}_{x} and velocity ℓ ¯ f \bar{\ell}_{f} losses are normalized by the squared L 2 L^{2} -norm of the reference trajectory, and shown as a function of the learned parameter λ ^ \hat{\lambda} , with true parameter λ = − 2 \lambda=-2 (left) and λ = 2 \lambda=2 (right). The bound (dashed green) decomposes into a product of two terms, namely ℓ ¯ f \bar{\ell}_{f} and the stability constant C 2 , 2 2 ​ ( Λ ) C_{2,2}^{2}(\Lambda) , with the former being convex in λ ^ \hat{\lambda} , and sharing the same minimum with ℓ ¯ x \bar{\ell}_{x} . A discrete-time counterpart of the continuous-time stability estimates can be established as well, when the learned system is simulated with a one-step Runge-Kutta (RK) scheme. Here, the idea is to relate the trajectory error to the one-step residual ε k + 1 \varepsilon_{k+1} , which measures the mismatch between true and learned dynamics over a single time step, when the current reference state x ⁡ ( t k ) x(t_{k}) is fed as input to scheme, and is defined as ε k + 1 ( x 0 ) := x ( t k + 1 , x 0 ) − x ( t k , x 0 ) − Δ t F ( t k , x ( t k , x 0 ) , Δ t ; f ^ ) , k = 0 , … , N t − 1 , \varepsilon_{k+1}(x_{0}):=x(t_{k+1},x_{0})-x(t_{k},x_{0})-{\Delta t}\,F(t_{k},x(t_{k},x_{0}),{\Delta t};\hat{f}),\quad k=0,\ldots,{N_{t}}-1, (16) with F F being the increment function associated with the RK scheme, in Henrici’s notation [ Quarteroni et al., 2007 , Hairer et al., 2008 ] . The proof of the following result is provided in Appendix B.2 . Lemma 3.4 (Discrete-time stability estimate) . Let 𝒳 0 ⊂ ℝ n \mathcal{X}_{0}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n} be compact, T > 0 T>0 finite, N t ∈ ℕ {N_{t}}\in\mathbb{N} , and Δ ​ t = T / N t {\Delta t}=T/{N_{t}} . Let f , f ^ ∈ C 0 ​ ( [ 0 , T ] , W loc 1 , ∞ ​ ( ℝ n , ℝ n ) ) f,\hat{f}\in C^{0}([0,T];W^{1,\infty}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})) , and assume that for every x 0 ∈ 𝒳 0 x_{0}\in\mathcal{X}_{0} the initial value problem ( 1 ) admits a classical solution on [ 0 , T ] [0,T] . Let { x ^ k ​ ( x 0 ) } k = 0 N t \{\hat{x}_{k}(x_{0})\}_{k=0}^{{N_{t}}} be the numerical solution obtained from integrating f ^ \hat{f} with a one-step explicit Runge-Kutta scheme with increment function F F , and step size Δ ​ t {\Delta t} from the initial condition x 0 x_{0} .
Then there exists a compact set K ⊂ ℝ n K\subset\mathbb{R}^{n} containing x ⁡ ( t , x 0 ) x(t,x_{0}) and x ^ k ​ ( x 0 ) \hat{x}_{k}(x_{0}) for every t ∈ [ 0 , T ] t\in[0,T] , k = 0 , … , N t k=0,\ldots,{N_{t}} , and x 0 ∈ 𝒳 0 x_{0}\in\mathcal{X}_{0} , such that max 0 ≤ k ≤ N t ⁡ | x ⁡ ( t k , x 0 ) − x ^ k ​ ( x 0 ) | ≤ C Δ ​ t ​ ( Λ ) ​ ∑ k = 1 N t | ε k ​ ( x 0 ) | , ∀ x 0 ∈ 𝒳 0 , \max_{0\leq k\leq{N_{t}}}|x(t_{k},x_{0})-\hat{x}_{k}(x_{0})|\leq C_{{\Delta t}}(\Lambda)\sum_{k=1}^{{N_{t}}}|\varepsilon_{k}(x_{0})|,\quad\forall\,x_{0}\in\mathcal{X}_{0}, (17) with C Δ ​ t ​ ( Λ ) = e T ​ Λ C_{{\Delta t}}(\Lambda)=e^{T\Lambda} and Λ := sup t ∈ [ 0 , T ] Lip ⁡ [ F ⁡ ( t , ⋅ , Δ ​ t , f ^ ) | K ] \Lambda:=\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\operatorname{Lip}[F(t,\cdot\,;{\Delta t},\hat{f})|_{K}] . Corollary 3.7 leverages the relation between the one-step residual ε k \varepsilon_{k} and the local truncation error (LTE), used to characterize the consistency of numerical methods for ODEs, to explicitly relate the empirical rollout objective ( 5 ) to the one-step teacher forcing objective ( 7 ). Remark 1 (Lax equivalence perspective) . The stability estimates provided in Lemma 3.3 and Lemma 3.4 can be interpreted as a Lax-Richtmyer, or, in an ODE context, Dahlquist-type equivalence result [ Lax and Richtmyer, 1956 , Dahlquist, 1956 ] .
In classical convergence theory for numerical methods for ODEs, stability estimates relate the convergence of the numerical solution to the notion of consistency, defined by means of the local truncation error, through a stability constant depending on the Lipschitz constant of the vector field, with the source of error being due to a numerical discretization. Here, the provided estimates mimic the same argument, with the difference that the source of error is due to an approximation f ^ \hat{f} of the true vector field f f , rather than a numerical discretization. The notion of consistency is here read in an infinitesimal way, as the error between true and learned velocity fields along the true trajectory, while convergence is read in terms of trajectory error over [ 0 , T ] [0,T] .
The role of numerical schemes, which inevitably enter the picture when simulating the learned dynamics, is made explicit in Lemma 3.4 , which more naturally fits into the classical Lax-Dahlquist framework. 3.2.2 Objective control Here a series of corollaries are provided, which are a direct consequence of the stability estimates established in the previous section, and relate the trajectory-based objective to the velocity-based one.
