While the results of this section hold at fixed f ^ \hat{f} , reading them as a justification for minimizing the velocity objective in place of the trajectory one, however, clearly requires the stability constant not to degrade as f ^ \hat{f} varies. Therefore, by restricting the hypothesis class to velocity fields of uniformly bounded Lipschitz constant, the following results can be read as a justification for minimizing the velocity objective ℒ f \mathcal{L}_{f} in place of the trajectory one ℒ x \mathcal{L}_{x} , given uniformly bounded stability constants C p , q ​ ( Λ ) C_{p,q}(\Lambda) . Such a restriction is natural in practice, and will be made explicit in the following sections.
First, the continuous-time L 2 L^{2} - L 2 L^{2} stability estimate is leveraged to control the theoretical trajectory-based objective in terms of the theoretical velocity-based one, as stated in the following result. Corollary 3.5 (Theoretical objective control) . Let ℒ x \mathcal{L}_{x} and ℒ f \mathcal{L}_{f} denote the theoretical objectives over trajectories and velocities, respectively defined in ( 4 ) and ( 10 ). Then, under the same assumptions of Lemma 3.3 , it holds that ℒ x ​ [ f ^ ] ≤ C 2 , 2 2 ​ ( Λ ) ​ ℒ f ​ [ f ^ ] . \mathcal{L}_{x}[\hat{f}]\leq C_{2,2}^{2}(\Lambda)\mathcal{L}_{f}[\hat{f}]. (18) The proof is a direct application of the L 2 L^{2} - L 2 L^{2} stability estimate in Lemma 3.3 , and is provided in Appendix B.3 .
The result provides a theoretical justification for the adoption of a velocity-based objective ℒ f \mathcal{L}_{f} as a surrogate objective for the trajectory-based objective ℒ x \mathcal{L}_{x} . The former is quadratic in f ^ \hat{f} and simulation-free, since f ^ \hat{f} is evaluated only along the reference trajectory x ⁡ ( t , x 0 ) x(t,x_{0}) , thus making it more amenable to optimization. As a further consequence of the continuous-time stability estimates, a bound on the 2-Wasserstein distance between the push-forward of the distribution over initial conditions ρ 0 \rho_{0} under the true and learned flow maps, namely ρ t = x ​ ( t , ⋅ ) # ​ ρ 0 \rho_{t}=x(t,\cdot)_{\#}\rho_{0} and ρ ^ t = x ^ ​ ( t , ⋅ ) # ​ ρ 0 \hat{\rho}_{t}=\hat{x}(t,\cdot)_{\#}\rho_{0} , can be established as well.
While weaker than the previous ones, the following result is relevant when the goal consists in recovering statistical properties of the system, rather than pointwise quantities, as it may be the case in the chaotic setting. Corollary 3.6 (2-Wasserstein stability estimate) . Let ρ 0 \rho_{0} denote a distribution compactly supported on the set of admissible initial conditions 𝒳 0 ⊂ ℝ n \mathcal{X}_{0}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n} . Let ρ t \rho_{t} and ρ ^ t \hat{\rho}_{t} be defined as the push-forward of ρ 0 \rho_{0} under the flow maps x ⁡ ( t , ⋅ ) x(t,\cdot) and x ^ ​ ( t , ⋅ ) \hat{x}(t,\cdot) , respectively. Then, under the same assumptions of Lemma 3.3 , the following 2 2 -Wasserstein stability estimate holds 𝒲 2 2 ​ ( ρ t , ρ ^ t ) ≤ T ​ C ∞ , 2 2 ​ ( Λ ) ​ ℒ f ​ [ f ^ ] , ∀ t ∈ [ 0 , T ] . \mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\rho_{t},\hat{\rho}_{t})\leq TC_{\infty,2}^{2}(\Lambda)\mathcal{L}_{f}[\hat{f}],\quad\forall t\in[0,T]. (19) The proof is provided in Appendix B.3 . The estimate relates to Li et al. [2026, Theorem 2.7] , who establishes universal approximation rates for transport equations via neural ODE approximators, namely neural transport equations , in 1 1 -Wasserstein distance. The result builds upon the duality between the ODEs ( 1 ) and ( 3 ) and the associated continuity equation [ Ambrosio et al., 2021 , Proposition 16.4] , with the densities ρ t \rho_{t} and ρ ^ t \hat{\rho}_{t} being its weak solutions, while the flow maps x ⁡ ( t , ⋅ ) x(t,\cdot) and x ^ ​ ( t , ⋅ ) \hat{x}(t,\cdot) are its characteristics. In this view, the result finds further application in the context of flow-based generative modeling [ Gao et al., 2024 , Ding et al., 2026 ] , where the learned flow map transports a source distribution to match the target distribution at some final time t = T t=T . In particular, the bound ( 19 ) connects to flow matching objectives [ Lipman et al., 2023 , Tong et al., 2024 ] , where minimization of 𝒲 2 ​ ( ρ T , ρ ^ T ) \mathcal{W}_{2}(\rho_{T},\hat{\rho}_{T}) is achieved by regressing the learned velocity field f ^ \hat{f} to the true one f f , through the minimization of a velocity-based objective ℒ f \mathcal{L}_{f} . Adopting the notation of Section 2.2 , the teacher forcing (TF) objective at time t k t_{k} admits a natural numerical interpretation. Defining the local truncation error (LTE) as τ k + 1 = ε k + 1 / Δ ​ t \tau_{k+1}=\varepsilon_{k+1}/{\Delta t} , the TF objective ( 7 ) is, up to a rescaling factor Δ ​ t 2 {\Delta t}^{2} , the mean squared LTE.
By classical numerical analysis, the LTE measures the consistency of a scheme, and its decay rate in Δ ​ t {\Delta t} characterizes the order of the scheme itself. Here the residual, and hence the LTE, carries an additional contribution arising from the evaluation of F F at the learned velocity field f ^ \hat{f} , rather than the true one f f , as the following decomposition makes explicit τ k + 1 = ( x ⁡ ( t k + 1 ) − x ⁡ ( t k ) Δ ​ t − F ⁡ ( t k , x ⁡ ( t k ) , Δ ​ t , f ) ) + ( F ⁡ ( t k , x ⁡ ( t k ) , Δ ​ t , f ) − F ⁡ ( t k , x ⁡ ( t k ) , Δ ​ t , f ^ ) ) . \tau_{k+1}=\left(\frac{x(t_{k+1})-x(t_{k})}{{\Delta t}}-F\big(t_{k},x(t_{k}),{\Delta t};f\big)\right)+\left(F\big(t_{k},x(t_{k}),{\Delta t};f\big)-F\big(t_{k},x(t_{k}),{\Delta t};\hat{f}\big)\right). (20) The first term in ( 20 ) is the classical LTE associated with the numerical scheme applied to the true velocity field f f . Independent of the learned velocity field f ^ \hat{f} , it can be made arbitrarily small by reducing the step size Δ ​ t {\Delta t} , being of order O ⁡ ( Δ ​ t p ) O({\Delta t}^{p}) for a scheme of order p ≥ 1 p\geq 1 .
At a fixed Δ ​ t {\Delta t} this term is governed solely by the order of the scheme, and may introduce a bias dominating the second term, hence motivating the adoption of higher-order schemes during training [ Krishnapriyan et al., 2023 , Zhou and Barati Farimani, 2025 ] .
The second term accounts instead for the mismatch between the true and learned velocity fields along the reference trajectory x ⁡ ( t k ) x(t_{k}) , by means of the increment function F F of the adopted scheme. TF thus minimizes such a second term, which for the case of explicit Euler reads as f ⁡ ( t k , x ⁡ ( t k ) ) − f ^ ​ ( t k , x ⁡ ( t k ) ) f(t_{k},x(t_{k}))-\hat{f}(t_{k},x(t_{k})) , thereby yielding an empirical counterpart of the continuous-time velocity-based objective ℒ f \mathcal{L}_{f} in ( 10 ). The following result establishes a connection between the rollout empirical risk ( 5 ) and the TF empirical risk ( 7 ), by leveraging the discrete-time stability estimate in Lemma 3.4 . Corollary 3.7 (Empirical objective control) . Let ℒ x , N , N t ​ [ f ^ ] \mathcal{L}_{x,N,{N_{t}}}[\hat{f}] denote the rollout empirical objective defined in ( 5 ), and ℒ x , N , N t , 1 ​ [ f ^ ] \mathcal{L}_{x,N,{N_{t}},1}[\hat{f}] denote the empirical objective associated with the teacher forcing loss defined in ( 7 ). Then, under the same assumptions of Lemma 3.4 , it holds that ℒ x , N , N t ​ [ f ^ ] ≤ N t 2 ​ C Δ ​ t 2 ​ ( Λ ) ​ ℒ x , N , N t , 1 ​ [ f ^ ] . \mathcal{L}_{x,N,{N_{t}}}[\hat{f}]\leq N_{t}^{2}C^{2}_{{\Delta t}}(\Lambda)\,\mathcal{L}_{x,N,{N_{t}},1}[\hat{f}]. (21) The proof is provided in Appendix B.3 . While the right-hand side of the above bound still reads as a trajectory-based quantity, the object at play is the velocity-based formulation of the TF loss ( 7 ), with the stability constant C Δ ​ t ​ ( Λ ) C_{{\Delta t}}(\Lambda) being inherited from the discrete-time stability estimate of Lemma 3.4 . The results of this section thus close the chain of arguments bridging the gap between trajectory-based and velocity-based objectives. In continuous-time, the theoretical trajectory objective ℒ x \mathcal{L}_{x} is controlled by the quadratic theoretical velocity objective ℒ f \mathcal{L}_{f} , quadratic in f ^ \hat{f} ; in discrete-time, the multi-step rollout loss is controlled by the one-step TF loss, with suitable stability constants quantifying the gap in either case. Simulation is thereby removed from training, at the price of a constant depending on the Lipschitz constant of the learned velocity field f ^ \hat{f} , growing with the time horizon T T . From an empirical perspective, what remains to be addressed is the estimation of velocities themselves from discrete observations, leading to the proposed velocity matching (VM) objective, discussed in Section 4 . 3.3 Universal and continuous-time approximation In this section we consider the case when the learned velocity field f ^ \hat{f} is parametrized by a deep neural network, and show that, under suitable regularity assumptions on the true vector field f f , the associated flow map x ⁡ ( t , x 0 ) x(t,x_{0}) can be approximated with arbitrary accuracy by a flow map x ^ ​ ( t , x 0 ) \hat{x}(t,x_{0}) generated by a deep neural network f ^ \hat{f} that approximates f f in W 1 , ∞ W^{1,\infty} -norm.
The result has twofold implications. First, it provides insights on the role of the Lipschitz constant of the learned vector field f ^ \hat{f} , which characterizes the stability constant in the provided estimates, hinting towards the adoption of a suitable empirical objective, which, in addition to minimizing the velocity error along observed trajectories, steers the Lipschitz constant of the learned vector field f ^ \hat{f} towards the one of the target vector field f f , rather than relying on arbitrary regularization strategies. Second, it enables to establish a continuous-time approximation result for neural ODEs, characterizing time super-resolution capabilities of the learned system at inference time, when simulating the learned dynamics with arbitrary time step size. 3.3.1 Universal approximation of flow maps Before establishing continuous-time approximation properties of the learned flow, we prove that the flow map of ( 1 ) is universally approximated by flow maps associated with neural ODEs ( 3 ), whose learned velocity field f ^ \hat{f} is parametrized by a deep neural network.
The argument rests on the L ∞ L^{\infty} - L ∞ L^{\infty} stability estimate of Lemma 3.3 , which bounds the trajectory error between true and learned flow maps in terms of the error between true and learned velocity fields along the reference trajectory, and thereby transfers the approximation problem from the space of flow maps to the space of velocity fields. The latter is then addressed by relying on universal approximation results for deep neural networks in Sobolev spaces [ Gühring and Raslan, 2021 ] . Two aspects require care for the application of the stability estimate, and for the argument to go through. First, given the dependence of the estimate on the Lipschitz constant of the learned velocity field f ^ \hat{f} , through the stability constant C ∞ , ∞ ​ ( Λ ) C_{\infty,\infty}(\Lambda) , the approximation must be carried out in W 1 , ∞ W^{1,\infty} -norm, rather than in L ∞ L^{\infty} -norm, so that joint control over velocity error and Lipschitz constants can be established. Second, both flows must be confined to a common compact set, which for the learned flow is not given a priori and can be established by means of an escape argument, standing on the following two assumptions. Assumption 1 . Denoting by K K the closed δ \delta -neighborhood 1 1 1 We define the closed δ \delta -neighborhood of a set Ω ⊂ ℝ n \Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n} as the set { x ∈ ℝ n : inf y ∈ Ω | x − y | ≤ δ } \{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:\inf_{y\in\Omega}|x-y|\leq\delta\} . of the convex hull of the reachable set 𝒳 \mathcal{X} defined in ( 2 ), the target velocity field f f belongs to ℱ = { g ∈ W loc 2 , ∞ ​ ( ℝ n + 1 , ℝ n ) : ‖ g ‖ W 2 , ∞ ​ ( [ 0 , T ] × K , ℝ n ) ≤ M } , \mathcal{F}=\left\{g\in W^{2,\infty}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1};\mathbb{R}^{n}):\|g\|_{W^{2,\infty}([0,T]\times K;\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq M\right\}, (22) for some δ > 0 \delta>0 and M > 0 M>0 . Assumption 2 . The activation function ϱ : ℝ → ℝ \varrho:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R} belongs to W loc 1 , ∞ ​ ( ℝ ) W^{1,\infty}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}) , and is admissible for Gühring and Raslan [2021, Proposition 4.8, Theorem 4.9] . Denote by 𝒩 ϱ ​ ( ℝ n + 1 , ℝ n ) \mathcal{N}_{\varrho}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1};\mathbb{R}^{n}) the class of feedforward neural networks with input dimension n + 1 n+1 , output dimension n n , and activation function ϱ \varrho . Assumption 2 then guarantees that 𝒩 ϱ ​ ( ℝ n + 1 , ℝ n ) ⊂ W loc 1 , ∞ ​ ( ℝ n + 1 , ℝ n ) \mathcal{N}_{\varrho}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1};\mathbb{R}^{n})\subset W^{1,\infty}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1};\mathbb{R}^{n}) , by the same argument as in Gühring et al. [2020, Remark 2.12] , yielding local Lipschitz continuity of the learned velocity field f ^ \hat{f} , and hence local existence and uniqueness of the associated flow map x ^ ​ ( t , x 0 ) \hat{x}(t,x_{0}) .
The admissibility conditions required by Assumption 2 involve a technical condition on the differentiability of the activation function, requiring non-vanishing second derivative at some point, and that ϱ \varrho admits an approximate partition of unity, in the sense of Gühring and Raslan [2021, Definition 4.2] , whose decay defines in turn the approximation rate of the associated neural network class. A wide class of activation functions, including exponential linear units (ELUs) and sigmoidal linear units (SiLU), satisfy these conditions.
We note that rectified linear units (ReLU), while in W loc 1 , ∞ ​ ( ℝ ) W^{1,\infty}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}) , are not admissible for the results of Gühring and Raslan [2021] , and hence excluded from Assumption 2 . These results can be eventually recovered by relying on Yarotsky [2017] , as discussed in Gühring and Raslan [2021, Remark 4.10] , at the cost of a depth bound which is no longer independent of the approximation accuracy. This is not a limitation in our context, as further regularity assumptions on the activation function ϱ \varrho will be required in Section 3.3.2 to establish continuous-time approximation results, and will be made explicit therein.
We proceed to establish the result, whose proof is provided in Appendix B.4 . Theorem 3.8 (Universal approximation of flow maps) . Let f f satisfy Assumption 1 , and ϱ \varrho satisfy Assumption 2 . Then, for every ϵ > 0 \epsilon>0 there exists a deep neural network f ^ ∈ 𝒩 ϱ ​ ( ℝ n + 1 , ℝ n ) \hat{f}\in\mathcal{N}_{\varrho}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1};\mathbb{R}^{n}) such that the associated flow map x ^ ​ ( t , x 0 ) \hat{x}(t,x_{0}) , solving ( 3 ), exists and remains in K K for all t ∈ [ 0 , T ] t\in[0,T] and x 0 ∈ 𝒳 0 x_{0}\in\mathcal{X}_{0} , and satisfies ‖ x − x ^ ‖ L ∞ ​ ( [ 0 , T ] × 𝒳 0 , ℝ n ) ≤ ϵ . \|x-\hat{x}\|_{L^{\infty}([0,T]\times\mathcal{X}_{0};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq\epsilon. (23) Moreover, for ϱ \varrho being a GELU activation function, f ^ \hat{f} can be chosen with a constant number of layers, independent of ϵ \epsilon , and O ⁡ ( n ​ ϵ − ( n + 1 ) ) O(n\epsilon^{-(n+1)}) nonzero weights. The result establishes that the flow map of ( 1 ) can be uniformly approximated, over [ 0 , T ] [0,T] and the set of admissible initial conditions 𝒳 0 \mathcal{X}_{0} , with arbitrary accuracy by a flow map generated by a deep neural network f ^ \hat{f} , which approximates f f in W 1 , ∞ W^{1,\infty} -norm.
Although the theorem allows for a wide class of activation functions satisfying Assumption 2 , the final statement provides a more explicit characterization for the case of GELU activation [ Hendrycks and Gimpel, 2023 ] , which we adopt in our numerical experiments. The corresponding approximation rate follows from showing that GELU belongs to the class of exponential partitions of unity (PU) [ Gühring and Raslan, 2021 , Definition 4.2] , and by applying Gühring and Raslan [2021, Theorem 4.9] . The argument is made explicit in Appendix B.4 . 3.3.2 Continuous-time approximation A central question of practical relevance is whether, at inference time, the learned neural ODE ( 3 ) can be simulated over arbitrary time discretizations, including ones finer than that employed during training, while retaining a prescribed accuracy in terms of trajectory error. Although neural ODEs rest on a continuous-time formulation, their training relies on discrete-time observations. Consequently, the learned system may overfit the training discretization, potentially compromising its ability to capture the underlying continuous-time dynamics and generalize to finer temporal discretizations.
This property, referred to as continuous-time approximation and interpreted as a form of time super-resolution Krishnapriyan et al., 2023 , Definition 1 ; Farenga et al., 2025 , Definition 1 , can be understood through the decomposition of the trajectory error, over a discrete grid { t k } k = 0 N t \{t_{k}\}_{k=0}^{{N_{t}}} , into two contributions, sup 0 ≤ k ≤ N t | x ⁡ ( t k , x 0 ) − x ^ k ​ ( x 0 ) | ≤ ‖ x ⁡ ( ⋅ , x 0 ) − x ^ ​ ( ⋅ , x 0 ) ‖ L ∞ ​ ( [ 0 , T ] , ℝ n ) + C ⁡ ( f ^ , T ) ​ Δ ​ t p , \sup_{0\leq k\leq{N_{t}}}|x(t_{k},x_{0})-\hat{x}_{k}(x_{0})|\leq\|x(\cdot,x_{0})-\hat{x}(\cdot,x_{0})\|_{L^{\infty}([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{n})}+C(\hat{f},T){\Delta t}^{p}, (24) namely the L ∞ L^{\infty} -error between the true and learned continuous-time trajectories, and the numerical error arising from the integration of the learned system. This decomposition shows that the learned dynamics’ continuous-time approximation properties are governed by two sources of error. The former is independent of the numerical scheme, and is controlled by the universal approximation property of flow maps established in Theorem 3.8 ; whereas the latter is governed by the convergence order of the numerical integration scheme employed at inference time, yielding an O ⁡ ( Δ ​ t p ) O({\Delta t}^{p}) error for a one-step RK scheme of order p ≥ 1 p\geq 1 , provided that the learned velocity field f ^ \hat{f} is sufficiently regular for such an order to be attained [ Hairer et al., 2008 , Section II.3] .
The following result formalizes such an argument. Theorem 3.9 (Continuous-time approximation) . Let f f satisfy Assumption 1 , and ϱ \varrho satisfy Assumption 2 , with in addition ϱ ∈ C p ​ ( ℝ ) \varrho\in C^{p}(\mathbb{R}) for some p ≥ 1 p\geq 1 . Then, for every ϵ > 0 \epsilon>0 there exists a deep neural network f ^ ∈ 𝒩 ϱ ​ ( ℝ n + 1 , ℝ n ) \hat{f}\in\mathcal{N}_{\varrho}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1};\mathbb{R}^{n}) and a step size Δ ​ t ∗ = Δ ​ t ∗ ​ ( ϵ , T , f ^ ) > 0 {\Delta t}^{*}={\Delta t}^{*}(\epsilon,T,\hat{f})>0 such that the numerical solution x ^ k ​ ( x 0 ) \hat{x}_{k}(x_{0}) of ( 3 ), obtained with a one-step RK scheme of order p p over a uniform discretization { t k } k = 0 N t \{t_{k}\}_{k=0}^{{N_{t}}} of [ 0 , T ] [0,T] with step size Δ ​ t = T / N t {\Delta t}=T/{N_{t}} , satisfies sup x 0 ∈ 𝒳 0 sup 0 ≤ k ≤ N t | x ⁡ ( t k , x 0 ) − x ^ k ​ ( x 0 ) | ≤ ϵ , ∀ Δ ​ t ≤ Δ ​ t ∗ . \sup_{x_{0}\in\mathcal{X}_{0}}\sup_{0\leq k\leq{N_{t}}}|x(t_{k},x_{0})-\hat{x}_{k}(x_{0})|\leq\epsilon,\qquad\forall\,{\Delta t}\leq{\Delta t}^{*}. (25) The proof is provided in Appendix B.4 . The result establishes that the learned neural ODE can be simulated at inference time with arbitrary time step size Δ ​ t ≤ Δ ​ t ∗ {\Delta t}\leq{\Delta t}^{*} , while retaining a prescribed accuracy in terms of trajectory error. The associated threshold Δ ​ t ∗ {\Delta t}^{*} is determined by the desired accuracy ϵ \epsilon , through the decomposition in ( 24 ), and by the learned velocity field f ^ \hat{f} in two ways. First, the threshold depends on the constant arising from the O ⁡ ( Δ ​ t p ) O({\Delta t}^{p}) term in ( 24 ), associated with the RK scheme and commonly depending on the Lipschitz constant of f ^ \hat{f} and the time horizon T T . Second, the threshold depends on the order of convergence p p of the numerical scheme, which is guaranteed to be attained only if f ^ \hat{f} is sufficiently regular, hence the requirement on the activation function ϱ \varrho to be C p ​ ( ℝ ) C^{p}(\mathbb{R}) . 4 Velocity matching The stability estimates provided in Section 3 justify replacing the trajectory-based objective with a velocity-based objective, a quadratic functional whose unique minimizer is the true velocity field f f . While avoiding simulation of the learned dynamics, this objective is, however, not directly accessible, as it requires knowledge of the true velocity field along the reference trajectories, not available in practice. Any empirical surrogate of the theoretical velocity objective must therefore rely on velocity estimates as supervision signal from discrete observations of the system state. In this view, this is a price to be paid for removing simulation from training.
Here we introduce a principled velocity-based objective, which we call velocity matching . Rather than supervising the learned velocity field f ^ \hat{f} at the discrete observation points, as in commonly adopted teacher forcing (TF) approaches, VM evaluates f ^ \hat{f} along the piecewise linear interpolant of the observed trajectories, at times sampled uniformly within each interval, and supervises it with the velocity obtained by differentiating the interpolant. In the following, in Section 4.1 we introduce the VM objective and describe its algorithmic implementation, framing it as an instance of an interpolant-based surrogate for ℒ f \mathcal{L}_{f} and relating it to prior work. Section 4.2 provides a bias-variance decomposition of the VM loss, and relates the VM bias term to the residual of an implicit Runge-Kutta method, highlighting the implicit nature of the resulting update. Section 4.3 interprets VM loss as a stochastic quadrature rule, establishing O ⁡ ( Δ ​ t 2 ) O({\Delta t}^{2}) order of approximation of the loss associated with the theoretical velocity objective ℒ f \mathcal{L}_{f} . Section 4.4 casts VM as a regularized velocity regression whose penalty implicitly matches the action of the Jacobian of the learned velocity field to that of the true velocity field. 4.1 Empirical objective and algorithmic aspects We seek an empirical counterpart of the theoretical velocity objective ℒ f \mathcal{L}_{f} , defined in ( 10 ), overcoming the limitation of requiring knowledge of the true velocity field along the reference trajectories. A natural approach, dating back to the early developments of system identification [ Bellman and Roth, 1971 , Varah, 1982 , Ramsay et al., 2007 ] , consists in relying on two-stage estimation, where a prior interpolation step produces a continuous approximation x ~ ​ ( t , x 0 ) \tilde{x}(t,x_{0}) of the observed trajectories, from which velocities can be estimated by differentiating such an interpolant.
Then, the learned vector field f ^ \hat{f} is matched with the estimated velocities along the interpolated trajectories, yielding a surrogate objective for ℒ f \mathcal{L}_{f} , which we denote by ℒ ~ f , N , N t \tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{f,N,{N_{t}}} , reading as ℒ ~ f , N , N t ​ [ f ^ ] = 1 N ​ ∑ i = 1 N ℓ ~ f , N t ​ ( f ^ , x 0 ( i ) ) , ℓ ~ f , N t ​ ( f ^ , x 0 ) = 1 T ​ ‖ ∂ t x ~ ​ ( ⋅ , x 0 ) − f ^ ​ ( ⋅ , x ~ ​ ( ⋅ , x 0 ) ) ‖ L 2 ​ ( [ 0 , T ] , ℝ n ) 2 . \tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{f,N,{N_{t}}}[\hat{f}]=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\tilde{\ell}_{f,{N_{t}}}(\hat{f};x_{0}^{(i)}),\quad\tilde{\ell}_{f,{N_{t}}}(\hat{f};x_{0})=\frac{1}{T}\|\partial_{t}\tilde{x}(\cdot,x_{0})-\hat{f}(\cdot,\tilde{x}(\cdot,x_{0}))\|^{2}_{L^{2}([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{n})}. (26) Although built from the finite set of N t + 1 {N_{t}}+1 observations, the surrogate objective is expressed in continuous-time form through the L 2 L^{2} -norm over [ 0 , T ] [0,T] , since the interpolant x ~ ​ ( ⋅ , x 0 ) \tilde{x}(\cdot,x_{0}) provides a, possibly almost everywhere, continuous approximation of the observed trajectories and velocities, whose regularity is set by the chosen interpolation strategy. While prior approaches have favored globally regular interpolants, as cubic [ Bellman and Roth, 1971 ] or quintic splines [ Hou et al., 2026 ] , we adopt piecewise linear interpolation for the following reasons. First, the L 2 L^{2} objective is insensitive to the regularity at the knots { t k } k = 0 N t \{t_{k}\}_{k=0}^{{N_{t}}} , a set of null measure, so that almost everywhere differentiability of the interpolant suffices to ensure well-posedness of the objective. Second, piecewise linear interpolation carries the computational cost of a first-order finite difference, as opposed to the cost of fitting higher-order splines. Although the resulting supervision signal is now piecewise constant, with discontinuities at the knots, the spectral bias [ Rahaman et al., 2019 , Xu et al., 2019 ] of neural network parameterizations of f ^ \hat{f} allows smoothness to be recovered at the level of the approximator rather than on the target. This choice yields the velocity matching (VM) objective, denoted by ℒ f , N , N t VM \mathcal{L}_{f,N,{N_{t}}}^{\rm{VM}} with per-sample loss ℓ f , N t VM \ell_{f,{N_{t}}}^{\rm{VM}} , as the specific instance of the empirical surrogate objective ( 26 ) obtained from piecewise linear interpolation of the observed trajectories, with x ~ ​ ( t , x 0 ) := x ⁡ ( t k , x 0 ) + ( t − t k ) ​ v k ​ ( x 0 ) , ∂ t x ~ ​ ( t , x 0 ) = v k ​ ( x 0 ) := x ⁡ ( t k + 1 , x 0 ) − x ⁡ ( t k , x 0 ) t k + 1 − t k , \tilde{x}(t,x_{0}):=x(t_{k},x_{0})+(t-t_{k})\,v_{k}(x_{0}),\qquad\partial_{t}\tilde{x}(t,x_{0})=v_{k}(x_{0}):=\dfrac{x(t_{k+1},x_{0})-x(t_{k},x_{0})}{t_{k+1}-t_{k}}, (27) for t ∈ [ t k , t k + 1 ) t\in[t_{k},t_{k+1}) and k = 0 , … , N t − 1 k=0,\dots,{N_{t}}-1 .
Under uniform temporal discretizations, the L 2 L^{2} -norm in ( 26 ) decomposes over subintervals, and sampling the integration variable uniformly within each of them recasts the loss as a stochastic perturbation of the time and space arguments in f ^ \hat{f} . Accordingly, we define the per-sample VM loss as ℓ f , N t VM ​ ( f ^ , x 0 ) = 1 N t ​ ∑ k = 0 N t − 1 𝔼 τ ∼ 𝒰 ⁡ [ 0 , Δ ​ t ] ​ | v k ​ ( x 0 ) − f ^ ​ ( t k + τ , x ⁡ ( t k , x 0 ) + τ ​ v k ​ ( x 0 ) ) | 2 , \ell_{f,{N_{t}}}^{\rm{VM}}(\hat{f};x_{0})=\frac{1}{{N_{t}}}\sum_{k=0}^{{N_{t}}-1}\mathbb{E}_{\tau\sim\mathcal{U}[0,{\Delta t}]}\big|v_{k}(x_{0})-\hat{f}(t_{k}+\tau,x(t_{k},x_{0})+\tau v_{k}(x_{0}))\big|^{2}, (28) which we adopt in practice. Its consistency with the interpolant-based objective is stated below and proved in Appendix C.1 . Proposition 4.1 . The loss ( 28 ) coincides with the surrogate objective ( 26 ) under piecewise linear interpolation ( 27 ), on a uniform temporal discretization of step size Δ ​ t {\Delta t} . Formulation ( 28 ) unfolds the structure of the VM objective in terms of a stochastic perturbation along the interpolant. Analytically, sampling the offset τ \tau to evaluate the learned vector field f ^ \hat{f} along the continuum of each subinterval, rather than at its left endpoint alone as in TF, yields: (i) a higher order of approximation of the theoretical velocity objective, and (ii) an implicit regularization effect on the Jacobian of the learned vector field, as shown in Section 4.3 and Section 4.4 , respectively.
Computationally, it reduces to a sampling procedure mirroring that of flow matching approaches [ Lipman et al., 2023 , Hou et al., 2026 ] , as discussed in Remark 2 . We detail the batched implementation in Algorithm 1 .
In particular, the triple ( i j , k j , τ j ) (i_{j},k_{j},\tau_{j}) denotes the indices of the j j -th sample in the batch, of size B B , with i j i_{j} and k j k_{j} denoting the trajectory and temporal indices, respectively, and τ j \tau_{j} the offset within the selected subinterval. The velocity target v j v_{j} is computed as the first-order finite difference approximation of the velocity along the selected trajectory and temporal index, while the collocation point ( t ~ j , x ~ j ) (\tilde{t}_{j},\tilde{x}_{j}) is obtained by advancing along the chord connecting the two consecutive observations, with offset τ j \tau_{j} . The learned vector field f ^ \hat{f} is then evaluated at the collocation point and matched with the velocity target v j v_{j} . Thus, as each step evaluates the learned vector field at a single collocation point per sample, and constructs the velocity target as a first-order finite difference, the computational cost of VM is comparable to that of an explicit Euler TF update.
Since the offset τ j \tau_{j} is sampled independently across the batch, the batch size B B controls the variance of the resulting gradient estimate. Algorithm 1 Velocity Matching - Batch Training Input: Trajectories { x ⁡ ( t k , x 0 ( i ) ) } k = 0 N t \{x(t_{k};x_{0}^{(i)})\}_{k=0}^{{N_{t}}} with initial conditions { x 0 ( i ) } i = 1 N \{x_{0}^{(i)}\}_{i=1}^{N} , temporal grid { t k } k = 0 N t \{t_{k}\}_{k=0}^{{N_{t}}} of step size Δ ​ t {\Delta t} , learnable velocity field f ^ θ : ℝ n + 1 → ℝ n \hat{f}_{\theta}:\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\to\mathbb{R}^{n} of parameters θ \theta , batch size B B , number of iterations N iter N_{\mathrm{iter}} , learning rate η \eta Output: Updated parameters θ \theta Initialize θ \theta for m = 1 , … , N iter m=1,\ldots,N_{\mathrm{iter}} do Draw B B samples ( i j , k j , τ j ) ∼ 𝒰 ⁡ { 1 , N } × 𝒰 ⁡ { 0 , N t − 1 } × 𝒰 ⁡ [ 0 , Δ ​ t ] (i_{j},k_{j},\tau_{j})\sim\mathcal{U}\{1,N\}\times\mathcal{U}\{0,{N_{t}}-1\}\times\mathcal{U}[0,{\Delta t}] , for j = 1 , … , B j=1,\ldots,B Construct velocity targets v j = x ⁡ ( t k j + 1 , x 0 ( i j ) ) − x ⁡ ( t k j , x 0 ( i j ) ) Δ ​ t v_{j}=\dfrac{x(t_{k_{j}+1};x_{0}^{(i_{j})})-x(t_{k_{j}};x_{0}^{(i_{j})})}{{\Delta t}} , for j = 1 , … , B j=1,\ldots,B Construct temporal collocation points t ~ j = t k j + τ j \tilde{t}_{j}=t_{k_{j}}+\tau_{j} , for j = 1 , … , B j=1,\ldots,B Construct spatial collocation points x ~ j = x ⁡ ( t k j , x 0 ( i j ) ) + τ j ​ v j \tilde{x}_{j}=x(t_{k_{j}};x_{0}^{(i_{j})})+\tau_{j}v_{j} , for j = 1 , … , B j=1,\ldots,B Compute loss ℒ f , batch VM ​ ( θ ) = 1 B ​ ∑ j = 1 B | v j − f ^ θ ​ ( t ~ j , x ~ j ) | 2 \mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{VM}}_{f,\text{batch}}(\theta)=\dfrac{1}{B}\sum_{j=1}^{B}\bigl|v_{j}-\hat{f}_{\theta}(\tilde{t}_{j},\tilde{x}_{j})\bigr|^{2} Gradient step θ ← θ − η ​ ∇ θ ℒ f , batch VM ​ ( θ ) \theta\leftarrow\theta-\eta\nabla_{\theta}\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{VM}}_{f,\text{batch}}(\theta) Return : θ \theta Remark 2 (Connection to flow matching) . The VM construction is formally a flow matching (FM) objective [ Lipman et al., 2023 ] , where a learned velocity field is regressed onto the time derivative of an interpolant joining two endpoint samples, with the velocity field being evaluated along the interpolant itself. The connection with operator learning, in a continuous-time setting, was made explicit by Hou et al. [2026] , within the stochastic interpolant framework [ Albergo and Vanden-Eijnden, 2023 , Albergo et al., 2025 ] .
However, VM differs from FM in the coupling between endpoints and, as a result, in the object being learned. Denote by ρ ⁡ ( t ) \rho(t) the law of x ⁡ ( t , x 0 ) x(t,x_{0}) with x 0 ∼ ρ 0 x_{0}\sim\rho_{0} , and by ν k \nu_{k} the joint law of ( x ⁡ ( t k , x 0 ) , x ⁡ ( t k + 1 , x 0 ) ) (x(t_{k},x_{0}),x(t_{k+1},x_{0})) . Then, ν k = ( Id , ϕ Δ ​ t ) # ​ ρ ​ ( t k ) \nu_{k}=(\operatorname{Id},\phi_{\Delta t})_{\#}\rho(t_{k}) , where ϕ Δ ​ t \phi_{\Delta t} denotes the flow map of ( 1 ) over a step Δ ​ t {\Delta t} . Hence, the conditional law of x ⁡ ( t k + 1 , x 0 ) x(t_{k+1},x_{0}) given x ⁡ ( t k , x 0 ) x(t_{k},x_{0}) is the Dirac measure at ϕ Δ ​ t ​ ( x ⁡ ( t k , x 0 ) ) \phi_{\Delta t}(x(t_{k},x_{0})) , and the coupling is deterministic . 2 2 2 Under the simplifying assumption of noise-free observations. In FM, by contrast, the coupling is typically independent or chosen for computational convenience, and the associated velocity field transporting ρ ⁡ ( t k ) \rho(t_{k}) to ρ ⁡ ( t k + 1 ) \rho(t_{k+1}) does not necessarily coincide with the physical dynamics.
In the present setting, the assumption of observing trajectories already yields a coupling between adjacent time steps, so that in VM each interpolant segment is anchored to an observed trajectory, and a single velocity field f ^ \hat{f} is shared across all intervals, effectively approximating the underlying f f . The choice of coupling, and of the resulting probability path, becomes instead essential when only the marginals { ρ ⁡ ( t k ) } k = 0 N t \{\rho(t_{k})\}_{k=0}^{{N_{t}}} are observed, as in stochastic or population dynamics settings [ Neklyudov et al., 2023 , Lavenant et al., 2024 , Berman et al., 2024 , Blickhan et al., 2025 ] , where trajectories need to be inferred. 4.2 Objective decomposition and implicit integration Here we address the connection between VM and implicit Runge-Kutta (IRK) schemes, and discuss its implications. We first isolate the two contributions governing the VM objective, through a bias-variance decomposition of the loss ( 28 ),
induced by its stochastic formulation with respect to the random offset τ \tau .
To establish the subsequent results, we impose further regularity requirements on both the target and learnable velocity fields, requiring Lipschitz continuity in time, in addition to the Lipschitz continuity in space already assumed in Section 3 . Assumption 3 . Denoting by K K the closed δ \delta -neighborhood of the convex hull of the reachable set 𝒳 \mathcal{X} defined in ( 2 ), both target and learnable velocity fields, f f and f ^ \hat{f} , belong to the function class ℱ = { g ∈ W loc 1 , ∞ ​ ( ℝ n + 1 , ℝ n ) : ‖ g ‖ W 1 , ∞ ​ ( [ 0 , T ] × K , ℝ n ) ≤ M } , \mathcal{F}=\left\{g\in W^{1,\infty}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1};\mathbb{R}^{n}):\|g\|_{W^{1,\infty}([0,T]\times K;\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq M\right\}, (29) for some δ > 0 \delta>0 and M > 0 M>0 . This assumption ensures that the stability constants introduced in Section 3 , as well as interpolation error bounds, can be controlled uniformly.
The following proposition establishes the bias-variance decomposition of the VM loss, which is proved in Appendix C.2 . Proposition 4.2 (Bias-variance decomposition) . Under Assumption 3 , denoting by f ^ k ​ ( τ , x 0 ) := f ^ ​ ( t k + τ , x ⁡ ( t k , x 0 ) + τ ​ v k ​ ( x 0 ) ) \hat{f}_{k}(\tau;x_{0}):=\hat{f}(t_{k}+\tau,x(t_{k},x_{0})+\tau v_{k}(x_{0})) , the per-sample VM loss ( 28 ) admits the decomposition ℓ f , N t VM ​ ( f ^ , x 0 ) = 1 N t ​ ∑ k = 0 N t − 1 ( | v k ​ ( x 0 ) − 𝔼 τ ​ [ f ^ k ​ ( τ , x 0 ) ] | 2 + Var τ ⁡ [ f ^ k ​ ( τ , x 0 ) ] ) , τ ∼ 𝒰 ⁡ [ 0 , Δ ​ t ] , \ell_{f,{N_{t}}}^{\rm{VM}}(\hat{f};x_{0})=\frac{1}{{N_{t}}}\sum_{k=0}^{{N_{t}}-1}\left(\big|v_{k}(x_{0})-\mathbb{E}_{\tau}[\hat{f}_{k}(\tau;x_{0})]\big|^{2}+\operatorname{Var}_{\tau}[\hat{f}_{k}(\tau;x_{0})]\right),\quad\tau\sim\mathcal{U}[0,{\Delta t}], (30) for every x 0 ∈ 𝒳 0 x_{0}\in\mathcal{X}_{0} . The decomposition ( 30 ) holds with equality, with the two terms carrying distinct roles. The variance term accounts for the deviation of f ^ \hat{f} along the interpolant, and is the contribution of the stochastic offset τ \tau to the loss, unavailable through any deterministic collocation rule; its regularizing effect on the Jacobian of the learned velocity field is discussed in Section 4.4 . The bias term reads as a continuous collocation condition averaged over the subinterval [ t k , t k + 1 ] [t_{k},t_{k+1}] , namely | v k ​ ( x 0 ) − 𝔼 τ ​ [ f ^ k ​ ( τ , x 0 ) ] | = | 1 Δ ​ t ​ ∫ t k t k + 1 ( f ⁡ ( t , x ⁡ ( t , x 0 ) ) − f ^ ​ ( t , x ~ ​ ( t , x 0 ) ) ) ​ 𝑑 t | , \big|v_{k}(x_{0})-\mathbb{E}_{\tau}[\hat{f}_{k}(\tau;x_{0})]\big|=\left|\frac{1}{{\Delta t}}\int_{t_{k}}^{t_{k+1}}\left(f(t,x(t,x_{0}))-\hat{f}(t,\tilde{x}(t,x_{0}))\right)dt\right|, (31) having used the identity v k ​ ( x 0 ) = Δ ​ t − 1 ​ ∫ t k t k + 1 ∂ t x ⁡ ( t , x 0 ) ​ 𝑑 t v_{k}(x_{0})={\Delta t}^{-1}\int_{t_{k}}^{t_{k+1}}\partial_{t}x(t,x_{0})dt , by the fundamental theorem of calculus.
Enforcing the ODE residual at a single node t k + γ ​ Δ ​ t t_{k}+\gamma{\Delta t} , with γ ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] \gamma\in[0,1] , on a polynomial of degree 1, is equivalent to a one-stage method with tableau a 11 = c 1 = γ a_{11}=c_{1}=\gamma and b 1 = 1 b_{1}=1 , implicit for γ > 0 \gamma>0 , by the equivalence between collocation methods and IRK schemes [ Hairer et al., 2008 , Theorem 7.7] . Given such a correspondence, the VM bias term can be interpreted as a TF update, where the single-step update is performed via an IRK method, with the collocation point γ \gamma sampled uniformly within the subinterval.
Proposition 4.3 establishes such an equivalence, where the VM bias term is identified with the residual of a continuous-stage Runge-Kutta (csRK) method, which generalizes IRK methods to a continuum of stages. The resulting csRK scheme associated with VM bias recovers the average vector field method [ Quispel and McLaren, 2008 , Hairer, 2010 ] , of order 2, A-stable and, in the case of Hamiltonian systems, energy preserving [ Celledoni et al., 2009 ] . Proposition 4.3 (VM as implicit TF) . Under Assumption 3 , the bias term in ( 30 ) reads as the residual obtained by inserting the observed pair ( x ⁡ ( t k , x 0 ) , x ⁡ ( t k + 1 , x 0 ) ) (x(t_{k},x_{0}),x(t_{k+1},x_{0})) into the update of a csRK method with continuous-stage tableau a ( γ , σ ) = γ , b ( γ ) = 1 , c ( γ ) = γ , γ , σ ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] . a(\gamma,\sigma)=\gamma,\quad b(\gamma)=1,\quad c(\gamma)=\gamma,\qquad\gamma,\sigma\in[0,1]. (32) Moreover, if Δ ​ t ​ M < 2 {\Delta t}M<2 , the corresponding TF update, starting from x ⁡ ( t k , x 0 ) x(t_{k},x_{0}) , admits a unique solution x ^ k + 1 \hat{x}_{k+1} , and its residual ε k + 1 TF ​ ( x 0 ) = x ⁡ ( t k + 1 , x 0 ) − x ^ k + 1 \varepsilon_{k+1}^{\rm TF}(x_{0})=x(t_{k+1},x_{0})-\hat{x}_{k+1} satisfies Δ ​ t ​ | v k ​ ( x 0 ) − 𝔼 τ ​ [ f ^ k ​ ( τ , x 0 ) ] | 1 + 1 2 ​ Δ ​ t ​ M ≤ | ε k + 1 TF ​ ( x 0 ) | ≤ Δ ​ t ​ | v k ​ ( x 0 ) − 𝔼 τ ​ [ f ^ k ​ ( τ , x 0 ) ] | 1 − 1 2 ​ Δ ​ t ​ M , \frac{{\Delta t}|v_{k}(x_{0})-\mathbb{E}_{\tau}[\hat{f}_{k}(\tau;x_{0})]|}{1+\frac{1}{2}{\Delta t}M}\leq|\varepsilon_{k+1}^{\rm TF}(x_{0})|\leq\frac{{\Delta t}|v_{k}(x_{0})-\mathbb{E}_{\tau}[\hat{f}_{k}(\tau;x_{0})]|}{1-\frac{1}{2}{\Delta t}M}, (33) for k = 0 , … , N t − 1 k=0,\dots,N_{t}-1 . The proof, along with details on csRK schemes, is provided in Appendix C.2 . Denoting by F F the increment function associated with the csRK method, performing a TF update with an implicit scheme requires solving x ^ k + 1 = x ⁡ ( t k , x 0 ) + Δ ​ t ​ F ​ ( t k , x ⁡ ( t k , x 0 ) , x ^ k + 1 , Δ ​ t , f ^ ) \hat{x}_{k+1}=x(t_{k},x_{0})+{\Delta t}F(t_{k},x(t_{k},x_{0}),\hat{x}_{k+1},{\Delta t};\hat{f}) for the predicted state x ^ k + 1 \hat{x}_{k+1} , so that the residual ε k + 1 TF ​ ( x 0 ) \varepsilon_{k+1}^{\rm TF}(x_{0}) can be formed. Thus, although TF is simulation-free, training would still require differentiating through the nonlinear solver, at a computational cost that scales with the number of iterations required for convergence. The VM bias, instead, avoids the implicit solve by directly evaluating the residual at the observed pair ( x ⁡ ( t k , x 0 ) , x ⁡ ( t k + 1 , x 0 ) ) (x(t_{k},x_{0}),x(t_{k+1},x_{0})) . In this sense, the VM objective carries the implicit method inside the optimization process, rather than in the forward pass, with the nonlinear system that a rollout or TF objective would require to solve at each step being replaced by a residual that the learned velocity field has to satisfy along the interpolant.
Such a correspondence is made quantitative by the bounds ( 33 ), which relate the two residuals up to multiplicative factors, showing that minimizing the VM bias term is equivalent to minimizing the residual of a TF update coupled with an IRK method.
In terms of stability properties, when considering the Dahlquist test equation x ˙ = λ ​ x \dot{x}=\lambda x , with λ ∈ ℂ \lambda\in\mathbb{C} , as in Section 2 , the stability function associated with the average vector field method reads as R ⁡ ( z ) = 1 + z / 2 1 − z / 2 R(z)=\frac{1+z/2}{1-z/2} , with z = λ ​ Δ ​ t z=\lambda{\Delta t} , so that | R ⁡ ( z ) | ≤ 1 |R(z)|\leq 1 for Re ⁡ ( z ) ≤ 0 \operatorname{Re}(z)\leq 0 , yielding an A-stable method, namely a method whose stability region covers the entire left half of the complex plane. In particular, R R coincides with the stability function of the implicit midpoint method, which is a second-order A-stable method. This property, inherited by the VM bias term, agrees with the findings of Lu and Tsai [2026] , where the implicit midpoint rule is shown to be a principled choice for learning conservative or dissipative dynamics, preserving structural properties of the underlying system. 4.3 Discretization bias, order of convergence and consistency The VM objective is an empirical surrogate of the theoretical velocity objective ( 10 ), and is therefore biased. We identify two sources of bias, both stemming from the discrete nature of observations over time.
First, the velocity target is estimated by a first-order finite difference v k ​ ( x 0 ) v_{k}(x_{0}) , being the derivative of the piecewise linear interpolant. Second, the L 2 L^{2} -norm in time is approximated by uniformly sampling the offset τ \tau , and by evaluating the learned velocity field across the continuum of the interpolant, framing VM as a stochastic quadrature rule.
The first source of bias is common to other empirical velocity-based objectives, as, for instance, in TF coupled with a first-order explicit Euler update ( 7 ). In this view, the distinguishing feature of VM lies in its stochastic collocation strategy, which, as shown below, yields an improved order of convergence in Δ ​ t {\Delta t} in the approximation of the theoretical velocity loss ℓ f \ell_{f} , as compared to what TF attains. Theorem 4.4 (Order of convergence, VM loss) . Under Assumption 3 , for every x 0 ∈ 𝒳 0 x_{0}\in\mathcal{X}_{0} , it holds that | ℓ f , N t VM ​ ( f ^ , x 0 ) − ℓ f ​ ( f ^ , x 0 ) | ≤ C 1 VM ​ Δ ​ t 2 + C 2 VM ​ Δ ​ t 4 , |\ell_{f,{N_{t}}}^{\rm{VM}}(\hat{f};x_{0})-\ell_{f}(\hat{f};x_{0})|\leq C^{\rm VM}_{1}{\Delta t}^{2}+C^{\rm VM}_{2}{\Delta t}^{4}, (34) with C 1 VM = 4 ​ M 3 ​ ( 1 + M ) + 1 3 ​ M 2 ​ ( 1 + M ) 2 C^{\rm VM}_{1}=4M^{3}(1+M)+\frac{1}{3}M^{2}(1+M)^{2} , and C 2 VM = M 4 ​ ( 1 + M ) 2 C^{\rm VM}_{2}=M^{4}(1+M)^{2} . The proof is provided in Appendix C.3 , alongside a similar result for TF, which is shown to be of order O ⁡ ( Δ ​ t ) O({\Delta t}) rather than O ⁡ ( Δ ​ t 2 ) O({\Delta t}^{2}) , thus highlighting the improved order of convergence of VM. The proof relies on the decomposition ( 30 ), whose terms are compared with the corresponding terms in the decomposition of the theoretical velocity loss ℓ f \ell_{f} , and separately bounded.
The bias terms differ through the velocity target, and through the argument at which the learned velocity field is evaluated, namely the interpolant in the case of ℓ f , N t VM \ell_{f,{N_{t}}}^{\rm{VM}} . Chord estimates, provided in Lemma C.1 , bound this difference by O ⁡ ( Δ ​ t 2 ) O({\Delta t}^{2}) . The variance terms are separately O ⁡ ( Δ ​ t 2 ) O({\Delta t}^{2}) , both integrands being Lipschitz in τ \tau , with constant controlled by M M .
The estimate is a statement about the objective at fixed f ^ \hat{f} , and is uniform over ℱ \mathcal{F} , and it therefore transfers to the minimizers of the two objectives. In particular, combined with the previously established stability estimates, the following corollary closes the chain of estimates begun in Section 3 , bounding the trajectory loss ℓ x \ell_{x} in terms of the VM loss ℓ f , N t VM \ell_{f,{N_{t}}}^{\rm{VM}} , and accounting for the discretization bias in Δ ​ t {\Delta t} . Corollary 4.5 (Loss control) . Under Assumption 3 , if C ∞ , 2 ​ ( M ) ​ ‖ f ⁡ ( ⋅ , x ⁡ ( ⋅ , x 0 ) ) − f ^ ​ ( ⋅ , x ⁡ ( ⋅ , x 0 ) ) ‖ L 2 ​ ( [ 0 , T ] , ℝ n ) < δ , C_{\infty,2}(M)\|f(\cdot,x(\cdot,x_{0}))-\hat{f}(\cdot,x(\cdot,x_{0}))\|_{L^{2}([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{n})}<\delta, (35) for every x 0 ∈ 𝒳 0 x_{0}\in\mathcal{X}_{0} , then it holds that ℓ x ​ ( f ^ , x 0 ) ≤ C 2 , 2 2 ​ ( M ) ​ ( ℓ f , N t VM ​ ( f ^ , x 0 ) + C 1 VM ​ Δ ​ t 2 + C 2 VM ​ Δ ​ t 4 ) . \ell_{x}(\hat{f};x_{0})\leq C_{2,2}^{2}(M)\left(\ell_{f,{N_{t}}}^{\rm{VM}}(\hat{f};x_{0})+C^{\rm VM}_{1}{\Delta t}^{2}+C^{\rm VM}_{2}{\Delta t}^{4}\right). (36) The proof is provided in Appendix C.3 .
The right hand side of ( 36 ) is computable, and requires no simulation of the learned dynamics, as it only depends on the training loss ℓ f , N t VM \ell_{f,{N_{t}}}^{\rm{VM}} plus an explicit discretization bias, with constants depending on the hypothesis class ℱ \mathcal{F} alone. Neither term requires solving ( 3 ), so that the bound is simulation-free , overcoming the need for backpropagation through an ODE solver, as instead required by the trajectory loss ℓ x \ell_{x} on the left hand side. The smallness condition ( 35 ) is a technical requirement, as it yields confinement of the learned flow within K K , under the δ \delta -neighborhood of Assumption 3 , without which the stability estimates of Lemma 3.3 would not hold. A similar argument has been employed in the proof of Theorem 3.8 . As a consequence of the previous result, we establish the consistency of the minimizer f ^ ∗ \hat{f}_{\ast} of the VM objective ℒ f , N , N t VM \mathcal{L}_{f,N,{N_{t}}}^{\rm{VM}} with the true velocity field f f , in the L 2 L^{2} sense, as Δ ​ t → 0 {\Delta t}\to 0 . Corollary 4.6 (Consistency of VM minimizer) . Under Assumption 3 , denote by μ N \mu_{N} the empirical occupation measure of the observed trajectories { x ⁡ ( t , x 0 ( i ) ) } i = 1 N \{x(t,x_{0}^{(i)})\}_{i=1}^{N} , with x 0 ( i ) ∼ ρ 0 x_{0}^{(i)}\sim\rho_{0} , and let f ^ ∗ ∈ arg ⁡ min f ^ ∈ ℱ ​ ℒ f , N , N t VM ​ [ f ^ ] \hat{f}_{\ast}\in\arg\min_{\hat{f}\in\mathcal{F}}\mathcal{L}_{f,N,{N_{t}}}^{\rm{VM}}[\hat{f}] . Then ‖ f ^ ∗ − f ‖ L 2 ​ ( μ N , ℝ n ) 2 ≤ 2 ​ ( C 1 VM ​ Δ ​ t 2 + C 2 VM ​ Δ ​ t 4 ) . \|\hat{f}_{\ast}-f\|_{L^{2}(\mu_{N};\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{2}\leq 2(C^{\rm VM}_{1}{\Delta t}^{2}+C^{\rm VM}_{2}{\Delta t}^{4}). (37) The proof is provided in Appendix C.3 . The result is stated in terms of the empirical occupation measure μ N \mu_{N} , for a finite set of observed trajectories, associated to sample of initial conditions of size N N from the prescribed distribution ρ 0 \rho_{0} , relying on the notation of Section 3.1 . 4.4 Implicit regularization and Jacobian matching The analysis carried out in the previous sections relies on a standing bound on the Lipschitz constant of the learned velocity field f ^ \hat{f} , which, for a continuously differentiable field on a convex domain, is equivalent to a bound on the norm of its Jacobian ∂ x f ^ \partial_{x}\hat{f} . Such a bound enters the stability estimates of Lemmas 3.3 and 3.4 , through the Gronwall constants depending on Lip ⁡ [ f ^ ] \operatorname{Lip}[\hat{f}] , and is required by the approximation results of Section 4.3 . Likewise, Assumption 3 , on which the subsequent analysis of the VM objective relies, enforces a uniform bound of a similar nature.
In practice, such a bound may be enforced through a penalty on the norm of ∂ x f ^ \partial_{x}\hat{f} . However, the proof of Theorem 3.8 , carried out in W 1 , ∞ W^{1,\infty} -norm, makes clear that controlling the norm of the learned Jacobian alone is not sufficient, as what should be controlled is its distance from the true Jacobian ∂ x f \partial_{x}f . Indeed, arbitrary penalties on the Jacobian norm of the learned velocity field may steer the learning towards trivial solutions with arbitrarily small Jacobian norm, yielding sub-optimal approximation of the true velocity field f f ; whereas, directly enforcing Jacobian matching , which has been shown to yield statistically accurate learning of chaotic dynamics [ Park et al., 2024 ] , is infeasible in the present setting, as ∂ x f \partial_{x}f is not accessible, and its computation would require O ⁡ ( n 2 ) O(n^{2}) complexity. In this section, we show that, relying on the bias-variance decomposition ( 30 ), VM implicitly provides a form of Jacobian control, and that the role of the perturbation τ \tau along the interpolant decouples. Specifically, the bias term gives rise to a penalty on the mismatch between the Jacobians’ action on the true velocity, yielding a property which we refer to as implicit Jacobian matching . The variance term penalizes the action of the learned Jacobian on the true velocity, hence acting as a regularizer.
As the following argument relies on a Taylor expansion of the bias and variance terms, we require further regularity, which is enforced by the following assumption. Assumption 4 . Let K K be as in Assumption 3 and M > 0 M>0 . Both target and learnable velocity fields, f f and f ^ \hat{f} , are autonomous and belong to the function class ℱ = { g ∈ W loc 2 , ∞ ​ ( ℝ n , ℝ n ) : ‖ g ‖ W 2 , ∞ ​ ( K , ℝ n ) ≤ M } . \mathcal{F}=\left\{g\in W^{2,\infty}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n}):\|g\|_{W^{2,\infty}(K;\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq M\right\}. (38) The following result formalizes the above observations. Proposition 4.7 (Implicit regularization) . Under Assumption 4 , for every x 0 ∈ 𝒳 0 x_{0}\in\mathcal{X}_{0} and k = 0 , … , N t − 1 k=0,\ldots,{N_{t}}-1 , the bias term in ( 30 ) of the per-sample VM loss ℓ f , N t VM \ell_{f,{N_{t}}}^{\rm{VM}} satisfies the expansion | v k ​ ( x 0 ) − 𝔼 τ ​ [ f ^ k ​ ( τ , x 0 ) ] | 2 = Δ ​ t 2 4 ​ | ( ∂ x f ⁡ ( x ⁡ ( t k , x 0 ) ) − ∂ x f ^ ​ ( x ⁡ ( t k , x 0 ) ) ) ​ f ​ ( x ⁡ ( t k , x 0 ) ) | 2 + O ⁡ ( Δ ​ t 3 ) , \big|v_{k}(x_{0})-\mathbb{E}_{\tau}[\hat{f}_{k}(\tau;x_{0})]\big|^{2}=\frac{{\Delta t}^{2}}{4}\left|\left(\partial_{x}f(x(t_{k},x_{0}))-\partial_{x}\hat{f}(x(t_{k},x_{0}))\right)f(x(t_{k},x_{0}))\right|^{2}+O({\Delta t}^{3}), (39) provided | f ⁡ ( x ⁡ ( t k , x 0 ) ) − f ^ ​ ( x ⁡ ( t k , x 0 ) ) | ≤ C ​ Δ ​ t 2 |f(x(t_{k},x_{0}))-\hat{f}(x(t_{k},x_{0}))|\leq C{\Delta t}^{2} , for some C > 0 C>0 independent of Δ ​ t {\Delta t} .
Analogously, the variance term in ( 30 ) satisfies the expansion Var τ ⁡ [ f ^ k ​ ( τ , x 0 ) ] = Δ ​ t 2 12 ​ | ∂ x f ^ ​ ( x ⁡ ( t k , x 0 ) ) ​ f ​ ( x ⁡ ( t k , x 0 ) ) | 2 + O ⁡ ( Δ ​ t 3 ) . \operatorname{Var}_{\tau}[\hat{f}_{k}(\tau;x_{0})]=\frac{{\Delta t}^{2}}{12}\left|\partial_{x}\hat{f}(x(t_{k},x_{0}))f(x(t_{k},x_{0}))\right|^{2}+O({\Delta t}^{3}). (40) The proof is provided in Appendix C.4 . The first claim holds in the regime where the learned velocity field is O ⁡ ( Δ ​ t 2 ) O({\Delta t}^{2}) -close to the true one at the observed points. There, the remaining terms in the bias expansion are of higher order, and the leading order term reduces to ( 39 ), namely a directional Jacobian matching condition. As a consequence, in this regime, the VM objective implicitly enforces a penalty on the mismatch between the Jacobians’ action on the true velocity, which, while weaker than full Jacobian matching, emerges naturally from the stochastic collocation strategy employed by VM, as shown empirically in Section 5 .
The second claim holds without the closeness condition, and shows that the variance term induces a penalty on the action of the Jacobian of the learned velocity field on the true velocity, weighted by the variance Var τ ⁡ [ τ ] = Δ ​ t 2 / 12 \operatorname{Var}_{\tau}[\tau]={\Delta t}^{2}/12 of the perturbation τ ∼ 𝒰 ⁡ [ 0 , Δ ​ t ] \tau\sim\mathcal{U}[0,{\Delta t}] . By contrast with an arbitrary penalty on the Jacobian norm, it constrains only the variation of the learned velocity field along the flow, leaving the directions transverse to the flow unconstrained. This is a direct contribution of the random perturbation along the interpolant, as a deterministic collocation rule with a fixed offset τ \tau would yield a vanishing variance term, so that an equivalent control would have to be enforced explicitly. To understand the effect of such a penalty on the learned flow, we may proceed formally. If the learned velocity field is sufficiently close to the true one, replacing the true trajectory and velocity with their learned counterparts in ( 40 ) yields ∂ x f ^ ​ ( x ⁡ ( t k , x 0 ) ) ​ f ​ ( x ⁡ ( t k , x 0 ) ) ≈ ∂ x f ^ ​ ( x ^ ​ ( t k , x 0 ) ) ​ f ^ ​ ( x ^ ​ ( t k , x 0 ) ) = ∂ t 2 x ^ ​ ( t k , x 0 ) , \partial_{x}\hat{f}(x(t_{k},x_{0}))f(x(t_{k},x_{0}))\approx\partial_{x}\hat{f}(\hat{x}(t_{k},x_{0}))\hat{f}(\hat{x}(t_{k},x_{0}))=\partial_{t}^{2}\hat{x}(t_{k},x_{0}), so that the variance term acts as a penalty on the acceleration of the learned system. In this sense, its role is akin to that of cubic spline interpolation, where the natural interpolant minimizes the acceleration among sufficiently smooth interpolants.
This analogy is only qualitative, since VM does not enforce interpolation of the learned trajectory at the observed points, its main aim being to reframe the learning problem as velocity regression rather than trajectory fitting. Nevertheless, it provides an intuitive interpretation of the regularization effect induced by the stochastic collocation strategy on the learned flow.
Finally, both penalties are of order Δ ​ t 2 {\Delta t}^{2} , so that the implicit regularization does not degrade the order of convergence of the VM loss to the theoretical velocity loss established in Theorem 4.4 . 5 Numerical results Having characterized the velocity matching (VM) objective analytically, we now assess its behavior empirically. In
Section 3 , we showed that the trajectory error is controlled by a simulation-free quadratic objective over velocities; in Section 4 , we showed that VM approximates such an objective to second order in the temporal step size of the observations, while implicitly matching the action of the Jacobians of the learned and true velocity fields along the flow.
In this section, we investigate these properties numerically, by comparing VM with trajectory-based strategies reviewed in Section 2.2 , on a range of nonlinear ordinary differential equations (ODEs) and spatially semi-discretized time-dependent partial differential equations (PDEs) benchmarks. In particular, Section 5.1 concerns a comparison in terms of accuracy attained by the different training strategies as a function of their training cost.
Section 5.2 assesses the continuous-time approximation properties of the learned models, namely whether they can be simulated with a time step different from the one used during training, or with arbitrary Runge–Kutta schemes, without degrading the approximation error.
Finally, Section 5.3 investigates properties specific to Hamiltonian and chaotic systems, namely the preservation of invariants and the reproduction of chaotic attractors. Benchmark systems. As ODEs, we consider the Euler equations of a free rigid body, namely a Lie–Poisson system whose trajectories lie on the unit sphere, and which conserves both its kinetic energy and a Casimir invariant; the Stuart–Landau oscillator, which is a normal form of a supercritical Hopf bifurcation whose trajectories are attracted by a stable limit cycle; an active particle in the Müller–Brown potential characterized by sustained oscillations within the potential wells; and the Lorenz system in its chaotic regime. As PDEs, we consider one-dimensional viscous Burgers, Fisher–KPP and Korteweg–de Vries (KdV) equations, and the Kuramoto–Sivashinsky (KS) equation in its chaotic regime. Their spatial semi-discretization yields a high-dimensional ODE system, so that the learning problem falls within the setting of Section 2 , with the learned velocity field acting on the discretized state. Further details are provided in Appendix D . Experimental setup. A common setup is adopted for all numerical experiments, in which models trained by minimizing the VM loss ( 28 ), according to Algorithm 1 , are compared with models trained on the multiple-shooting rollout loss ( 6 ) in five configurations, spanning the single-step and multi-step settings of Section 2.2 : teacher forcing (TF), namely L = 1 L=1 ; fixed shooting lengths L = 5 L=5 and L = 10 L=10 ; a randomized shooting length drawn uniformly in { 1 , … , 20 } \{1,\dots,20\} at each iteration; and a curriculum strategy, in which the shooting length grows linearly from L = 1 L=1 to L = 50 L=50 along training. Simulation of the learned dynamics in rollout training relies on explicit Euler updates, the typical scheme adopted for neural surrogates. For a given benchmark system, all training strategies share the same data, architecture, and optimization hyperparameters, so that the resulting models differ only in the objective being minimized.
The accuracy of the learned dynamics is measured by the relative L 2 L^{2} error between reference and learned trajectories over the time interval [ 0 , T ] [0,T] , with T T denoting either the validation or test horizon, ℰ traj \displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{traj}} : = 𝔼 x 0 ∼ ρ 0 ​ ‖ x ⁡ ( ⋅ , x 0 ) − x ^ ​ ( ⋅ , x 0 ) ‖ L 2 ​ ( [ 0 , T ] , ℝ n ) ‖ x ⁡ ( ⋅ , x 0 ) ‖ L 2 ​ ( [ 0 , T ] , ℝ n ) , \displaystyle:=\mathbb{E}_{x_{0}\sim\rho_{0}}\frac{\|x(\cdot,x_{0})-\hat{x}(\cdot,x_{0})\|_{L^{2}([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{n})}}{\|x(\cdot,x_{0})\|_{L^{2}([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{n})}}, (Trajectory error) (41) where x ⁡ ( t , x 0 ) x(t,x_{0}) and x ^ ​ ( t , x 0 ) \hat{x}(t,x_{0}) denote the solutions of the target and learned systems, respectively.
In particular, training and validation share the same temporal horizon, T valid = T train T_{\mathrm{valid}}=T_{\mathrm{train}} , whereas testing is performed over a horizon twice as long, T test = 2 ​ T train T_{\mathrm{test}}=2T_{\mathrm{train}} , so that the test set assesses both generalization to unseen initial conditions and time extrapolation.
For the ODE benchmarks, the true velocity field f f and its Jacobian ∂ x f \partial_{x}f are available, so that the accuracy of the learned field itself can also be measured, through the following relative errors ℰ vel \displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{vel}} : = 𝔼 x 0 ∼ ρ 0 ​ ‖ f ⁡ ( x ⁡ ( ⋅ , x 0 ) ) − f ^ ​ ( x ⁡ ( ⋅ , x 0 ) ) ‖ L 2 ​ ( [ 0 , T ] , ℝ n ) ‖ f ⁡ ( x ⁡ ( ⋅ , x 0 ) ) ‖ L 2 ​ ( [ 0 , T ] , ℝ n ) , \displaystyle:=\mathbb{E}_{x_{0}\sim\rho_{0}}\frac{\|f(x(\cdot,x_{0}))-\hat{f}(x(\cdot,x_{0}))\|_{L^{2}([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{n})}}{\|f(x(\cdot,x_{0}))\|_{L^{2}([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{n})}}, (Velocity error) (42) ℰ Jac \displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{Jac}} : = 𝔼 x 0 ∼ ρ 0 ​ ‖ ∂ x f ⁡ ( x ⁡ ( ⋅ , x 0 ) ) − ∂ x f ^ ​ ( x ⁡ ( ⋅ , x 0 ) ) ‖ L 2 ​ ( [ 0 , T ] , ℝ n × n ) ‖ ∂ x f ⁡ ( x ⁡ ( ⋅ , x 0 ) ) ‖ L 2 ​ ( [ 0 , T ] , ℝ n × n ) , \displaystyle:=\mathbb{E}_{x_{0}\sim\rho_{0}}\frac{\|\partial_{x}f(x(\cdot,x_{0}))-\partial_{x}\hat{f}(x(\cdot,x_{0}))\|_{L^{2}([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})}}{\|\partial_{x}f(x(\cdot,x_{0}))\|_{L^{2}([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})}}, (Jacobian error) (43) with ℝ n × n \mathbb{R}^{n\times n} equipped with the Frobenius norm. Note that both quantities are evaluated along the reference trajectories x ⁡ ( t , x 0 ) x(t,x_{0}) , rather than the learned ones, as they measure the discrepancy between the velocity fields on the support of the data, independently of the error accumulated by integrating the learned dynamics. Together, the velocity and Jacobian errors provide an L 2 L^{2} proxy, along the reference trajectories, for the W 1 , ∞ W^{1,\infty} error of f ^ \hat{f} on which our approximation results rely.
In practice, we report the empirical counterparts of ( 41 )–( 43 ), in which the expectation over initial conditions and the time integrals are replaced by sample averages.
Each training objective was run with three independent seeds, and, for each run, the model attaining the lowest validation trajectory error ( 41 ) was retained for testing.
Further details are given in Appendix D . 5.1 Accuracy, fast convergence and implicit regularization Figure 5: Validation and test errors - ODEs . ( Left ) Validation trajectory, velocity and Jacobian errors as functions of wall-clock training time, for Euler, Stuart–Landau and active Müller–Brown systems. Solid lines denote the mean, and shaded areas the standard deviation, over three independent runs. ( Right ) Test trajectory error against the total training time, for each independent run. Trajectory-based strategies saturate at a common error level, whereas VM attains errors up to one order of magnitude lower, at a training cost comparable with that of TF. We first compare the proposed VM objective against TF and rollout training strategies in terms of accuracy and of the computational cost required to attain it, under a common iteration budget, on both ODE and PDE benchmarks. For the ODE benchmarks, we then assess how the learned velocity fields and their Jacobians compare with the true ones. Accuracy and training efficiency. Figure 5 reports, for the Euler, Stuart–Landau and active Müller–Brown systems, the evolution of the three validation errors ( 41 - 43 ) against elapsed training time, alongside the test trajectory error attained by each seed, plotted against the total training time of the corresponding run. The former shows how fast each objective reduces the validation errors during training, whereas the latter relates the final test accuracy to the overall computational cost under the common iteration budget.
The corresponding validation and test errors are collected in Table 3 in Appendix D . Two observations hold across all three test cases. First, VM and TF have a comparable cost per iteration, since both evaluate f ^ \hat{f} once per sample, as discussed in Section 4.1 , while, by contrast, the cost of the rollout-based strategies grows with the shooting length L L , as their gradients are obtained by backpropagating through L L explicit Euler steps. Second, all trajectory-based strategies saturate at a common error level, in all three metrics and irrespective of L L , whereas the validation errors of VM keep decreasing well below such a level, within a training time comparable with that of TF.
On the test set, VM reduces the trajectory error by up to an order of magnitude relative to rollout strategies, consistently across test cases and seeds, whereas the latter yield test errors of 10 − 1 10^{-1} or larger. A comparable reduction is observed in the velocity and Jacobian errors.
These observations are consistent with the analysis carried out in the previous sections. Although VM never simulates the learned dynamics during training, it attains the lowest trajectory error, as expected from Corollary 4.5 , by which the trajectory loss is controlled by the VM loss up to a discretization bias of order Δ ​ t 2 {\Delta t}^{2} . By contrast, the TF objective approximates the theoretical velocity objective only to first order in Δ ​ t {\Delta t} (Theorem C.2 ), with its minimizers inheriting the corresponding bias. Longer shooting horizons do not remove this bias, since the same explicit Euler update is adopted, but rather reshape the loss landscape, as discussed in Section 2.3 . Figure 6: Validation and test errors - PDEs . Validation trajectory error against elapsed training time ( left ), and test trajectory error against total training time ( right ), for the Burgers, Fisher–KPP, and KdV equations, respectively. Solid lines and shaded areas denote the mean and standard deviation over three seeds; each marker in the right panels corresponds to a single seed. Analogously, Figure 6 reports, for the Burgers, Fisher–KPP, and KdV equations, the evolution of the validation trajectory error against elapsed training time, alongside the test trajectory error attained by each seed against the total training time of the corresponding run. In this case, the spatial discretization of the PDE solution u ⁡ ( x , t ) u(x,t) 3 3 3 In the PDE setting, we denote by u u the solution and by x x the spatial coordinate, the latter not to be confused with the state of ( 1 ), which here corresponds to the discretized solution u h u_{h} , with h h the spatial discretization step. yields a 1024 1024 -dimensional time-dependent state u h ​ ( t ) u_{h}(t) , whose evolution is governed by a system of the form ( 1 ), in which the vector field f f corresponds to the spatially discretized differential operator on the right-hand side of the PDE, approximated by the learned one f ^ \hat{f} .
Validation and test trajectory errors for each equation and training strategy, averaged across seeds, are collected in Table 4 of Appendix D , while Figures 11 – 13 report the pointwise absolute errors on four test trajectories per equation.
Unlike in the ODE test cases, the dependence of the validation and test errors on the shooting length L L is strongly problem-dependent. For the Fisher–KPP equation, both errors improve slightly as L L increases, whereas for the Burgers and KdV equations rollout training exhibits instabilities that become more pronounced for larger L L .
By contrast, VM – using the same architecture and optimization hyperparameters – yields stable optimization procedure on all three test cases with the validation trajectory error steadily decreasing throughout training without the instabilities observed for rollout objectives. The retained models attain the lowest test errors across equations and seeds. This is consistent with the analysis of Section 2.3 , which shows that the smoothness of trajectory-based objectives deteriorates as the rollout length L L increases, with gradients that vanish or explode exponentially in L L , thus hindering the convergence of gradient-based optimization. Velocity approximation. The velocity error ( 42 ) measures the accuracy of the learned field along the reference trajectories in an averaged L 2 L^{2} sense. Figure 7 complements it by showing the pointwise error over the state space of the Stuart–Landau and active Müller–Brown systems. The error of VM is lowest over the region spanned by the training trajectories, namely around the limit cycle and along the transients spiraling towards it for the former, and within the potential wells for the latter, and grows away from this region.
This agrees with the variational characterization of Proposition 3.2 , by which the minimizer of the velocity objective is determined only on the support of the occupation measure ( 12 ), and is left unconstrained elsewhere. Corollary 4.6 transfers this characterization to the empirical VM objective, whose minimizer is O ⁡ ( Δ ​ t 2 ) O({\Delta t}^{2}) -consistent with f f on the same support. Although TF also admits a velocity regression reading, its validation velocity error plateaus at the same level as that of rollout training, as shown in Figure 5 , suggesting that its O ⁡ ( Δ ​ t ) O({\Delta t}) bias, compounded by the spectral constraints induced by the explicit Euler step, limits the attainable accuracy. The gap between the pointwise errors of VM and rollout training in Figure 7 reflects a structural difference between the two objectives, as rollout training does not recover the true velocity field f f , but rather the field that, when numerically simulated with the scheme and time step used during training, best reproduces the observed trajectories. This aspect will be further addressed in Section 5.2 , since continuous-time approximation hinges on the capability of recovering f f itself. Figure 7: Learned velocity fields. Stuart–Landau (top) and active Müller–Brown (bottom) systems. ( Left ) Magnitude and direction of the true velocity field, together with the training trajectories. ( Right ) Pointwise error | f ​ ( x ) − f ^ ​ ( x ) | |f(x)-\hat{f}(x)| of the velocity fields learned by VM, teacher forcing (TF), and randomized rollout with L = 20 L=20 , over the two-dimensional state space ( x 1 , x 2 ) (x_{1},x_{2}) ; arrows denote the normalized learned velocity field. For the active Müller–Brown system, the learned acceleration field is shown over the position plane ( x 1 , x 2 ) (x_{1},x_{2}) , averaged over velocity directions of magnitude | x ˙ | = v 0 |\dot{x}|=v_{0} , at which the active friction vanishes and the true acceleration reduces to the force − κ ∇ V ( x ) -\kappa\nabla V(x) ; the error is averaged over the same directions. The error of VM is lowest on the support of the training data, where it is one to two orders of magnitude below that of the other strategies. Implicit Jacobian matching. As shown in Proposition 4.7 , the bias term of the VM loss aligns the action of the learned Jacobian with that of the true Jacobian along the flow, without having access to the latter, while its variance term penalizes the variation of the learned field along the flow. Neither mechanism is available to TF, which evaluates f ^ \hat{f} only at the observed states, or to rollout training, which relies on the same explicit Euler update. The Jacobian errors ( 43 ) in Figure 5 are consistent with this picture. Indeed, although no information about the Jacobians is provided, the Jacobian error of VM decreases steadily throughout training, and, as reported in Table 3 , its test value is lower than that of the trajectory-based strategies by a factor of 3–9 across the three test cases.
We stress that Jacobian accuracy matters beyond velocity accuracy, since the Jacobian of the vector field governs the local behavior of the dynamics, determining the stability of equilibria, as well as the rates at which nearby trajectories contract or separate. An accurate Jacobian thus preserves the qualitative structure of the target dynamics, and keeps the stability constants entering Lemma 3.3 close to those of the true system. 5.2 Continuous-time approximation Figure 8: Continuous-time approximation . Test trajectory error ( 41 ) as a function of the inference step size Δ ​ t {\Delta t} , when the learned dynamics are simulated with the explicit Euler (RK1), Heun (RK2) and classical fourth-order Runge–Kutta (RK4) schemes (columns), for the Euler, Stuart–Landau and active Müller–Brown systems (rows). The dashed vertical line marks the training step size, and the green dashed curve labeled Ground Truth is the error obtained by integrating the true velocity field f f with the same scheme, while the dash-dotted line indicates the rate Δ ​ t p {\Delta t}^{p} of a scheme of order p p . The error of VM follows the error behavior of the true field down to a plateau that does not depend on the scheme, whereas trajectory-based strategies are accurate only at the training step size and with explicit Euler. Formulating a model in continuous time, as an ODE, does not by itself guarantee that, once trained on observations at a fixed step, it can be simulated with other step sizes and numerical schemes without loss of accuracy. Theorem 3.9 guarantees this property for a learned velocity field that is close to f f in W 1 , ∞ W^{1,\infty} , through the decomposition ( 24 ) of the error into the continuous-time error of the learned flow and the discretization error of the scheme. We assess the continuous-time approximation capability of the models learned by VM, TF, and the selected rollout strategies by studying the test trajectory error as a function of the step size Δ ​ t {\Delta t} used at inference. Figure 8 reports this error against Δ ​ t {\Delta t} for the Euler, Stuart–Landau, and active Müller–Brown systems, with Δ ​ t {\Delta t} ranging from 10 − 4 10^{-4} to well beyond the training step size, and with each column corresponding to an explicit Runge–Kutta scheme of order p ∈ { 1 , 2 , 4 } p\in\{1,2,4\} . In all cases, the reference trajectories are computed with step size 10 − 4 10^{-4} and interpolated onto the coarser grids. Beyond assessing whether the learned model can be simulated on temporal grids other than the training one without retraining, a critical computational aspect for neural surrogates, this experiment also serves as a proxy for the closeness of f ^ \hat{f} to f f . By ( 24 ), the error of a model close to f f should follow that of f f itself for large Δ ​ t {\Delta t} , where the discretization error dominates, and saturate at the continuous-time error of the learned flow as Δ ​ t → 0 {\Delta t}\to 0 .
We therefore restrict this experiment to the ODE benchmarks, where f f is available in closed form, so that the error attained by integrating f f with the same schemes and step sizes (dashed green lines) is used as a reference.
Note that, since Theorem 3.9 is an approximation result, as it requires f ^ \hat{f} to be close to f f on a region containing the trajectories of interest, continuous-time approximation would naturally be assessed on the training trajectories. Here, we instead evaluate the error on an additional test set, since such a property is of practical interest only if it holds for unseen initial conditions, thus yielding a more challenging setting that combines approximation and generalization errors. The error associated with VM exactly follows the decomposition ( 24 ). For large step sizes, it coincides with the error obtained by integrating the true velocity field with the same scheme, and decreases at rate Δ ​ t p {\Delta t}^{p} , since the discretization error dominates. Following such a decomposition, this formally indicates that a field f ^ \hat{f} sufficiently close to f f in W 1 , ∞ W^{1,\infty} has been learned, even though everything here is measured in L 2 L^{2} norms. As the step size decreases, it saturates at a plateau that does not depend on the scheme, but rather on the continuous-time approximation error ‖ x − x ^ ‖ L ∞ \|x-\hat{x}\|_{L^{\infty}} of the learned flow, and is in all cases smaller than or equal to the error attained at the training discretization, so that the accuracy is not affected, thus yielding a model that can handle finer discretizations. Consistently, the plateau is reached at larger step sizes as the order p p increases, since the discretization error falls below the continuous-time error sooner for higher-order schemes. The errors associated with rollout strategies behave differently. When integrated with explicit Euler (RK1), the scheme used for training, their error attains a sharp minimum at the training step size, and increases as the step size is refined, until it saturates at a plateau well above both their minimum and the VM plateau, which instead retains the training accuracy. Moreover, higher-order schemes do not improve their accuracy at any step size. Thus, the velocity field learned via rollouts is accurate only when combined with the scheme and step size used for training. So, the picture outlined in the previous section repeats, since, rather than approximating f f , the model learns an Euler update that compensates for the discretization error of the scheme, thus overfitting the training discretization.
This is the empirical counterpart of the decomposition ( 20 ), which shows that TF minimizes the residual of the numerical scheme rather than the mismatch between velocity fields, and is in agreement with the observations of Krishnapriyan et al. [2023] . By contrast, the VM objective approximates the theoretical velocity objective ℒ f \mathcal{L}_{f} to second order in Δ ​ t {\Delta t} without reference to any integrator (Theorem 4.4 ), yielding a learned velocity field that can be simulated with arbitrary schemes and step sizes at inference. 5.3 Learning Hamiltonian and chaotic dynamics Figure 9: Hamiltonian and chaotic dynamics. ( Top ) Euler equations for the free rigid body. Trajectories of the ground truth and of the dynamics learned by VM and TF on the unit sphere, from the same test initial conditions (markers), and evolution of the energy H H and of the Casimir C C along the learned trajectories, averaged over seeds, with shaded areas denoting the standard deviation. VM preserves both invariants, whereas TF and rollout strategies dissipate them, so that trajectories drift off the sphere. ( Bottom ) Lorenz system. Orbits of the ground truth and of the dynamics learned by VM and TF with training step size Δ ​ t = 2 ⋅ 10 − 2 {\Delta t}=2\cdot 10^{-2} , from the same initial condition (marker), and real part of the complex-conjugate eigenvalues of the learned Jacobian at the critical point C − C_{-} against the training step size. As Δ ​ t {\Delta t} increases, TF and rollout strategies learn increasingly negative real parts, turning C − C_{-} into a stable focus onto which trajectories collapse, whereas VM retains a nonnegative real part and reproduces the attractor. Dashed lines denote the true values. Finally, we consider systems characterized by relevant qualitative features, possibly emerging over long times, namely the preservation of invariants and the structure of chaotic attractors. Since none of the tested objectives enforces these features explicitly, they provide an indirect test of the accuracy of the learned velocity field and its Jacobian. Euler. The Euler equations of a free rigid body conserve the kinetic energy, namely the Hamiltonian H H , and the Casimir invariant C ⁡ ( x ) = | x | 2 C(x)=|x|^{2} , so that trajectories lie on the unit sphere. As shown in Figure 9 , the trajectories learned by VM conserve both invariants up to small fluctuations, over a test horizon twice as long as the training one. By contrast, those learned by TF and by the rollout-based strategies drift monotonically, and lose approximately 15 % 15\% of H H and 10 % 10\% of C C by the end of the horizon, which reveals a systematic dissipation in the learned dynamics. Such dissipation is consistent with Proposition 2.2 , by which training through explicit Euler updates constrains the spectrum of the learned Jacobian to lie within the stability region of the scheme, and hence towards contractive dynamics. Note that, for conservative dynamics such as the rigid body, the linearization about a stationary rotation around the major or minor axis [ Marsden and Ratiu, 1999 , Theorem 15.9.1] has purely imaginary eigenvalues [ Marsden and Ratiu, 1999 , Eq. (15.9.12)] when restricted to the tangent space at the equilibrium of the unit sphere in ℝ 3 \mathbb{R}^{3} , which therefore lie outside the stability region of explicit Euler for every step size. By contrast, the VM objective does not push the spectrum of the learned Jacobian off the imaginary axis, since its bias term is the residual of the average vector field method (Proposition 4.3 ), whose stability function satisfies | R ⁡ ( z ) | = 1 |R(z)|=1 on the imaginary axis. As a result, the learned system preserves the structural properties of the reference dynamics. Lorenz. In the chaotic regime, where pointwise trajectory errors become uninformative beyond a few Lyapunov times, the relevant question is whether the learned dynamics reproduces the attractor. We consider the case of the Lorenz system [ Lorenz, 1963 ] , with training trajectories starting from initial conditions sampled from a concentrated distribution ρ 0 ≈ δ x 0 \rho_{0}\approx\delta_{x_{0}} . We vary the training step size Δ ​ t {\Delta t} from 10 − 3 10^{-3} to 10 − 1 10^{-1} , and report the orbits of the learned systems for Δ ​ t = 2 ⋅ 10 − 2 {\Delta t}=2\cdot 10^{-2} in Figure 9 , alongside the spectrum of the Jacobian of the learned dynamics at the critical point C − C_{-} , for the considered range of training step sizes Δ ​ t {\Delta t} .
The latter explains the collapse of TF trajectories onto C − C_{-} in Figure 9 , and analogously for the rollout strategies. For the true dynamics, the two critical points C ± C_{\pm} , other than the origin, are unstable [ Sparrow, 1982 ] . Their complex-conjugate eigenvalues have a small, but positive, real part, which makes trajectories escape from their neighborhood and switch between the two regions of the attractor.
