As the training step size increases, TF and the rollout-based strategies learn complex eigenvalues with an increasingly negative real part, so that C − C_{-} becomes a stable equilibrium, inducing the collapse of trajectories onto it. This stabilization, a consequence of the spectral constraint of Proposition 2.2 , prevents the chaotic dynamics and its attractor from being learned. By contrast, VM recovers the real part of these eigenvalues accurately over most of the range of step sizes, in line with the implicit Jacobian matching mechanism of Proposition 4.7 .
We stress that, although formal, the connection between VM and Jacobian matching strategies, established through the implicit regularization result of Proposition 4.7 , becomes relevant when learning statistically accurate chaotic dynamics.
Indeed, Park et al. [2024] showed, through an ergodic-theoretic analysis based on shadowing, that small errors in the vector field alone do not guarantee the recovery of the invariant measure, whereas closeness of both the learned dynamics and its Jacobian does, proposing, consistently, to augment the regression loss with a Jacobian matching term.
This approach has recently been extended by Kang et al. [2026] , who match Jacobians at randomly perturbed states to implicitly enforce second-order consistency.
Both strategies, however, require access to the true Jacobian at the training states, and involve forming Jacobian matrices, at a cost of O ⁡ ( n ) O(n) derivative evaluations and O ⁡ ( n 2 ) O(n^{2}) memory per training state.
By contrast, VM only matches the action of the Jacobian along the flow, which stochastic collocation extracts from trajectory data alone at the cost of a single evaluation of f ^ \hat{f} per collocation point, without forming any Jacobian. Figure 10: Kuramoto–Sivashinsky. ( Left ) Evolution of the discretized solution u h ​ ( t ) u_{h}(t) for the ground truth and for the dynamics learned by VM and TF, from the same test initial condition, over the test temporal interval [ 0,800 ] [0,800] ; the dashed line marks the end of the training horizon, T = 200 T=200 . ( Right ) Joint probability density of the first two spatial derivatives ( ∂ x u , ∂ x 2 u ) (\partial_{x}u,\partial_{x}^{2}u) , estimated over the test trajectories. Kuramoto–Sivashinsky. We conclude with the KS equation on a periodic domain of length 64 64 , discretized on 256 256 grid points, whose dynamics exhibits spatiotemporal chaos. Although the comparison between VM and TF is only qualitative, this test case extends the previous discussion to a high-dimensional setting. While the fourth-order term makes the semi-discretized system stiff [ Kassam and Trefethen, 2005 ] , the associated modes may decay on time scales much shorter than the sampling step Δ ​ t {\Delta t} , so that neither objective can identify them from the data. The two objectives differ instead in their treatment of the advective term, which transports the solution without dissipating it, and whose linearization thus has eigenvalues close to the imaginary axis [ LeVeque, 2007 ] . As for the rigid body and the Lorenz system, TF compensates for the explicit Euler update by damping these modes, which acts as an additional diffusion on the learned dynamics.
The training trajectories cover the time interval [ 0,200 ] [0,200] , while the test trajectories extend to T = 800 T=800 . Figure 10 provides a qualitative comparison of the space-time evolution of the learned dynamics and of their statistics on the attractor, through the joint probability density of the first two spatial derivatives ( ∂ x u , ∂ x 2 u ) (\partial_{x}u,\partial_{x}^{2}u) [ Linot and Graham, 2020 ] . Although individual trajectories decorrelate from the reference after a few Lyapunov times, as expected for chaotic dynamics, the dynamics learned by VM generates spatiotemporal chaos qualitatively similar to the true one, both within and well beyond the training horizon, and its joint density closely matches the reference. By contrast, the dynamics learned by TF evolves towards a more regular regime, dominated by low-wavenumber patterns, and its joint density concentrates around the origin, consistently with the spectral constraints induced by the explicit Euler bias, which damps the high-wavenumber content of the solution. 6 Conclusions This work introduces Velocity Matching (VM), a simulation-free framework for learning continuous-time dynamical systems formulated as ordinary differential equations (ODEs), a task commonly formulated as the minimization of a trajectory-based objective. Beyond the computational cost associated with simulation-based training, we show that trajectory-based objectives suffer from structural degeneracies, which hinder gradient-based optimization and bias the learned dynamics, thus motivating the transition towards simulation-free velocity-based learning.
To this end, we develop a theoretical framework for velocity-based learning, in which the velocity field of the underlying ODE is characterized as the minimizer of a quadratic functional, and relate this objective to its trajectory-based counterpart through the introduction of suitable L p L^{p} - L q L^{q} stability estimates, which can be read as a Lax equivalence result in a learning setting. These estimates justify simulation-free training based on velocity supervision and enable us to establish a universal approximation result for the flow of the true dynamics by neural ODEs, whose velocity field is parameterized by deep neural networks. This result in turn allows us to characterize the continuous-time approximation property of the learned dynamics.
Relying on this theoretical framework, we characterize the properties of the VM objective. In particular, through a bias-variance decomposition, we relate the VM loss to implicit integration and show that VM provides a higher-order approximation of the velocity population risk. Combined with the stability estimates, this result yields a simulation-free bound on the trajectory loss, alongside the consistency of the VM minimizer as the step size vanishes. Moreover, the same decomposition reveals an implicit regularization of the Jacobian of the learned field along the observed trajectories, connecting VM to Jacobian matching approaches, without requiring access to the true Jacobian.
Numerical results on ODEs and semi-discretized time-dependent PDEs, featuring limit cycles as well as conservative and chaotic dynamics, support these findings, showing that VM improves accuracy over trajectory-based strategies at a lower training cost. Several questions remain open. The effect of the stochastic collocation of VM on specific classes of nonlinear dynamics, possibly with noisy observations, remains largely unexplored, and the analysis of Section 4 is only a first step in this direction. It would be valuable to clarify for which classes of systems, whether oscillatory, conservative, dissipative, or multiscale, stochastic collocation is most beneficial, as this could guide the design of collocation distributions that are adapted to specific classes of systems. Moreover, the constants appearing in the stability estimates of Section 3.2 , on which the subsequent results rely, grow exponentially with the time horizon and the Lipschitz constant, as is common for Grönwall-type estimates, and may thus become uninformative. Specializing these estimates to specific classes of dynamics, by exploiting their structure, could yield sharper bounds and more informative guarantees for learning. Finally, although we have extended the stability estimates to a Wasserstein estimate, adopting an ergodic perspective could connect the implicit Jacobian regularization of VM to statistical guarantees on the invariant measures of chaotic dynamics. Appendix A Additional results and proofs of Section 2 In this section we shall extend the discussion on the challenges of trajectory-based learning, presented in Section 2.3 , by providing detailed arguments for the issues related to the optimization of the trajectory-based learning objective, in both continuous- and discrete-time settings. A.1 Additional results of Section 2.3.1 To address the analysis of the trajectory-based learning objective a simplified setting is considered in which the target system is linear, and the learning problem is cast as a parameter identification one. This allows to derive closed-form expressions and to characterize the criticalities of the trajectory-based learning objective, alongside their dependence on the regime of the target and learned dynamics, as well as on the temporal horizon. Our main interest is in the limiting behavior of the loss landscape, and its higher-order derivatives, in the tails of the parameter space, as well as in the infinite-horizon limit, being the main sources of degeneracy in the loss landscape. Asymptotic expressions are derived, aiming at characterizing the rates at which such degeneracies occur, and the regimes in which they are more pronounced.
Hence, we proceed by deriving explicit expressions for the energy-normalized trajectory objective, its gradient and Hessian, by considering the problem of learning a 1-dimensional linear system, with dynamics given by x ˙ = λ ​ x \dot{x}=\lambda x , for some λ ∈ ℝ \lambda\in\mathbb{R} . We cast such a problem into a parameter identification one, by considering a parametric family of linear systems, given by x ^ ˙ = λ ^ ​ x ^ \dot{\hat{x}}=\hat{\lambda}\hat{x} , with λ ^ ∈ ℝ \hat{\lambda}\in\mathbb{R} .
We assume the initial condition x ⁡ ( 0 ) = x ^ ​ ( 0 ) = x 0 x(0)=\hat{x}(0)=x_{0} to be fixed, and λ , λ ^ ≠ 0 \lambda,\hat{\lambda}\neq 0 .
The trajectory-based learning objective, for a single fixed initial condition x 0 x_{0} , normalized by the average trajectory energy, is defined as ℓ ¯ x ​ ( λ ^ , x 0 ) := ‖ x ⁡ ( ⋅ , x 0 ) − x ^ ​ ( ⋅ , x 0 ) ‖ L 2 ​ ( [ 0 , T ] , ℝ ) 2 ‖ x ⁡ ( ⋅ , x 0 ) ‖ L 2 ​ ( [ 0 , T ] , ℝ ) 2 , \bar{\ell}_{x}(\hat{\lambda};x_{0}):=\frac{\|x(\cdot,x_{0})-\hat{x}(\cdot,x_{0})\|_{L^{2}([0,T];\mathbb{R})}^{2}}{\|x(\cdot,x_{0})\|_{L^{2}([0,T];\mathbb{R})}^{2}}, (44) which, considering the explicit solution of the linear systems, can be expressed as ℓ ¯ x ​ ( λ ^ ) = ∫ 0 T ( e λ ​ t − e λ ^ ​ t ) 2 ​ 𝑑 t ∫ 0 T e 2 ​ λ ​ t ​ 𝑑 t , \bar{\ell}_{x}(\hat{\lambda})=\frac{\int_{0}^{T}(e^{\lambda t}-e^{\hat{\lambda}t})^{2}dt}{\int_{0}^{T}e^{2\lambda t}dt}, thus depending only on the parameters λ , λ ^ \lambda,\hat{\lambda} and the time horizon T T . The normalization is introduced to ignore the scaling effects of the solution norm, initial condition and time horizon on the loss landscape, which would otherwise dominate in certain regimes.
To perform explicit computations, we define the auxiliary moment function M k ​ ( ξ ) = ∫ 0 T t k ​ e ξ ​ t ​ 𝑑 t M_{k}(\xi)=\int_{0}^{T}t^{k}e^{\xi t}dt , for ξ ∈ ℝ \xi\in\mathbb{R} and k ∈ ℕ k\in\mathbb{N} , allowing to express the above objective, alongside its derivatives, in closed-form.
In particular, k k -th order moments can be computed by means of integration by parts, yielding the following recursive formula M k ​ ( ξ ) = T k ​ e ξ ​ T ξ − k ξ ​ M k − 1 ​ ( ξ ) , k ∈ ℕ ≥ 1 , ξ ∈ ℝ , ξ ≠ 0 , M_{k}(\xi)=\frac{T^{k}e^{\xi T}}{\xi}-\frac{k}{\xi}M_{k-1}(\xi),\quad k\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1},\quad\xi\in\mathbb{R},\ \xi\neq 0, from which closed-form expressions for orders k = 0 , 1 , 2 , 3 k=0,1,2,3 can be derived, resulting in M 0 ​ ( ξ ) = e ξ ​ T − 1 ξ , M 1 ​ ( ξ ) = ( ξ ​ T − 1 ) ​ e ξ ​ T + 1 ξ 2 , M_{0}(\xi)=\frac{e^{\xi T}-1}{\xi},\qquad M_{1}(\xi)=\frac{(\xi T-1)e^{\xi T}+1}{\xi^{2}}, M 2 ​ ( ξ ) = ( ξ 2 ​ T 2 − 2 ​ ξ ​ T + 2 ) ​ e ξ ​ T − 2 ξ 3 , M 3 ​ ( ξ ) = ( ξ 3 ​ T 3 − 3 ​ ξ 2 ​ T 2 + 6 ​ ξ ​ T − 6 ) ​ e ξ ​ T + 6 ξ 4 . M_{2}(\xi)=\frac{(\xi^{2}T^{2}-2\xi T+2)e^{\xi T}-2}{\xi^{3}},\qquad M_{3}(\xi)=\frac{(\xi^{3}T^{3}-3\xi^{2}T^{2}+6\xi T-6)e^{\xi T}+6}{\xi^{4}}. We recall the following identities for higher-order derivatives of the moment function, verifiable by direct differentiation, ∂ ξ k M 0 ​ ( ξ ) = M k ( ξ ) , k ∈ ℕ , ∂ ξ M 0 ​ ( h ⁡ ( ξ ) ) = h ′ ​ ( ξ ) ​ M 1 ​ ( h ⁡ ( ξ ) ) , ∂ ξ 2 M 0 ​ ( h ⁡ ( ξ ) ) = h ′′ ​ ( ξ ) ​ M 1 ​ ( h ⁡ ( ξ ) ) + ( h ′ ​ ( ξ ) ) 2 ​ M 2 ​ ( h ⁡ ( ξ ) ) , ∂ ξ 3 M 0 ​ ( h ⁡ ( ξ ) ) = h ′′′ ​ ( ξ ) ​ M 1 ​ ( h ⁡ ( ξ ) ) + 3 ​ h ′ ​ ( ξ ) ​ h ′′ ​ ( ξ ) ​ M 2 ​ ( h ⁡ ( ξ ) ) + ( h ′ ​ ( ξ ) ) 3 ​ M 3 ​ ( h ⁡ ( ξ ) ) , \displaystyle\begin{split}\partial_{\xi}^{k}M_{0}(\xi)&=M_{k}(\xi),\quad k\in\mathbb{N},\\
\partial_{\xi}M_{0}(h(\xi))&=h^{\prime}(\xi)M_{1}(h(\xi)),\\

M_{k}(\xi)\to\frac{k!}{|\xi|^{k+1}},\quad\xi<0,\qquad M_{k}(\xi)\sim\frac{T^{k}e^{\xi T}}{\xi},\quad\xi>0. (47) The behavior of the moment is what drives the asymmetric behavior and regime dependence of the loss landscape studied in the following, being dominated by the exponential growth of the moment for positive rates, and instead by saturation for negative rates. Loss landscape analysis. We proceed to derive closed-form expressions for the loss, gradient and Hessian of the energy-normalized trajectory objective ( 44 ).
Relying on the explicit solution of the linear systems, with x ⁡ ( t , x 0 ) = e λ ​ t ​ x 0 x(t,x_{0})=e^{\lambda t}x_{0} and x ^ ​ ( t , x 0 ) = e λ ^ ​ t ​ x 0 \hat{x}(t,x_{0})=e^{\hat{\lambda}t}x_{0} ,
and the definition of the zero-th order moment, the loss can be expressed as ℓ ¯ x ​ ( λ ^ ) \displaystyle\bar{\ell}_{x}(\hat{\lambda}) = 1 + M 0 ​ ( 2 ​ λ ^ ) M 0 ​ ( 2 ​ λ ) − 2 ​ M 0 ​ ( λ ^ + λ ) M 0 ​ ( 2 ​ λ ) , \displaystyle=1+\frac{M_{0}(2\hat{\lambda})}{M_{0}(2\lambda)}-2\frac{M_{0}(\hat{\lambda}+\lambda)}{M_{0}(2\lambda)}, (48) = 1 + λ λ ^ ​ e 2 ​ λ ^ ​ T − 1 e 2 ​ λ ​ T − 1 − 4 ​ λ λ + λ ^ ​ e ( λ + λ ^ ) ​ T − 1 e 2 ​ λ ​ T − 1 . \displaystyle=1+\frac{\lambda}{\hat{\lambda}}\frac{e^{2\hat{\lambda}T}-1}{e^{2\lambda T}-1}-\frac{4\lambda}{\lambda+\hat{\lambda}}\frac{e^{(\lambda+\hat{\lambda})T}-1}{e^{2\lambda T}-1}. Finally, applying again the moment identities ( 45 ) with h ⁡ ( λ ^ ) = 2 ​ λ ^ h(\hat{\lambda})=2\hat{\lambda} and h ⁡ ( λ ^ ) = λ + λ ^ h(\hat{\lambda})=\lambda+\hat{\lambda} , the Hessian of ( 44 ) with respect to λ ^ \hat{\lambda} is given by ∂ λ ^ 2 ℓ ¯ x ​ ( λ ^ ) \displaystyle\partial^{2}_{\hat{\lambda}}\bar{\ell}_{x}(\hat{\lambda}) = 2 M 0 ​ ( 2 ​ λ ) ​ ( 2 ​ M 2 ​ ( 2 ​ λ ^ ) − M 2 ​ ( λ + λ ^ ) ) , \displaystyle=\frac{2}{M_{0}(2\lambda)}\left(2M_{2}(2\hat{\lambda})-M_{2}(\lambda+\hat{\lambda})\right), (50) = λ λ ^ 3 ​ ( 4 ​ λ ^ 2 ​ T 2 − 4 ​ λ ^ ​ T + 2 ) ​ e 2 ​ λ ^ ​ T − 2 e 2 ​ λ ​ T − 1 − 4 ​ λ ( λ + λ ^ ) 3 ​ ( ( λ + λ ^ ) 2 ​ T 2 − 2 ​ ( λ + λ ^ ) ​ T + 2 ) ​ e ( λ + λ ^ ) ​ T − 2 e 2 ​ λ ​ T − 1 . \displaystyle=\frac{\lambda}{\hat{\lambda}^{3}}\frac{(4\hat{\lambda}^{2}T^{2}-4\hat{\lambda}T+2)e^{2\hat{\lambda}T}-2}{e^{2\lambda T}-1}-\frac{4\lambda}{(\lambda+\hat{\lambda})^{3}}\frac{((\lambda+\hat{\lambda})^{2}T^{2}-2(\lambda+\hat{\lambda})T+2)e^{(\lambda+\hat{\lambda})T}-2}{e^{2\lambda T}-1}. Given the closed-form expressions above, we characterize the landscape of ℓ ¯ x ​ ( λ ^ ) \bar{\ell}_{x}(\hat{\lambda}) through two results reading against the requirements of gradient-based optimization, namely degeneracy of the gradient and of the Hessian. In particular, we show that the loss landscape exhibits a regime-dependent behavior, with the presence of vanishing or exploding gradients, alongside ill-conditioning, and how the issues are made worse, at fast rates, in the infinite-horizon limit T → + ∞ T\to+\infty . Regarding the loss, it is immediate to verify that, for every fixed T > 0 T>0 , the minimizer is attained at λ ^ = λ \hat{\lambda}=\lambda , with ℓ ¯ x ​ ( λ ) = 0 \bar{\ell}_{x}(\lambda)=0 . By the asymptotic behavior of the moment function ( 46 ), we have that the loss flattens out for λ ^ → − ∞ \hat{\lambda}\to-\infty , with ℓ ¯ x ​ ( λ ^ ) → 1 \bar{\ell}_{x}(\hat{\lambda})\to 1 , while
it inherits exponential growth for λ ^ → + ∞ \hat{\lambda}\to+\infty , with ℓ ¯ x ​ ( λ ^ ) ∼ M 0 ​ ( 2 ​ λ ^ ) / M 0 ​ ( 2 ​ λ ) \bar{\ell}_{x}(\hat{\lambda})\sim M_{0}(2\hat{\lambda})/M_{0}(2\lambda) .
Given that the loss is smooth, the presence of the horizontal asymptote, having a positive value, alongside with the presence of the minimizer at which the loss vanishes, implies a non-convex landscape.
Considering the infinite-horizon limit T → + ∞ T\to+\infty , the loss landscape exhibits a transition between the two regimes, with the following limiting behavior lim T → + ∞ ℓ ¯ x ​ ( λ ^ ) = { ( λ − λ ^ ) 2 λ ^ ​ ( λ + λ ^ ) , λ < 0 , λ ^ < 0 , + ∞ , λ ​ < 0 , λ ^ > ​ 0 , lim T → + ∞ ℓ ¯ x ​ ( λ ^ ) = { 1 , λ > 0 , λ ^ < λ , 0 , λ > 0 , λ ^ = λ , + ∞ , λ > 0 , λ ^ > λ . \lim_{T\to+\infty}\bar{\ell}_{x}(\hat{\lambda})=\begin{cases}\dfrac{(\lambda-\hat{\lambda})^{2}}{\hat{\lambda}(\lambda+\hat{\lambda})},&\lambda<0,\ \hat{\lambda}<0,\\[10.0pt]
+\infty,&\lambda<0,\ \hat{\lambda}>0,\end{cases}\qquad\lim_{T\to+\infty}\bar{\ell}_{x}(\hat{\lambda})=\begin{cases}1,&\lambda>0,\ \hat{\lambda}<\lambda,\\
0,&\lambda>0,\ \hat{\lambda}=\lambda,\\
+\infty,&\lambda>0,\ \hat{\lambda}>\lambda.\end{cases} (51) Hence, the loss landscape converges to a smooth, non-convex profile in the dissipative regime λ < 0 \lambda<0 , while it converges to a discontinuous function in the unstable regime λ > 0 \lambda>0 , with a blow-up at λ ^ > λ \hat{\lambda}>\lambda happening in both cases.
This is consistent with the intuition that learning in the unstable regime is a challenging task, and that increasing the time horizon may not be beneficial, with the collapse to a discontinuous landscape happening exponentially fast in T T . It is legitimate to expect that, the flattening of the loss landscape in the dissipative regime, and the blow-up in the unstable one, induce vanishing and exploding gradients, respectively. In particular, the exponential nature of moments, and their asymptotic behavior, suggests that the rates at which such degeneracies occur are exponential in T T .
We proceed to characterize the gradient of the loss landscape, being the main quantity of interest for gradient-based optimization. The following proposition characterizes the asymptotic behavior of the gradient, both in the tails ( λ ^ → ± ∞ ) (\hat{\lambda}\to\pm\infty) and in the infinite-horizon limit ( T → + ∞ ) (T\to+\infty) , highlighting the presence of vanishing or exploding gradients, depending on the regime of the target dynamics. Proposition A.1 (Gradient behavior) . Let T > 0 T>0 and λ ≠ 0 \lambda\neq 0 be fixed. The asymptotic behavior of the gradient of the energy-normalized trajectory objective ( 44 ) is given by ∂ λ ^ ℓ ¯ x ​ ( λ ^ ) ∼ { − 1 M 0 ​ ( 2 ​ λ ) ​ 3 2 ​ λ ^ 2 , λ ^ → − ∞ , 1 M 0 ​ ( 2 ​ λ ) ​ T ​ e 2 ​ λ ^ ​ T λ ^ , λ ^ → + ∞ . \partial_{\hat{\lambda}}\bar{\ell}_{x}(\hat{\lambda})\sim\begin{cases}-\dfrac{1}{M_{0}(2\lambda)}\dfrac{3}{2\hat{\lambda}^{2}},&\hat{\lambda}\to-\infty,\\[15.0pt]
\dfrac{1}{M_{0}(2\lambda)}\dfrac{Te^{2\hat{\lambda}T}}{\hat{\lambda}},&\hat{\lambda}\to+\infty.\end{cases} (52) Considering the infinite-horizon limit, the asymptotic behavior of the gradient, in the unstable regime λ > 0 \lambda>0 , is given by ∂ λ ^ ℓ ¯ x ​ ( λ ^ ) ∼ { 1 e 2 ​ λ ​ T ​ ( λ λ ^ 2 − 4 ​ λ ( λ + λ ^ ) 2 ) , λ ^ < − λ , − 4 ​ λ ​ T λ + λ ^ ​ e ( λ ^ − λ ) ​ T , | λ ^ | < λ , 2 ​ λ ​ T λ ^ ​ e 2 ​ ( λ ^ − λ ) ​ T , λ ^ > λ , T → + ∞ , \partial_{\hat{\lambda}}\bar{\ell}_{x}(\hat{\lambda})\sim\begin{cases}\dfrac{1}{e^{2\lambda T}}\left(\dfrac{\lambda}{\hat{\lambda}^{2}}-\dfrac{4\lambda}{(\lambda+\hat{\lambda})^{2}}\right),&\hat{\lambda}<-\lambda,\\[15.0pt]
-\dfrac{4\lambda T}{\lambda+\hat{\lambda}}e^{(\hat{\lambda}-\lambda)T},&|\hat{\lambda}|<\lambda,\\[15.0pt]
\dfrac{2\lambda T}{\hat{\lambda}}e^{2(\hat{\lambda}-\lambda)T},&\hat{\lambda}>\lambda,\\
\end{cases}\qquad T\to+\infty, (53) while in the stable regime λ < 0 \lambda<0 , it holds that lim T → + ∞ ∂ λ ^ ℓ ¯ x ​ ( λ ^ ) = 4 ​ λ ( λ + λ ^ ) 2 − λ λ ^ 2 , λ ^ < 0 , ∂ λ ^ ℓ ¯ x ​ ( λ ^ ) ∼ − 2 ​ λ ​ T λ ^ ​ e 2 ​ λ ^ ​ T , λ ^ > 0 . \lim_{T\to+\infty}\partial_{\hat{\lambda}}\bar{\ell}_{x}(\hat{\lambda})=\frac{4\lambda}{(\lambda+\hat{\lambda})^{2}}-\frac{\lambda}{\hat{\lambda}^{2}},\quad\hat{\lambda}<0,\qquad\partial_{\hat{\lambda}}\bar{\ell}_{x}(\hat{\lambda})\sim-\dfrac{2\lambda T}{\hat{\lambda}}e^{2\hat{\lambda}T},\quad\hat{\lambda}>0. (54) Proof. The asymptotic behavior of the gradient in ( 52 ),
for λ ^ → ± ∞ \hat{\lambda}\to\pm\infty , is obtained by applying ( 46 ) to the closed-form expression of the gradient ( 49 ).
Considering the infinite-horizon behavior, in the unstable regime λ > 0 \lambda>0 we distinguish between three cases:
(i) for λ ^ < − λ \hat{\lambda}<-\lambda , both M 1 ​ ( 2 ​ λ ^ ) M_{1}(2\hat{\lambda}) and M 1 ​ ( λ + λ ^ ) M_{1}(\lambda+\hat{\lambda}) converge to finite values
by ( 47 ), while M 0 ​ ( 2 ​ λ ) M_{0}(2\lambda) at the denominator diverges exponentially fast in T T , hence governing the asymptotic behavior of the gradient towards zero; (ii) for | λ ^ | < λ |\hat{\lambda}|<\lambda , both terms M 1 ​ ( 2 ​ λ ^ ) / M 0 ​ ( 2 ​ λ ) M_{1}(2\hat{\lambda})/M_{0}(2\lambda) and M 1 ​ ( λ + λ ^ ) / M 0 ​ ( 2 ​ λ ) M_{1}(\lambda+\hat{\lambda})/M_{0}(2\lambda) converge exponentially fast to zero, with the second term being slower, hence dominating; (iii) for λ ^ > λ \hat{\lambda}>\lambda , both terms diverge exponentially fast in T T , with the first term, M 1 ​ ( 2 ​ λ ^ ) / M 0 ​ ( 2 ​ λ ) M_{1}(2\hat{\lambda})/M_{0}(2\lambda) , dominating over the second, M 1 ​ ( λ + λ ^ ) / M 0 ​ ( 2 ​ λ ) M_{1}(\lambda+\hat{\lambda})/M_{0}(2\lambda) , thus yielding ( 53 ).
In the stable regime λ < 0 \lambda<0 , it is enough to consider the following two cases: (i) when λ ^ < 0 \hat{\lambda}<0 both terms in ( 49 ) converge to finite values, yielding the first limit in ( 54 ); (ii) while, in the case λ ^ > 0 \hat{\lambda}>0 , the first term diverges exponentially fast in T T , both in the case in which λ ^ < − λ \hat{\lambda}<-\lambda , when the second term converges to a finite value, and in the case in which λ ^ > − λ \hat{\lambda}>-\lambda , when the second term diverges exponentially fast in T T , but at a slower rate than the first term, thus yielding the asymptotic in ( 54 ). ∎ The results of Proposition A.1 highlight the presence of vanishing or exploding gradients, depending on the regime of the target dynamics, and how fast such degeneracies occur in the infinite-horizon limit. The behavior of the gradient in the tails of the parameter space, reported in ( 52 ), is consistent with flattening of the loss landscape for λ ^ → − ∞ \hat{\lambda}\to-\infty , and with the blow-up of the loss landscape for λ ^ → + ∞ \hat{\lambda}\to+\infty . In particular, the gradient vanishes polynomially fast for λ ^ → − ∞ \hat{\lambda}\to-\infty , while it explodes exponentially fast for λ ^ → + ∞ \hat{\lambda}\to+\infty , trivially inheriting the exponential growth of the loss landscape in that regime. In the infinite-horizon limit, the gradient presents a regime-dependent behavior, visualized in
Figure 2 over the ( λ ^ , λ ) (\hat{\lambda},\lambda) -plane, and summarized as follows. In the unstable regime λ > 0 \lambda>0 , the gradient vanishes as O ⁡ ( e − 2 ​ λ ​ T ) O(e^{-2\lambda T}) for λ ^ < − λ \hat{\lambda}<-\lambda , and as O ⁡ ( T ​ e ( λ ^ − λ ) ​ T ) O(Te^{(\hat{\lambda}-\lambda)T}) for | λ ^ | < λ |\hat{\lambda}|<\lambda ; while, for λ ^ > λ \hat{\lambda}>\lambda , it explodes at O ⁡ ( T ​ e 2 ​ ( λ ^ − λ ) ​ T ) O(Te^{2(\hat{\lambda}-\lambda)T}) -rate. Instead, in the stable regime λ < 0 \lambda<0 , the gradient converges to a smooth profile for λ ^ < 0 \hat{\lambda}<0 , while it explodes at O ⁡ ( T ​ e 2 ​ λ ^ ​ T ) O(Te^{2\hat{\lambda}T}) -rate for λ ^ > 0 \hat{\lambda}>0 . Now, we consider the Hessian of the loss landscape. In particular, being the loss not globally convex, the perspective of local convergence for gradient-based optimization is adopted, thus requiring a characterization of the Hessian at the minimizer λ ^ = λ \hat{\lambda}=\lambda .
Besides regularity of the loss, as minimum requirement for local convergence of gradient descent methods, it can be verified that the Hessian ( 50 ) is positive definite at the minimizer λ ^ = λ \hat{\lambda}=\lambda , with ∂ λ ^ 2 ℓ ¯ x ​ ( λ ) = 2 ​ M 2 ​ ( 2 ​ λ ) M 0 ​ ( 2 ​ λ ) ∈ ( 0 , 2 ​ T 2 ) . \partial^{2}_{\hat{\lambda}}\bar{\ell}_{x}(\lambda)=\frac{2M_{2}(2\lambda)}{M_{0}(2\lambda)}\in(0,2T^{2}). (55) The following proposition characterizes the behavior of the Hessian in the infinite-horizon limit, highlighting the presence of ill-conditioning in the unstable regime. Proposition A.2 (Hessian behavior) . Let λ ≠ 0 \lambda\neq 0 be fixed. Considering the Hessian of the energy-normalized trajectory objective ( 44 ) at the minimizer λ ^ = λ \hat{\lambda}=\lambda , it holds that lim T → + ∞ ∂ λ ^ 2 ℓ ¯ x ​ ( λ ) = { 1 λ 2 , λ < 0 , + ∞ , λ > 0 , \lim_{T\to+\infty}\partial^{2}_{\hat{\lambda}}\bar{\ell}_{x}(\lambda)=\begin{cases}\dfrac{1}{\lambda^{2}},&\lambda<0,\\
+\infty,&\lambda>0,\end{cases} where the divergence in the unstable regime λ > 0 \lambda>0 happens at a quadratic rate in T T , with ∂ λ ^ 2 ℓ ¯ x ​ ( λ ) ∼ 2 ​ T 2 \partial^{2}_{\hat{\lambda}}\bar{\ell}_{x}(\lambda)\sim 2T^{2} . Proof. The behavior of the Hessian in the infinite-horizon limit is obtained by applying ( 47 ) to the Hessian at the minimizer ( 55 ). The diverging behavior in the unstable regime λ > 0 \lambda>0 at a quadratic rate in T T is a direct consequence of the asymptotic behavior of the second-order moment M 2 ​ ( 2 ​ λ ) ∼ T 2 ​ e 2 ​ λ ​ T / ( 2 ​ λ ) M_{2}(2\lambda)\sim T^{2}e^{2\lambda T}/(2\lambda) .
∎ Implications for gradient-based optimization. We now turn to the perspective of local convergence of gradient descent, to study the practical implications of the above results on the optimization of the trajectory-based learning objective via first-order methods.
Proposition A.2 hints at the increasing sharpness of the loss landscape at the minimizer, with the Hessian diverging at a quadratic rate in T T , thus suggesting that the learning rate and the radius of guaranteed local convergence given by Nesterov [2018, Theorem 1.2.4] shrink with T T .
The following arguments formalize such an intuition, quantifying the rates at which such degeneracies develop.
Following Nesterov [2018, Section 1.2] , the gradient descent update for the energy-normalized trajectory objective ( 44 ) reads as λ ^ ( k + 1 ) = λ ^ ( k ) − η k ​ ∂ λ ^ ℓ ¯ x ​ ( λ ^ ( k ) ) , \hat{\lambda}^{(k+1)}=\hat{\lambda}^{(k)}-\eta_{k}\partial_{\hat{\lambda}}\bar{\ell}_{x}(\hat{\lambda}^{(k)}), with η k \eta_{k} the learning rate at iteration k k . Under suitable regularity assumptions on the loss (e.g. C 2 C^{2} with Lipschitz-continuous Hessian), local convergence of gradient descent is generally tied to the spectral properties of the Hessian at the minimizer. In particular, letting r k := | λ ^ ( k ) − λ | r_{k}:=|\hat{\lambda}^{(k)}-\lambda| be the distance of the k k -th iterate from the minimizer, we have that requiring convergence of the gradient descent update yields the following inequality 0 < r k < r ¯ := 2 ​ ∂ λ ^ 2 ℓ ¯ x ​ ( λ ) β , 0<r_{k}<\bar{r}:=\frac{2\partial_{\hat{\lambda}}^{2}\bar{\ell}_{x}(\lambda)}{\beta}, with β \beta the Lipschitz 4 4 4 Here, Lipschitz continuity is intended in a local sense, hence holding in a neighborhood of the minimizer, which is sufficient for the study of local convergence. constant of the Hessian ∂ λ ^ 2 ℓ ¯ x ​ ( λ ^ ) \partial_{\hat{\lambda}}^{2}\bar{\ell}_{x}(\hat{\lambda}) , and r ¯ \bar{r} the radius of the ball of attraction of the minimizer. By Proposition A.2 , we have that ∂ λ ^ 2 ℓ ¯ x ​ ( λ ) ∼ 2 ​ T 2 \partial_{\hat{\lambda}}^{2}\bar{\ell}_{x}(\lambda)\sim 2T^{2} in the unstable regime, while β \beta can be bounded below by the value of the third derivative of the loss at the minimizer, leading to r ¯ ≤ 4 ​ T 2 ∂ λ ^ 3 ℓ ¯ x ​ ( λ ) ∼ 4 ​ T 2 6 ​ T 3 = 2 3 ​ T , T → + ∞ , \bar{r}\leq\frac{4T^{2}}{\partial_{\hat{\lambda}}^{3}\bar{\ell}_{x}(\lambda)}\sim\frac{4T^{2}}{6T^{3}}=\frac{2}{3T},\quad T\to+\infty, showing that the guaranteed radius shrinks polynomially in T T . In particular, the asymptotic behavior of the third derivative of the loss at the minimizer can be derived by applying ( 47 ) to the closed-form expression of the third derivative, which reads as ∂ λ ^ 3 ℓ ¯ x ​ ( λ ) = 2 M 0 ​ ( 2 ​ λ ) ​ ( 4 ​ M 3 ​ ( 2 ​ λ ^ ) − M 3 ​ ( λ + λ ^ ) ) | λ ^ = λ = 6 ​ M 3 ​ ( 2 ​ λ ) M 0 ​ ( 2 ​ λ ) ∼ 6 ​ T 3 , T → + ∞ . \partial_{\hat{\lambda}}^{3}\bar{\ell}_{x}(\lambda)=\frac{2}{M_{0}(2\lambda)}\left(4M_{3}(2\hat{\lambda})-M_{3}(\lambda+\hat{\lambda})\right)\bigg|_{\hat{\lambda}=\lambda}=\frac{6M_{3}(2\lambda)}{M_{0}(2\lambda)}\sim 6T^{3},\quad T\to+\infty. Analogously, relying on the optimal choice of the learning rate, we have η k = 1 ∂ λ ^ 2 ℓ ¯ x ​ ( λ ) ∼ 1 2 ​ T 2 , T → + ∞ , \eta_{k}=\frac{1}{\partial_{\hat{\lambda}}^{2}\bar{\ell}_{x}(\lambda)}\sim\frac{1}{2T^{2}},\quad T\to+\infty, thus showing that the learning rate shrinks polynomially in T T , at a quadratic rate, in the unstable regime.
The above considerations highlight the role of the time horizon on the smoothness of the loss landscape, as already observed by Ribeiro et al. [2020] , and its implications on the convergence of gradient-based optimization. The analysis, while limited to a one-dimensional linear system, sheds light on the possible challenges that may arise in the context of high-dimensional, nonlinear systems learning via trajectory-based objectives, where the presence of unstable modes may dominate the loss landscape. In particular, the exponential rates at which gradients become uninformative, and the polynomial rates at which the learning rate and the basin of attraction shrink with T T , suggest that casting the learning problem as a minimization of a trajectory-based objective to be addressed via gradient-based optimization may be ill-suited. A.2 Proof of Proposition 2.1 , 2.2 See 2.1 Remark 3 . For a Runge-Kutta method with tableau ( A , b ) (A,b) , the stability function R ⁡ ( ξ ) R(\xi) is rational, with denominator det ( I − ξ ​ A ) \det(I-\xi A) [ Hairer and Wanner, 1991 , Proposition 3.1] , so in our case, det ( I − λ ^ ​ Δ ​ t ​ A ) \det(I-\hat{\lambda}{\Delta t}A) is a polynomial in λ ^ \hat{\lambda} admitting finitely many roots, at which the recursion x ^ k + 1 = R ⁡ ( λ ^ ​ Δ ​ t ) ​ x ^ k \hat{x}_{k+1}=R(\hat{\lambda}{\Delta t})\hat{x}_{k} , and hence the loss ( 5 ), is undefined. Since | R ⁡ ( ξ ) | → + ∞ |R(\xi)|\to+\infty as ξ \xi approaches such roots, each admits a punctured neighborhood where | R ⁡ ( λ ^ ​ Δ ​ t ) | > 1 |R(\hat{\lambda}{\Delta t})|>1 . Setting ℓ x , ∞ ​ ( λ ^ , x 0 ) = + ∞ \ell_{x,\infty}(\hat{\lambda};x_{0})=+\infty at such points is therefore a continuous extension of the loss, under which the result of Proposition 2.1 holds for every λ ^ ∈ ℂ \hat{\lambda}\in\mathbb{C} . We proceed in three steps, showing that x ¯ \bar{x} is an equilibrium for the learned dynamics; that Φ Δ ​ t \Phi_{\Delta t} , being the one-step map associated to the numerical integration of the learned dynamics, is a local diffeomorphism; finally, that the Jacobian of Φ Δ ​ t \Phi_{\Delta t} at x ¯ \bar{x} satisfies the spectral constraint. 1. x ¯ \bar{x} is an equilibrium for the learned dynamics. Denote by a k ​ ( x 0 ) := | x ⁡ ( t k , x 0 ) − x ^ k ​ ( x 0 ) | 2 a_{k}(x_{0}):=|x(t_{k},x_{0})-\hat{x}_{k}(x_{0})|^{2} . The series being convergent by ( 9 ), and a k ​ ( x 0 ) ≥ 0 a_{k}(x_{0})\geq 0 , then it holds that a k ​ ( x 0 ) → 0 a_{k}(x_{0})\to 0 as k → + ∞ k\to+\infty . By triangle inequality, it follows that | x ^ k ​ ( x 0 ) − x ¯ | ≤ | x ⁡ ( t k , x 0 ) − x ¯ | + | x ⁡ ( t k , x 0 ) − x ^ k ​ ( x 0 ) | , |\hat{x}_{k}(x_{0})-\bar{x}|\leq|x(t_{k},x_{0})-\bar{x}|+|x(t_{k},x_{0})-\hat{x}_{k}(x_{0})|, thus yielding x ^ k ​ ( x 0 ) → x ¯ \hat{x}_{k}(x_{0})\to\bar{x} as k → + ∞ k\to+\infty , for every x 0 ∈ B ⁡ ( x ¯ , δ ) x_{0}\in B(\bar{x},\delta) . Hence, considering that x ^ k + 1 ​ ( x 0 ) = Φ Δ ​ t ​ ( x ^ k ​ ( x 0 ) ) \hat{x}_{k+1}(x_{0})=\Phi_{\Delta t}(\hat{x}_{k}(x_{0})) , and passing to the limit k → + ∞ k\to+\infty , we have that x ¯ = x ¯ + Δ ​ t ​ f ^ ​ ( x ¯ ) \bar{x}=\bar{x}+{\Delta t}\hat{f}(\bar{x}) , by continuity of f ^ \hat{f} , concluding that:
(i) x ¯ \bar{x} is a fixed point of the one-step map Φ Δ ​ t \Phi_{\Delta t} , and (ii) f ^ ​ ( x ¯ ) = 0 \hat{f}(\bar{x})=0 , that is, x ¯ \bar{x} is an equilibrium for the learned dynamics. 2. Φ Δ ​ t \Phi_{\Delta t} is a local diffeomorphism. The Jacobian of Φ Δ ​ t \Phi_{\Delta t} reads as ∂ x Φ Δ ​ t ​ ( x ) = I + Δ ​ t ​ ∂ x f ^ ​ ( x ) \partial_{x}\Phi_{\Delta t}(x)=I+{\Delta t}\partial_{x}\hat{f}(x) .
With a slight abuse of notation, we denote by | ⋅ | |\cdot| also the operator norm induced by the Euclidean norm, so that | ∂ x f ^ ​ ( x ) | = sup | v | = 1 | ∂ x f ^ ​ ( x ) ​ v | |\partial_{x}\hat{f}(x)|=\sup_{|v|=1}|\partial_{x}\hat{f}(x)v| . With this notation, we have | ∂ x f ^ ​ ( x ) | ≤ Lip ⁡ [ f ^ ] |\partial_{x}\hat{f}(x)|\leq\operatorname{Lip}[\hat{f}] at every x ∈ ℝ n x\in\mathbb{R}^{n} , hence the assumption Δ ​ t ​ Lip ⁡ [ f ^ ] < 1 {\Delta t}\,\operatorname{Lip}[\hat{f}]<1 gives | Δ ​ t ​ ∂ x f ^ ​ ( x ) | < 1 |{\Delta t}\partial_{x}\hat{f}(x)|<1 , implying that ∂ x Φ Δ ​ t ​ ( x ) \partial_{x}\Phi_{\Delta t}(x) is invertible by the Neumann series. By the inverse function theorem, Φ Δ ​ t \Phi_{\Delta t} is then a local C 1 C^{1} diffeomorphism at every x ∈ ℝ n x\in\mathbb{R}^{n} . 3. Jacobian spectral constraint. We proceed by contradiction, supposing that ρ ⁡ ( I + Δ ​ t ​ ∂ x f ^ ​ ( x ¯ ) ) > 1 \rho(I+{\Delta t}\partial_{x}\hat{f}(\bar{x}))>1 , so that the unstable subspace of ∂ x Φ Δ ​ t ​ ( x ¯ ) \partial_{x}\Phi_{\Delta t}(\bar{x}) is nontrivial.
Being Φ Δ ​ t \Phi_{\Delta t} a local C 1 C^{1} diffeomorphism, and x ¯ \bar{x} a fixed point of Φ Δ ​ t \Phi_{\Delta t} , the center-stable manifold theorem [ Shub, 1987 , Theorem III.7] applies, so that there exist a neighborhood U U of x ¯ \bar{x} and a C 1 C^{1} embedded manifold ℳ cs \mathcal{M}^{\rm cs} with dim ℳ cs < n \dim\mathcal{M}^{\rm cs}<n , such that, if { Φ Δ ​ t k ​ ( y ) } k ≥ 0 ⊂ U \{\Phi_{\Delta t}^{k}(y)\}_{k\geq 0}\subset U , namely, the forward orbit starting from y ∈ U y\in U remains in U U , then y ∈ ℳ cs y\in\mathcal{M}^{\rm cs} .
Fix x 0 ∈ B ⁡ ( x ¯ , δ ) x_{0}\in B(\bar{x},\delta) and write x ^ k = Φ Δ ​ t k ​ ( x 0 ) \hat{x}_{k}=\Phi_{\Delta t}^{k}(x_{0}) . By step 1, we have that x ^ k → x ¯ \hat{x}_{k}\to\bar{x} , so there is some time index k ∗ = k ∗ ​ ( x 0 ) ∈ ℕ k_{*}=k_{*}(x_{0})\in\mathbb{N} with x ^ k ∈ U \hat{x}_{k}\in U for every k ≥ k ∗ k\geq k_{*} , namely, the forward orbit of x ^ k ∗ \hat{x}_{k_{*}} is such that { x ^ k ∗ + j } j ≥ 0 ⊂ U \{\hat{x}_{k_{*}+j}\}_{j\geq 0}\subset U , hence x ^ k ∗ ∈ ℳ cs \hat{x}_{k_{*}}\in\mathcal{M}^{\rm cs} , that is x 0 ∈ Φ Δ ​ t − k ∗ ​ ( ℳ cs ) x_{0}\in\Phi_{\Delta t}^{-{k_{*}}}(\mathcal{M}^{\rm cs}) . Note that the initial condition x 0 x_{0} itself need not belong
to ℳ cs \mathcal{M}^{\rm cs} , its orbit being unconstrained before time k ∗ k_{*} . Since k ∗ k_{*} depends on x 0 x_{0} , this
yields B ⁡ ( x ¯ , δ ) ⊆ ⋃ k ∈ ℕ Φ Δ ​ t − k ​ ( ℳ cs ) . B(\bar{x},\delta)\ \subseteq\ \bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\Phi_{\Delta t}^{-k}\big(\mathcal{M}^{\rm cs}\big). Since ℳ cs \mathcal{M}^{\rm cs} has positive codimension < n <n , it has null Lebesgue measure in ℝ n \mathbb{R}^{n} , and being Φ Δ ​ t \Phi_{\Delta t} a local diffeomorphism by step 2, each preimage Φ Δ ​ t − k ​ ( ℳ cs ) \Phi_{\Delta t}^{-k}(\mathcal{M}^{\rm cs}) has null measure as well. So, the countable union of such sets has thus null Lebesgue measure, contradicting the fact that B ⁡ ( x ¯ , δ ) B(\bar{x},\delta) has positive measure. Therefore ρ ⁡ ( I + Δ ​ t ​ ∂ x f ^ ​ ( x ¯ ) ) ≤ 1 \rho(I+{\Delta t}\partial_{x}\hat{f}(\bar{x}))\leq 1 , and since σ ⁡ ( I + Δ ​ t ​ ∂ x f ^ ​ ( x ¯ ) ) = { 1 + Δ ​ t ​ ν ^ : ν ^ ∈ σ ⁡ ( ∂ x f ^ ​ ( x ¯ ) ) } \sigma(I+{\Delta t}\partial_{x}\hat{f}(\bar{x}))=\{1+{\Delta t}\hat{\nu}:\hat{\nu}\in\sigma(\partial_{x}\hat{f}(\bar{x}))\} by spectral mapping, this reads as σ ⁡ ( ∂ x f ^ ​ ( x ¯ ) ) ⊆ 𝒜 ¯ Δ ​ t \sigma(\partial_{x}\hat{f}(\bar{x}))\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{\Delta t} . ∎ Remark 4 . While x ¯ \bar{x} is asymptotically stable for the target dynamics by assumption, and hence Lyapunov stable, the first step of the proof only establishes that it is an attracting fixed point of the one-step map Φ Δ ​ t \Phi_{\Delta t} .
Lyapunov stability of the equilibrium x ¯ \bar{x} for Φ Δ ​ t \Phi_{\Delta t} would yield the result at once by a linearization argument, but it would require a stronger assumption than ( 9 ), namely that ∑ k ≥ 1 a k ​ ( x 0 ) \sum_{k\geq 1}a_{k}(x_{0}) vanish as x 0 → x ¯ x_{0}\to\bar{x} , rather than merely be finite for each x 0 x_{0} .
Attraction only constrains the asymptotic behavior of the orbit x ^ k ​ ( x 0 ) \hat{x}_{k}(x_{0}) , which may leave B ⁡ ( x ¯ , δ ) B(\bar{x},\delta) during the transient, before converging.
The center-stable manifold theorem circumvents this, being applied to the tail of each converging orbit. Then, local invertibility of the one-step map Φ Δ ​ t \Phi_{\Delta t} ensures that, whenever ρ ⁡ ( ∂ x Φ Δ ​ t ​ ( x ¯ ) ) > 1 \rho(\partial_{x}\Phi_{\Delta t}(\bar{x}))>1 , the set of initial conditions attracted to x ¯ \bar{x} has null measure, yielding a contradiction with the fact that it contains the ball B ⁡ ( x ¯ , δ ) B(\bar{x},\delta) , of positive measure. ∎ B.2 Proof of Lemma 3.3 , 3.4 See 3.3 Proof. We proceed by first identifying a compact set within which the solutions lie, and then applying Gronwall’s lemma to obtain the desired estimates. 1. Confinement. By assumption, both flow maps are defined on [ 0 , T ] × 𝒳 0 [0,T]\times\mathcal{X}_{0} . Since f f and f ^ \hat{f} are locally Lipschitz in the second argument, the flow maps ( t , x 0 ) ↦ x ⁡ ( t , x 0 ) (t,x_{0})\mapsto x(t,x_{0}) and ( t , x 0 ) ↦ x ^ ​ ( t , x 0 ) (t,x_{0})\mapsto\hat{x}(t,x_{0}) are jointly continuous there [ Teschl, 2012 , Theorem 2.9] . As continuous images of the compact set [ 0 , T ] × 𝒳 0 [0,T]\times\mathcal{X}_{0} , the reachable sets 𝒳 := x ⁡ ( [ 0 , T ] × 𝒳 0 ) \mathcal{X}:=x([0,T]\times\mathcal{X}_{0}) and 𝒳 ^ := x ^ ​ ( [ 0 , T ] × 𝒳 0 ) \widehat{\mathcal{X}}:=\hat{x}([0,T]\times\mathcal{X}_{0}) are compact, and so is K := 𝒳 ∪ 𝒳 ^ K:=\mathcal{X}\cup\widehat{\mathcal{X}} . We proceed by first identifying a compact set enclosing the solutions, and then applying discrete Gronwall’s lemma to obtain the desired estimate. 1. Confinement. By similar arguments as in the proof of Lemma 3.3 , the continuous-time flow map x ⁡ ( t , x 0 ) x(t,x_{0}) is jointly continuous, yielding a compact reachable set 𝒳 := x ⁡ ( [ 0 , T ] × 𝒳 0 ) \mathcal{X}:=x([0,T]\times\mathcal{X}_{0}) . For any two probability measures μ , ν \mu,\nu on X X , the Wasserstein distance of order p p between μ \mu and ν \nu is defined as 𝒲 p p ​ ( μ , ν ) := inf π ∈ Π ⁡ ( μ , ν ) ∫ X × X d ​ ( x , y ) p ​ π ​ ( 𝑑 x , 𝑑 y ) , \mathcal{W}_{p}^{p}(\mu,\nu):=\inf_{\pi\in\Pi(\mu,\nu)}\int_{X\times X}d(x,y)^{p}\pi(dx,dy), (62) with Π \Pi denoting the set of all joint probability measures on X × X X\times X whose marginals are μ \mu and ν \nu . ∎ B.4 Proof of Theorem 3.8 , 3.9 We first provide a result establishing that the Gaussian error linear unit (GELU) activation function is an exponential ( 1 , 1 ) (1,1) partition of unity (PU), defined in Gühring and Raslan [2021, Section 4.1] and required by Gühring and Raslan [2021, Theorem 4.9] to establish approximation rates in Theorem 3.8 . Lemma B.1 . The GELU activation function ϱ ⁡ ( x ) = x ​ Φ ​ ( x ) \varrho(x)=x\Phi(x) , with Φ \Phi being the standard Gaussian cumulative distribution function, is an exponential ( 1 , 1 ) (1,1) -PU-admissible in the sense of Gühring and Raslan [2021, Definition 4.2] , with A = 0 A=0 , B = 1 B=1 , C = 2 / π C=\sqrt{2/\pi} , D = 1 / 2 D=1/2 and R = 1 R=1 . Proof. The proof follows by direct verification of the conditions (a)-(d) in Gühring and Raslan [2021, Definition 4.2] . Denote by ϕ = Φ ′ \phi=\Phi^{\prime} the standard Gaussian probability density function. Then note that ϱ ′ ​ ( x ) = Φ ⁡ ( x ) + x ​ ϕ ​ ( x ) , ϱ ′′ ​ ( x ) = ( 2 − x 2 ) ​ ϕ ​ ( x ) . \varrho^{\prime}(x)=\Phi(x)+x\phi(x),\qquad\varrho^{\prime\prime}(x)=(2-x^{2})\phi(x). (63) We proceed to verify the conditions (a)-(d) in Gühring and Raslan [2021, Definition 4.2] . (a) ϱ \varrho is Lipschitz continuous, noting that ϱ ∈ C ∞ ​ ( ℝ ) \varrho\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}) and ϱ ′ ​ ( x ) → 1 \varrho^{\prime}(x)\to 1 as x → + ∞ x\to+\infty and ϱ ′ ​ ( x ) → 0 \varrho^{\prime}(x)\to 0 as x → − ∞ x\to-\infty , and that ϱ ′′ \varrho^{\prime\prime} vanishes only at x = ± 2 x=\pm\sqrt{2} , so that ϱ ′ \varrho^{\prime} attains its maximum at x = 2 x=\sqrt{2} , with ϱ ′ ​ ( 2 ) = Φ ⁡ ( 2 ) + 2 ​ ϕ ​ ( 2 ) \varrho^{\prime}(\sqrt{2})=\Phi(\sqrt{2})+\sqrt{2}\phi(\sqrt{2}) , so that ‖ ϱ ′ ‖ L ∞ ​ ( ℝ ) < ∞ \|\varrho^{\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}<\infty . (b) ϕ , Φ ∈ C ∞ ​ ( ℝ ) \phi,\Phi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}) , so ϱ ∈ C ∞ ​ ( ℝ ) ⊂ C 1 ​ ( ℝ ∖ [ − R , R ] ) \varrho\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})\subset C^{1}(\mathbb{R}\setminus[-R,R]) , for every R > 0 R>0 . (c) ϱ ′ ∈ W 0 , ∞ ​ ( ℝ ) = L ∞ ​ ( ℝ ) \varrho^{\prime}\in W^{0,\infty}(\mathbb{R})=L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}) , by (a). (d) Take A = 0 A=0 , B = 1 B=1 . Then, recalling that 1 − Φ ⁡ ( x ) ≤ ϕ ⁡ ( x ) / x 1-\Phi(x)\leq\phi(x)/x for x > 0 x>0 , we have | 1 − ϱ ′ ​ ( x ) | = | 1 − Φ ⁡ ( x ) − x ​ ϕ ​ ( x ) | ≤ ( 1 − Φ ⁡ ( x ) ) + x ​ ϕ ​ ( x ) ≤ ( x + 1 x ) ​ ϕ ​ ( x ) , |1-\varrho^{\prime}(x)|=|1-\Phi(x)-x\phi(x)|\leq(1-\Phi(x))+x\phi(x)\leq\left(x+\frac{1}{x}\right)\phi(x), (64) for x > 0 x>0 , while, by symmetry of the Gaussian distribution, for x < 0 x<0 we have | ϱ ′ ​ ( x ) | \displaystyle|\varrho^{\prime}(x)| = | Φ ⁡ ( x ) + x ​ ϕ ​ ( x ) | = | 1 − Φ ⁡ ( − x ) + x ​ ϕ ​ ( x ) | , \displaystyle=|\Phi(x)+x\phi(x)|=|1-\Phi(-x)+x\phi(x)|, ≤ ( 1 − Φ ⁡ ( − x ) ) + | x | ​ ϕ ​ ( x ) ≤ ( | x | + 1 | x | ) ​ ϕ ​ ( x ) . \displaystyle\leq(1-\Phi(-x))+|x|\phi(x)\leq\left(|x|+\frac{1}{|x|}\right)\phi(x). (65) Both ( 64 ) and ( 65 ) yield the same bound, namely g ⁡ ( x ) := ( | x | + 1 / | x | ) ​ ϕ ​ ( x ) g(x):=(|x|+1/|x|)\phi(x) for x ≠ 0 x\neq 0 . In particular, for x > 0 x>0 , we have that g ′ ​ ( x ) = − ( x 2 + 1 / x 2 ) ​ ϕ ​ ( x ) < 0 g^{\prime}(x)=-(x^{2}+1/x^{2})\phi(x)<0 , so that g g is strictly decreasing for x > 0 x>0 , while it is strictly increasing for x < 0 x<0 , by symmetry. Hence, setting h ⁡ ( x ) = g ⁡ ( x ) ​ e x 2 h(x)=g(x)e^{\frac{x}{2}} , we have that h h is strictly decreasing for x > 0 x>0 , so that | 1 − ϱ ′ ​ ( x ) | ≤ g ⁡ ( x ) = h ⁡ ( x ) ​ e − x 2 ≤ h ⁡ ( 1 ) ​ e − x 2 = 2 π ​ e − x 2 , x > 1 . |1-\varrho^{\prime}(x)|\leq g(x)=h(x)e^{-\frac{x}{2}}\leq h(1)e^{-\frac{x}{2}}=\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}e^{-\frac{x}{2}},\quad x>1. Similarly, setting h ⁡ ( x ) = g ⁡ ( x ) ​ e − x 2 h(x)=g(x)e^{-\frac{x}{2}} , we have that h h is strictly increasing for x < 0 x<0 , so that | ϱ ′ ​ ( x ) | ≤ g ⁡ ( x ) = h ⁡ ( x ) ​ e x 2 ≤ h ⁡ ( − 1 ) ​ e x 2 = 2 π ​ e x 2 , x < − 1 . |\varrho^{\prime}(x)|\leq g(x)=h(x)e^{\frac{x}{2}}\leq h(-1)e^{\frac{x}{2}}=\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}e^{\frac{x}{2}},\quad x<-1. Hence, exponential decay conditions (d.1) and (d.2) are satisfied by taking C = 2 / π C=\sqrt{2/\pi} , D = 1 / 2 D=1/2 and R = 1 R=1 , while (d.3) holds vacuously. ∎ See 3.8 Proof. The proof develops in four steps. 1. Approximation of the velocity field and Lipschitz bound. By Assumption 1 , f ∈ W 2 , ∞ ​ ( [ 0 , T ] × K , ℝ n ) f\in W^{2,\infty}([0,T]\times K;\mathbb{R}^{n}) . The affine reparameterization and rescaling can be respectively absorbed into the first and last layer of the neural network architecture, so that the resulting approximant belongs to the same class 𝒩 ϱ ​ ( ℝ n + 1 , ℝ n ) \mathcal{N}_{\varrho}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1};\mathbb{R}^{n}) . ∎ Prior to proving Proposition 4.3 , we recall the definition of continuous-stage Runge-Kutta (csRK) methods, which generalize s s -stage Runge-Kutta schemes to a continuum of stages, following Hairer [2010] and Tang [2018] . Definition 2 (Continuous-stage Runge-Kutta methods) . Let a ⁡ ( γ , σ ) a(\gamma,\sigma) , b ⁡ ( γ ) b(\gamma) , and c ⁡ ( γ ) c(\gamma) be continuous functions of γ , σ ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] \gamma,\sigma\in[0,1] , with c ⁡ ( γ ) = ∫ 0 1 a ⁡ ( γ , σ ) ​ 𝑑 σ c(\gamma)=\int_{0}^{1}a(\gamma,\sigma)d\sigma . The one-step method x k ↦ x k + 1 x_{k}\mapsto x_{k+1} , applied to ( 1 ), defined by X ⁡ ( γ ) \displaystyle X(\gamma) = x k + Δ ​ t ​ ∫ 0 1 a ⁡ ( γ , σ ) ​ f ​ ( t k + c ⁡ ( σ ) ​ Δ ​ t , X ⁡ ( σ ) ) ​ 𝑑 σ , \displaystyle=x_{k}+{\Delta t}\int_{0}^{1}a(\gamma,\sigma)f(t_{k}+c(\sigma){\Delta t},X(\sigma))\,d\sigma, (68) x k + 1 \displaystyle x_{k+1} = x k + Δ ​ t ​ ∫ 0 1 b ⁡ ( γ ) ​ f ​ ( t k + c ⁡ ( γ ) ​ Δ ​ t , X ⁡ ( γ ) ) ​ 𝑑 γ , \displaystyle=x_{k}+{\Delta t}\int_{0}^{1}b(\gamma)f(t_{k}+c(\gamma){\Delta t},X(\gamma))\,d\gamma, (69) is a continuous-stage Runge-Kutta (csRK) method, with continuous-stage variable X ⁡ ( γ ) X(\gamma) . Here, the continuous-stage tableau ( a ⁡ ( γ , σ ) , b ⁡ ( γ ) , c ⁡ ( γ ) ) (a(\gamma,\sigma),b(\gamma),c(\gamma)) generalizes the coefficients ( a i ​ j , b i , c i ) (a_{ij},b_{i},c_{i}) of a classical Runge-Kutta method.
In the autonomous setting, X ⁡ ( γ ) X(\gamma) identifies with the collocation polynomial [ Hairer, 2010 ] , yielding the continuous approximation of the solution X ⁡ ( γ ) ≈ x ⁡ ( t k + γ ​ Δ ​ t ) X(\gamma)\approx x(t_{k}+\gamma{\Delta t}) over the subinterval [ t k , t k + 1 ] [t_{k},t_{k+1}] . Given the relation ( 68 ), the resulting method is implicit. See 4.3 Proof. Equivalence with implicit TF update. In a teacher forcing setting, the learned dynamics f ^ \hat{f} is advanced by a single step from the observed state x ⁡ ( t k , x 0 ) x(t_{k},x_{0}) , over the subinterval [ t k , t k + 1 ] [t_{k},t_{k+1}] , yielding the implicit update x ^ k + 1 = x ⁡ ( t k , x 0 ) + Δ ​ t ​ F ​ ( t k , x ⁡ ( t k , x 0 ) , x ^ k + 1 , Δ ​ t , f ^ ) , \hat{x}_{k+1}=x(t_{k},x_{0})+{\Delta t}F(t_{k},x(t_{k},x_{0}),\hat{x}_{k+1},{\Delta t};\hat{f}), (74) whose solution defines the residual ε k + 1 TF ​ ( x 0 ) = x ⁡ ( t k + 1 , x 0 ) − x ^ k + 1 \varepsilon_{k+1}^{\rm TF}(x_{0})=x(t_{k+1},x_{0})-\hat{x}_{k+1} , having denoted by F F the increment function associated with the csRK method. Hence, TF first solves the implicit equation for x ^ k + 1 \hat{x}_{k+1} , and then computes the residual, carrying the nonlinear solve in the forward pass. On the other hand, when addressing VM bias minimization, the residual ( 72 ) is computed directly given the observed pair, without solving the implicit equation for x ^ k + 1 \hat{x}_{k+1} . We thus proceed to derive the bounds relating the two residuals, showing equivalence between the two approaches.
First, F F is Lipschitz continuous with respect to its third argument, with Lipschitz constant at most M / 2 M/2 , since | F Δ ​ t ​ ( t , y , z , f ^ ) − F Δ ​ t ​ ( t , y , z ′ , f ^ ) | \displaystyle|F_{\Delta t}(t,y,z;\hat{f})-F_{\Delta t}(t,y,z^{\prime};\hat{f})| ≤ ∫ 0 1 | f ^ ​ ( t + γ ​ Δ ​ t , ( 1 − γ ) ​ y + γ ​ z ) − f ^ ​ ( t + γ ​ Δ ​ t , ( 1 − γ ) ​ y + γ ​ z ′ ) | ​ 𝑑 γ , \displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{1}|\hat{f}(t+\gamma{\Delta t},(1-\gamma)y+\gamma z)-\hat{f}(t+\gamma{\Delta t},(1-\gamma)y+\gamma z^{\prime})|d\gamma, ≤ M ​ ∫ 0 1 γ ​ | z − z ′ | ​ 𝑑 γ = M 2 ​ | z − z ′ | . \displaystyle\leq M\int_{0}^{1}\gamma|z-z^{\prime}|d\gamma=\frac{M}{2}|z-z^{\prime}|. Hence, the map z ↦ x ⁡ ( t k , x 0 ) + Δ ​ t ​ F ​ ( t k , x ⁡ ( t k , x 0 ) , z , Δ ​ t , f ^ ) z\mapsto x(t_{k},x_{0})+{\Delta t}F(t_{k},x(t_{k},x_{0}),z,{\Delta t};\hat{f}) is Lipschitz, and a contraction whenever Δ ​ t ​ M < 2 {\Delta t}M<2 . Thus, by Banach fixed point theorem, the implicit equation ( 74 ) admits a unique solution x ^ k + 1 ∈ B ¯ ​ ( x ⁡ ( t k + 1 ) , δ ) \hat{x}_{k+1}\in\bar{B}(x(t_{k+1}),\delta) , so the TF update is well defined.
Then, subtracting the two residuals ε k + 1 VM − ε k + 1 TF = Δ ​ t ​ ( F ⁡ ( t k , x ⁡ ( t k ) , x ⁡ ( t k + 1 ) , Δ ​ t , f ^ ) − F ⁡ ( t k , x ⁡ ( t k ) , x ^ k + 1 , Δ ​ t , f ^ ) ) , \varepsilon_{k+1}^{\rm VM}-\varepsilon_{k+1}^{\rm TF}={\Delta t}\left(F(t_{k},x(t_{k}),x(t_{k+1}),{\Delta t};\hat{f})-F(t_{k},x(t_{k}),\hat{x}_{k+1},{\Delta t};\hat{f})\right), (75) by the triangle inequality and Lipschitz continuity of F F , the lower bound follows | ε k + 1 VM | \displaystyle|\varepsilon_{k+1}^{\rm VM}| ≤ | ε k + 1 TF | + Δ ​ t ​ | F ⁡ ( t k , x ⁡ ( t k ) , x ⁡ ( t k + 1 ) , Δ ​ t , f ^ ) − F ⁡ ( t k , x ⁡ ( t k ) , x ^ k + 1 , Δ ​ t , f ^ ) | , \displaystyle\leq|\varepsilon_{k+1}^{\rm TF}|+{\Delta t}\left|F(t_{k},x(t_{k}),x(t_{k+1}),{\Delta t};\hat{f})-F(t_{k},x(t_{k}),\hat{x}_{k+1},{\Delta t};\hat{f})\right|, ≤ | ε k + 1 TF | + Δ ​ t ​ M 2 ​ | x ⁡ ( t k + 1 ) − x ^ k + 1 | , \displaystyle\leq|\varepsilon_{k+1}^{\rm TF}|+\frac{{\Delta t}M}{2}|x(t_{k+1})-\hat{x}_{k+1}|, = | ε k + 1 TF | + Δ ​ t ​ M 2 ​ | ε k + 1 TF | , \displaystyle=|\varepsilon_{k+1}^{\rm TF}|+\frac{{\Delta t}M}{2}|\varepsilon_{k+1}^{\rm TF}|, = ( 1 + Δ ​ t ​ M 2 ) ​ | ε k + 1 TF | , \displaystyle=\left(1+\frac{{\Delta t}M}{2}\right)|\varepsilon_{k+1}^{\rm TF}|, yielding 1 1 + 1 2 ​ Δ ​ t ​ M ​ | ε k + 1 VM | ≤ | ε k + 1 TF | , \frac{1}{1+\frac{1}{2}{\Delta t}M}|\varepsilon_{k+1}^{\rm VM}|\leq|\varepsilon_{k+1}^{\rm TF}|, where dependence on x 0 x_{0} is omitted for notational convenience.

Bound on B k B_{k} . Given a Lipschitz continuous function g : [ 0 , Δ ​ t ] → ℝ n g:[0,{\Delta t}]\to\mathbb{R}^{n} , it holds that Var τ ⁡ [ g ⁡ ( τ ) ] = 𝔼 τ ​ | g ⁡ ( τ ) − 𝔼 τ ​ [ g ⁡ ( τ ) ] | 2 ≤ 𝔼 τ ​ | g ⁡ ( τ ) − g ⁡ ( Δ ​ t 2 ) | 2 ≤ Lip ⁡ [ g ] 2 ​ Var τ ​ [ τ ] = Lip ⁡ [ g ] 2 ​ Δ ​ t 2 12 . \operatorname{Var}_{\tau}[g(\tau)]=\mathbb{E}_{\tau}|g(\tau)-\mathbb{E}_{\tau}[g(\tau)]|^{2}\leq\mathbb{E}_{\tau}\left|g(\tau)-g\left(\frac{{\Delta t}}{2}\right)\right|^{2}\leq\operatorname{Lip}[g]^{2}\operatorname{Var}_{\tau}[\tau]=\operatorname{Lip}[g]^{2}\frac{{\Delta t}^{2}}{12}. (79) Combining the bounds ( 78 ) and ( 79 ) concludes the proof.

We proceed by Taylor expansion with integral remainder, similarly as in the proof of Theorem 4.4 in Appendix C.3 , denoting by ∂ x f \partial_{x}f the Jacobian of f f , and similarly for f ^ \hat{f} . By Evans [2010, Section 5.8.2, Theorem 4] applied to g g and ∂ x g \partial_{x}g , any g ∈ ℱ g\in\mathcal{F} admits a representative in C 1 ​ ( K , ℝ n ) C^{1}(K;\mathbb{R}^{n}) , with | g | |g| , | ∂ x g | |\partial_{x}g| , Lip ⁡ [ g ] \operatorname{Lip}[g] and Lip ⁡ [ ∂ x g ] \operatorname{Lip}[\partial_{x}g] bounded by M M on K K . First consider the velocity target, which is a first-order finite difference. We notice that ∂ t 2 x ⁡ ( t , x 0 ) = ∂ x f ⁡ ( x ⁡ ( t , x 0 ) ) ​ f ​ ( x ⁡ ( t , x 0 ) ) \partial_{t}^{2}x(t,x_{0})=\partial_{x}f(x(t,x_{0}))f(x(t,x_{0})) , and the map t ↦ ∂ t 2 x ⁡ ( t , x 0 ) t\mapsto\partial_{t}^{2}x(t,x_{0}) is Lipschitz continuous with constant 2 ​ M 3 2M^{3} , namely, for every t , s ∈ [ 0 , T ] t,s\in[0,T] , | ∂ t 2 x ⁡ ( t , x 0 ) − ∂ t 2 x ⁡ ( s , x 0 ) | \displaystyle|\partial_{t}^{2}x(t,x_{0})-\partial_{t}^{2}x(s,x_{0})| = | ∂ x f ⁡ ( x ⁡ ( t , x 0 ) ) ​ f ​ ( x ⁡ ( t , x 0 ) ) − ∂ x f ⁡ ( x ⁡ ( s , x 0 ) ) ​ f ​ ( x ⁡ ( s , x 0 ) ) | , \displaystyle=|\partial_{x}f(x(t,x_{0}))f(x(t,x_{0}))-\partial_{x}f(x(s,x_{0}))f(x(s,x_{0}))|, ≤ | ∂ x f ⁡ ( x ⁡ ( t , x 0 ) ) | ​ | f ⁡ ( x ⁡ ( t , x 0 ) ) − f ⁡ ( x ⁡ ( s , x 0 ) ) | \displaystyle\leq|\partial_{x}f(x(t,x_{0}))||f(x(t,x_{0}))-f(x(s,x_{0}))| + | ∂ x f ⁡ ( x ⁡ ( t , x 0 ) ) − ∂ x f ⁡ ( x ⁡ ( s , x 0 ) ) | ​ | f ⁡ ( x ⁡ ( s , x 0 ) ) | , \displaystyle\qquad+|\partial_{x}f(x(t,x_{0}))-\partial_{x}f(x(s,x_{0}))||f(x(s,x_{0}))|, ≤ 2 ​ M 3 ​ | t − s | . \displaystyle\leq 2M^{3}|t-s|. +2\langle f(x(t_{k},x_{0}))-\hat{f}(x(t_{k},x_{0})),O({\Delta t}^{2})\rangle+O({\Delta t}^{3}). Then ( 39 ) follows, since, under | f ⁡ ( x ⁡ ( t k , x 0 ) ) − f ^ ​ ( x ⁡ ( t k , x 0 ) ) | ≤ C ​ Δ ​ t 2 |f(x(t_{k},x_{0}))-\hat{f}(x(t_{k},x_{0}))|\leq C{\Delta t}^{2} , the zero-order term is bounded by C 2 ​ Δ ​ t 4 C^{2}{\Delta t}^{4} , while, by Cauchy–Schwarz, the cross-terms are absorbed into O ⁡ ( Δ ​ t 3 ) O({\Delta t}^{3}) . 2. Variance term. Subtracting ( 85 ) from ( 84 ), the zero-order term cancels, and we obtain f ^ k ​ ( τ , x 0 ) − 𝔼 τ ​ [ f ^ k ​ ( τ , x 0 ) ] \displaystyle\hat{f}_{k}(\tau;x_{0})-\mathbb{E}_{\tau}[\hat{f}_{k}(\tau;x_{0})] = ( τ − Δ ​ t 2 ) ​ ∂ x f ^ ​ ( x ⁡ ( t k , x 0 ) ) ​ f ​ ( x ⁡ ( t k , x 0 ) ) + O ⁡ ( Δ ​ t 2 ) , \displaystyle=\left(\tau-\frac{{\Delta t}}{2}\right)\partial_{x}\hat{f}(x(t_{k},x_{0}))f(x(t_{k},x_{0}))+O({\Delta t}^{2}), from which, taking squared norm and expectation, we obtain Var τ ⁡ [ f ^ k ​ ( τ , x 0 ) ] \displaystyle\operatorname{Var}_{\tau}[\hat{f}_{k}(\tau;x_{0})] = 𝔼 τ ​ [ ( τ − Δ ​ t 2 ) 2 ] ​ | ∂ x f ^ ​ ( x ⁡ ( t k , x 0 ) ) ​ f ​ ( x ⁡ ( t k , x 0 ) ) | 2 + O ⁡ ( Δ ​ t 3 ) , \displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\tau}\left[\left(\tau-\frac{{\Delta t}}{2}\right)^{2}\right]\left|\partial_{x}\hat{f}(x(t_{k},x_{0}))f(x(t_{k},x_{0}))\right|^{2}+O({\Delta t}^{3}), = Δ ​ t 2 12 ​ | ∂ x f ^ ​ ( x ⁡ ( t k , x 0 ) ) ​ f ​ ( x ⁡ ( t k , x 0 ) ) | 2 + O ⁡ ( Δ ​ t 3 ) , \displaystyle=\frac{{\Delta t}^{2}}{12}|\partial_{x}\hat{f}(x(t_{k},x_{0}))f(x(t_{k},x_{0}))|^{2}+O({\Delta t}^{3}), having used the fact that Var τ ⁡ [ τ ] = Δ ​ t 2 / 12 \operatorname{Var}_{\tau}[\tau]={\Delta t}^{2}/12 . ∎ Appendix D Experimental details This appendix details the experiments of Section 5 . Section D.1 describes the benchmark systems and the generation of the datasets, Section D.2 the model architectures, and
Section D.3 the training and evaluation procedures. Section D.4 provides additional material supporting our results, including the tables of validation and test errors for ordinary differential equations (ODEs) and partial differential equations (PDEs) benchmarks, together with visualizations of the solutions. All experiments were run on a single NVIDIA L40S GPU with 48GB of memory.
The code to reproduce the experiments is available at github.com/farenga/velocity-matching . D.1 Benchmark systems and datasets D.1.1 Ordinary differential equations For each ODE system, training, validation and test trajectories were generated from independently sampled initial conditions, by integrating the true dynamics with the DOP853 scheme [ Hairer et al., 2008 ] , with absolute and relative tolerances equal to 10 − 12 10^{-12} , and were observed on a uniform grid of step size Δ ​ t {\Delta t} over [ 0 , T ] [0,T] . An additional set of test trajectories, observed with step size 10 − 4 10^{-4} , was used for the continuous-time approximation study of Section 5.2 . The number of trajectories, the step sizes and the time horizons of each split are reported in Table 1 . Note that test trajectories extend over a time horizon twice as long as the training one, so that test errors also measure extrapolation in time. Training Validation Test Test (fine) System N N Δ ​ t {\Delta t} T T N N Δ ​ t {\Delta t} T T N N Δ ​ t {\Delta t} T T N N Δ ​ t {\Delta t} T T Euler 100 5 ⋅ 10 − 2 5\cdot 10^{-2} 20 20 20 10 − 2 10^{-2} 20 20 50 5 ⋅ 10 − 3 5\cdot 10^{-3} 40 40 10 10 − 4 10^{-4} 20 20 Stuart–Landau 50 5 ⋅ 10 − 2 5\cdot 10^{-2} 10 10 10 10 − 2 10^{-2} 10 10 50 2 ⋅ 10 − 3 2\cdot 10^{-3} 20 20 10 10 − 4 10^{-4} 10 10 Active Müller–Brown 200 5 ⋅ 10 − 2 5\cdot 10^{-2} 10 10 20 10 − 2 10^{-2} 10 10 50 2 ⋅ 10 − 3 2\cdot 10^{-3} 20 20 10 10 − 4 10^{-4} 10 10 Lorenz 80 Δ ​ t {\Delta t} 10 10 – – – 50 10 − 3 10^{-3} 20 20 – – – Table 1: ODE datasets. Number of trajectories N N , observation step size Δ ​ t {\Delta t} and time horizon T T of each split. For the Lorenz system, the training step size takes the values Δ ​ t ∈ { 1 , 2 , 5 , 10 , 20 , 50 , 100 } ⋅ 10 − 3 {\Delta t}\in\{1,2,5,10,20,50,100\}\cdot 10^{-3} , used to assess the Jacobian spectrum at C − C_{-} , reported in Figure 9 . We report the formulation of the ODE benchmarks below, specifying for each system its governing equations, the parametric configuration, and the distribution ρ 0 \rho_{0} of the initial conditions. Euler. The angular momentum x ∈ ℝ 3 x\in\mathbb{R}^{3} of a free rigid body evolves according to a Lie–Poisson system [ Marsden and Ratiu, 1999 , Hairer, 2010 ] , reading as x ˙ = x × I − 1 ​ x , I = diag ⁡ ( I 1 , I 2 , I 3 ) . \dot{x}=x\times\mathrm{I}^{-1}x,\qquad\mathrm{I}=\operatorname{diag}(I_{1},I_{2},I_{3}). The kinetic energy H ⁡ ( x ) = 1 2 ​ x ⊤ ​ I − 1 ​ x H(x)=\frac{1}{2}x^{\top}\mathrm{I}^{-1}x and the Casimir invariant C ⁡ ( x ) = | x | 2 C(x)=|x|^{2} are conserved, so that trajectories lie on the unit sphere in ℝ 3 \mathbb{R}^{3} . We set ( I 1 , I 2 , I 3 ) = ( 2 , 1 , 2 / 3 ) (I_{1},I_{2},I_{3})=(2,1,2/3) , and sample the initial conditions uniformly on the unit sphere. Since | x | |x| is conserved, the trajectories carry no information on the radial component of the velocity field, the Jacobian error ( 43 ) is computed in the tangent directions only, namely as the error between ∂ x f ⁡ ( x ) ​ P ​ ( x ) \partial_{x}f(x)P(x) and ∂ x f ^ ​ ( x ) ​ P ​ ( x ) \partial_{x}\hat{f}(x)P(x) , where P ⁡ ( x ) = Id − x ​ x ⊤ / | x | 2 P(x)=\operatorname{Id}-xx^{\top}/|x|^{2} is the orthogonal projector onto the tangent space of the sphere at x x . Stuart–Landau. The normal form of a supercritical Hopf bifurcation [ Perko, 2001 ] reads as x ˙ 1 = μ ​ x 1 − x 2 + ( a ​ x 1 − b ​ x 2 ) ​ r 2 , x ˙ 2 = x 1 + μ ​ x 2 + ( b ​ x 1 + a ​ x 2 ) ​ r 2 , r 2 = x 1 2 + x 2 2 , \dot{x}_{1}=\mu x_{1}-x_{2}+(ax_{1}-bx_{2})\,r^{2},\qquad\dot{x}_{2}=x_{1}+\mu x_{2}+(bx_{1}+ax_{2})\,r^{2},\qquad r^{2}=x_{1}^{2}+x_{2}^{2}, and, for μ > 0 \mu>0 and a < 0 a<0 , admits a stable limit cycle of radius − μ / a \sqrt{-\mu/a} . We set ( μ , a , b ) = ( 1 , − 1 , 1 ) (\mu,a,b)=(1,-1,1) , so that the limit cycle is the unit circle, and sample the initial conditions in polar coordinates, with radius r 0 ∼ 𝒰 ⁡ [ 0.2 , 2 ] r_{0}\sim\mathcal{U}[0.2,2] and angle θ 0 ∼ 𝒰 ⁡ [ 0 , 2 ​ π ] \theta_{0}\sim\mathcal{U}[0,2\pi] , so that trajectories approach the limit cycle from both inside and outside. Active Müller–Brown system. We consider an active particle of unit mass [ Romanczuk et al., 2012 ] , with position x ∈ ℝ 2 x\in\mathbb{R}^{2} , subject to the Müller–Brown potential [ Müller and Brown, 1979 ] and to Rayleigh–Helmholtz friction, namely x ¨ = − κ ∇ V ( x ) + ( α − β | x ˙ | 2 ) x ˙ , \ddot{x}=-\kappa\nabla V(x)+(\alpha-\beta|\dot{x}|^{2})\dot{x}, where V ⁡ ( x ) = ∑ i = 1 4 A i ​ exp ⁡ { a i ​ ( x 1 − x ¯ 1 , i ) 2 + b i ​ ( x 1 − x ¯ 1 , i ) ​ ( x 2 − x ¯ 2 , i ) + c i ​ ( x 2 − x ¯ 2 , i ) 2 } , V(x)=\sum_{i=1}^{4}A_{i}\exp\left\{a_{i}(x_{1}-\bar{x}_{1,i})^{2}+b_{i}(x_{1}-\bar{x}_{1,i})(x_{2}-\bar{x}_{2,i})+c_{i}(x_{2}-\bar{x}_{2,i})^{2}\right\}, with the standard coefficients of Müller and Brown [1979] . The friction term injects energy at low speed and dissipates it at high speed, and vanishes at the characteristic speed v 0 = α / β v_{0}=\sqrt{\alpha/\beta} . We set ( κ , α , β ) = ( 10 − 2 , 0.25 , 1 ) (\kappa,\alpha,\beta)=(10^{-2},0.25,1) , so that v 0 = 0.5 v_{0}=0.5 . With this choice, the system is characterized by self-sustained oscillations within each of the two deepest wells of the potential, whereas the intermediate minimum is only visited transiently. Initial positions are sampled uniformly in [ − 1.5 , 1.2 ] × [ − 0.2 , 2 ] [-1.5,1.2]\times[-0.2,2] , and retained only if V ⁡ ( q 0 ) < 0 V(q_{0})<0 , which keeps each trajectory within its initial well. Initial velocities have magnitude v 0 v_{0} and a uniformly sampled direction. The resulting velocity field spans several orders of magnitude over the domain. Lorenz system. The Lorenz system [ Lorenz, 1963 ] reads as x ˙ 1 = σ ⁡ ( x 2 − x 1 ) , x ˙ 2 = x 1 ​ ( ρ − x 3 ) − x 2 , x ˙ 3 = x 1 ​ x 2 − β ​ x 3 , \dot{x}_{1}=\sigma(x_{2}-x_{1}),\qquad\dot{x}_{2}=x_{1}(\rho-x_{3})-x_{2},\qquad\dot{x}_{3}=x_{1}x_{2}-\beta x_{3}, and is considered with the classical parameters ( σ , ρ , β ) = ( 10 , 28 , 8 / 3 ) (\sigma,\rho,\beta)=(10,28,8/3) , for which the dynamics is chaotic, with the nontrivial equilibria C ± = ( ± β ⁡ ( ρ − 1 ) , ± β ⁡ ( ρ − 1 ) , ρ − 1 ) C_{\pm}=(\pm\sqrt{\beta(\rho-1)},\pm\sqrt{\beta(\rho-1)},\rho-1) being unstable [ Sparrow, 1982 ] . Initial conditions are drawn from a localized distribution 𝒩 ⁡ ( x ¯ 0 , 10 − 4 ​ I 3 ) \mathcal{N}(\bar{x}_{0},10^{-4}I_{3}) , with x ¯ 0 = ( − 9 , − 3 , 34 ) \bar{x}_{0}=(-9,-3,34) , so that the separation induced by the chaotic nature of the system is the only driver of the exploration of the attractor. D.1.2 Partial differential equations The considered PDE test cases are posed on a one dimensional domain Ω = [ 0 , L Ω ) \Omega=[0,L_{\Omega}) , with interval length L Ω L_{\Omega} and periodic boundary conditions, and discretized with N h N_{h} equispaced grid points, so that the resulting discretized state is u h ∈ ℝ N h u_{h}\in\mathbb{R}^{N_{h}} . Time stepping is performed via Fourier pseudo-spectral, second-order exponential time-differencing Runge–Kutta schemes provided by Koehler et al. [2024] .
Initial conditions are random superpositions of k k sine waves, with independent coefficients. Trajectories were generated with a fine time discretization and then subsampled in time for training, while validation and test trajectories were kept at the generation step size. Training Validation Test Equation L Ω L_{\Omega} N h N_{h} N N Δ ​ t {\Delta t} T T N N Δ ​ t {\Delta t} T T N N Δ ​ t {\Delta t} T T Burgers 1 1024 100 10 − 2 10^{-2} 0.5 0.5 20 5 ⋅ 10 − 3 5\cdot 10^{-3} 0.5 0.5 50 10 − 3 10^{-3} 1 1 Fisher–KPP 1 1024 100 5 ⋅ 10 − 3 5\cdot 10^{-3} 0.5 0.5 20 5 ⋅ 10 − 4 5\cdot 10^{-4} 0.5 0.5 50 5 ⋅ 10 − 4 5\cdot 10^{-4} 1 1 Korteweg–de Vries 30 1024 100 2 ⋅ 10 − 2 2\cdot 10^{-2} 2 2 20 2 ⋅ 10 − 3 2\cdot 10^{-3} 2 2 50 10 − 3 10^{-3} 4 4 Kuramoto–Sivashinsky 64 256 100 1 1 200 200 – – – 50 10 − 1 10^{-1} 800 800 Table 2: PDE datasets. Domain length L Ω L_{\Omega} , number of grid points N h N_{h} , and number of trajectories N N , step size Δ ​ t {\Delta t} and time horizon T T of each split. The training step size results from subsampling trajectories generated with a finer step size, by a factor 2 2 for the Burgers equation and 10 10 for the other equations. We report the PDE benchmarks below, specifying for each equation the spatial domain, the parametric configuration, and the distribution of the initial conditions. Burgers. We consider the viscous Burgers equation, reading as ∂ t u + u ​ ∂ x u = ν ​ ∂ x 2 u , \partial_{t}u+u\partial_{x}u=\nu\partial_{x}^{2}u, with viscosity ν = 10 − 2 \nu=10^{-2} , L Ω = 1 L_{\Omega}=1 and k = 2 k=2 , for which the initial waves develop into viscous fronts that propagate and decay over time. Fisher–KPP. We consider the following reaction-diffusion equation ∂ t u = ν ​ ∂ x 2 u + r ​ u ​ ( 1 − u ) , \partial_{t}u=\nu\partial_{x}^{2}u+r\,u(1-u), characterized by a logistic growth term, combined with diffusion. We set the diffusivity ν = 10 − 2 \nu=10^{-2} and the reaction rate r = 1 r=1 , on a domain of length L Ω = 1 L_{\Omega}=1 . Initial conditions, with k = 5 k=5 , are clipped to the range [ 0 , 1 ] [0,1] . Korteweg–de Vries. We consider the hyperviscous Korteweg–de Vries (KdV) equation ∂ t u − 6 u ∂ x u + ∂ x 3 u = − ζ ∂ x 4 u , \partial_{t}u-6u\partial_{x}u+\partial_{x}^{3}u=-\zeta\partial_{x}^{4}u, with hyperviscosity coefficient ζ = 10 − 2 \zeta=10^{-2} , on a domain of length L Ω = 30 L_{\Omega}=30 . The initial waves, sampled with k = 5 k=5 , break into solitary waves propagating at speeds depending on their amplitude, while the hyperviscous term damps small scales. Kuramoto–Sivashinsky. We consider the Kuramoto–Sivashinsky equation in conservative form, reading as ∂ t u + u ​ ∂ x u + ∂ x 2 u + ∂ x 4 u = 0 , \partial_{t}u+u\,\partial_{x}u+\partial_{x}^{2}u+\partial_{x}^{4}u=0, on a domain of length L Ω = 64 L_{\Omega}=64 .
For this domain length, the anti-diffusive second-order term, which destabilizes large scales, the fourth-order term, which dissipates small scales, and the nonlinear term, which transfers energy between them, together result in spatiotemporal chaos. D.2 Architectures and normalization We detail the architectures used to parameterize the learned velocity field in the ODE and PDE settings, together with the adopted normalization strategy. ODEs. The learned velocity field is a fully connected network of width 256 256 for the active Müller–Brown system, and 64 64 for the remaining test cases. Three residual connections are employed, with each residual block being a gated linear unit, in which a GELU branch is modulated by a tanh \tanh gate. The output layer is initialized to zero, so that training starts from the trivial dynamics f ^ = 0 \hat{f}=0 . Since all systems are autonomous, the network takes the state as its only input. PDEs. The learned velocity field is a convolutional residual network acting on the discretized state, as in Stachenfeld et al. [2022] . In particular, a first convolutional layer lifts the state to 64 64 channels, which is then followed by a sequence of residual blocks with the same number of channels, and finally by a GELU activation and a convolution projecting back to a single channel. Each residual block is composed of two GELU activations and two convolutions, and, for the Burgers, Fisher–KPP and KdV equations, of a layer normalization over the channels, applied independently at each grid point before each convolution. We use four residual blocks for the Burgers, Fisher–KPP and KdV equations, and eight residual blocks for the Kuramoto–Sivashinsky equation. All convolutions have kernel size 5 5 and circular padding, consistently with the periodic boundary conditions. The last convolution of each residual block and the output convolution are initialized to zero. Asymmetric normalization. The input and the output of the learned velocity field are of different nature, namely states and velocities, and are therefore normalized separately, as also noted by McCabe et al. [2025] .
Denoting by ( μ x , σ x ) (\mu_{x},\sigma_{x}) the mean and standard deviation of the training states, and by ( μ v , σ v ) (\mu_{v},\sigma_{v}) those of the finite difference velocities ( x ⁡ ( t k + 1 ) − x ⁡ ( t k ) ) / Δ ​ t (x(t_{k+1})-x(t_{k}))/{\Delta t} computed on the training trajectories, the learned velocity field f ^ \hat{f} is parameterized as f ^ ​ ( x ) = σ v ⊙ f ~ ​ ( ( x − μ x ) ⊘ σ x ) + μ v , \hat{f}(x)=\sigma_{v}\odot\tilde{f}\big((x-\mu_{x})\oslash\sigma_{x}\big)+\mu_{v}, where f ~ \tilde{f} is one of the networks described above, and ⊙ \odot and ⊘ \oslash denote componentwise multiplication and division, respectively. For ODEs the statistics are computed componentwise, while for PDEs, they are computed over the whole discretized solution. D.3 Training and evaluation Training. All models were trained with AdamW [ Loshchilov and Hutter, 2019 ] , with weight decay 10 − 5 10^{-5} and, for a given system, the same number of iterations for all strategies. For the Euler, Stuart–Landau and Lorenz systems, a constant learning rate η = 10 − 4 \eta=10^{-4} , a batch size of 64 64 , and 2 ⋅ 10 4 2\cdot 10^{4} iterations were used. For the active Müller–Brown system, we used a batch size of 128 128 and a cosine schedule decaying the learning rate from 10 − 3 10^{-3} to 10 − 6 10^{-6} over 5 ⋅ 10 4 5\cdot 10^{4} iterations.
For the PDEs test cases, we used a batch size of 128 128 and a cosine learning rate schedule from 10 − 4 10^{-4} to 10 − 7 10^{-7} over 2 ⋅ 10 4 2\cdot 10^{4} iterations for the Burgers and Fisher–KPP equations, 4 ⋅ 10 4 4\cdot 10^{4} for the KdV equation, and 10 4 10^{4} for the Kuramoto–Sivashinsky equation. These were preceded by 10 3 10^{3} warm-up iterations, during which the learning rate increased linearly from 10 − 6 10^{-6} to 10 − 4 10^{-4} . Gradients were clipped to unit norm, except for the Kuramoto–Sivashinsky equation. Validation. Every 100 100 iterations, the validation trajectory error ( 41 ) was computed by simulating the learned dynamics with RK4, at the prescribed validation step size, over the full validation horizon. For the ODE benchmarks, the velocity and Jacobian errors ( 42 )–( 43 ) were also computed along the validation trajectories, solely to assess their decay during training and to check the implicit Jacobian matching property of VM, and were not used for model selection.
For each run, the checkpoint attaining the lowest validation trajectory error was retained for testing. For the chaotic systems, no validation was performed, and the last model checkpoint was retained. Testing. Test trajectory errors were computed at the step size of the test data, with the implicit midpoint for the Euler and Stuart–Landau systems, and RK4 for all others. For the continuous-time approximation study, the reference trajectories were obtained from an additional test set generated over a fine discretization, sharing the same time horizon of the training set (Table 1 ), and then by linearly interpolating onto the inference grid. D.4 Additional results Table 3 reports, for the Euler, Stuart–Landau, and active Müller–Brown systems, the validation and test values of the trajectory, velocity, and Jacobian errors ( 41 )–( 43 ), attained by teacher forcing, rollout training with fixed and scheduled shooting lengths L ∈ { 5 , 10 , 20 , 50 } L\in\{5,10,20,50\} , alongside VM. Table 4 reports the validation and test trajectory errors ( 41 ) for the Burgers, Fisher–KPP, and KdV equations, for the same training strategies. In both tables, the validation errors are evaluated over the training horizon T train T_{\mathrm{train}} , and the test errors over the extended horizon T test = 2 ​ T train T_{\mathrm{test}}=2T_{\mathrm{train}} , as reported in Tables 1 and 2 .
Finally, Figures 11 – 13 report, for each PDE, the reference solutions for four test initial conditions and the pointwise absolute errors of the solutions learned by VM, TF, and rollout training with L = 5 L=5 , both within and beyond the training horizon.
